# Defect register

**Append-only. Numbered without gaps. Nothing is ever deleted from this file.**

It exists because three human-reported defects were removed from `PROGRESS.md`
while it was being rewritten to close M4 — not archived, removed — on the day
the human was being asked to sign that milestone off. A milestone close is
exactly when a ledger is least able to afford losing its open items and most
likely to: the file is rewritten wholesale, and a bullet that disappears leaves
nothing behind.

A row cannot disappear the same way. `scripts/gates/docs-lint.sh` checks that
the ids run `D001`, `D002`, … with no holes and no duplicates, so a deleted row
is a gap the gate names, and an edited row is a diff a reviewer sees. That is the
whole mechanism: not a database, a shape a check can hold.

## What goes here

Defects **observed** — by a human using the engine, or by the agent finding one
in something that already shipped. Not a backlog, not a design list, not
milestone scope: those live in `docs/roadmap.md`. If nobody has seen it go
wrong, it is not a defect yet.

`PROGRESS.md` still carries whatever is being worked on right now, in prose,
with the reasoning. This file carries the fact that it exists.

## Format

One row per defect. `State` is one of:

- `open` — reproducible and unfixed.
- `fixed` — corrected, with the commit that did it.
- `not-a-defect` — investigated, and the behaviour is correct. Kept, because a
  defect closed silently gets re-reported.
- `scheduled` — real, understood, and deliberately owned by a later milestone.
- `quarantined` — a CHECK that flaked twice (MASTER_PROMPT.md §12). It still
  runs, still measures and still reports; it no longer gates. The row says what
  has to replace the instrument, because a quarantine with no successor named is
  a deletion somebody has not admitted to yet.

| Id | Reported | What | State | Where |

| D001 | human, 2026-08-20 | `BasePart.Transparency` changes nothing | fixed | `e86f9b0b` — the value could not reach the renderer at all; `DrawItem` had no field for it. Sorted blended pass, M4.5 |
| D002 | human, 2026-08-20 | The sun never moves and shadows never lengthen | fixed | `4e4e3fc2` — `Lighting` was created on first `GetService`, after the host cached its id; a boot service now |
| D003 | human, 2026-08-20 | The sun's shadow flickers | fixed | `27c47549` — the shadow texel grid slid with the camera; snapped to whole texels. The rotational half was named and deliberately left out of M4.5. **It is D050**, fixed 2026-08-22: the snap rounded `R(t) * centreWorld`, an absolute position in a light space that turns with the sun, so a moving light swept the lattice out from under every shadow in the picture. Rounding against LAST FRAME's box in this frame's light space is what fixed it, and the numbers say which: 201 then 1,312 pixels between consecutive frames before, which is a discrete jump, against 224 / 570 / 1,029 / 1,571 after, which is what sliding looks like. Recorded here because this row read as an open residual for four days after the thing it names was closed (S8.5) |
| D004 | human, 2026-08-20 | A crash while dragging `Size`/`CFrame` in the inspector, no log | fixed | **Not reproduced, and two halves ruled out.** The write path was driven through zero, negative, 1e30 and infinity for 32 frames with a render extraction each time and did not fault (`world_host_tests`); the window was minimized and restored 25 times over 900 windowed frames and the host exited 0. What remains is the ImGui half. The handler and log file that would have captured it exist as of `7cc1c77c` **Closed 2026-08-23 on the reporter's word**: the person who filed it says it no longer happens. Nothing here reproduced it and nothing here fixed it on purpose, so what closed it is almost certainly one of the changes that rebuilt this panel between M4.5 and E2 -- the inspector's write path went through a gesture, the selection became a set, and the row layout was rebuilt around one height. The row stays because the register is append-only and a deleted id is a hole the docs gate names; what it no longer is, is OPEN. **If it returns, it returns with evidence**: the crash handler is in place and the report should carry `luaug-crash-<pid>.dmp` and `luaug.log` from beside whatever was being run |

| D011 | agent, 2026-08-20 | Debug wire boxes and `DebugService` lines were drawn in world space through a camera-relative view-projection, displacing every one by the camera's distance from the origin | fixed | `e86f9b0b` — the buffer is rebased once, after extraction |
| D012 | agent, 2026-08-20 | `Model:PivotTo` was `PrimaryPart.CFrame = cf` under a new name: `PivotOffset` did not exist, so nothing could hinge | fixed | `721f6194` — `PVInstance` with a real pivot offset |
| D013 | agent, 2026-08-20 | `modelPivot` said "the centre of the extents box" and averaged part positions, which is a different point whenever parts differ in size | fixed | `721f6194` |

| D016 | agent, 2026-08-20 | `BindToClose` has no capped grace period: a close handler that yields is cut off at the next drain rather than waited for. `architecture.md` §app promises the cap | fixed | `c18f12f4` — the host keeps ticking while a handler is parked, up to a wall-clock cap, and warns when the cap runs out. Both halves tested: a handler that yields and finishes, and one that never does |
| D017 | agent, 2026-08-20 | The `DebugShell` has no memory-category table and no log/REPL pane, both named by `architecture.md` §app | fixed | **M6.** The memory table is §6's plan made real: every entry script gets a category from the 32..255 pool, assigned by CHUNK NAME so a hot reload reuses the row and the number stays comparable across one, and `lua_setmemcat` on the script's own thread is what attributes its allocations. The table shows only categories that hold something -- a table where seven rows are permanently zero is a table nobody reads.

The console pane is both halves. The log is a bounded ring the overlay fills from a sink that CHAINS to the console one, so the pane and the log file both get every line; a shell that ate the log would be the last place anybody looked for it. The REPL is `runSource` with a name and the reserved category 7 -- deliberately the same path into the VM an entry script takes, because a second path would be a second thing to keep honest. Four cases cover what is testable without a window: a chunk that runs, one that does not compile and leaves the VM alive, a per-script row with real bytes, and two scripts getting two categories with a reload reusing one |
| D018 | agent, 2026-08-20 | `luaug_net_tests` hung once on Windows, holding CTest until it was killed; a re-run passed | fixed | **Reproduced 2026-08-21 during M7 and root-caused rather than quarantined.** §12 would have allowed a quarantine on this second occurrence; the hung process answered the question before that was needed. It held exactly one socket -- `127.0.0.1:65533` in `LISTEN`, with nothing established -- and two threads, both waiting. `tests/loopback_server.h` called `::accept` with **no deadline**, and `~LoopbackServer` joined that thread BEFORE closing the listener. So the state a FAILING test leaves behind -- a client that never connected -- was being converted into a suite that hangs forever, which is why it presented as a flake: the underlying assertion failure was invisible. `Connection` has carried a ten-second read deadline since it was written, with a comment saying a suite that hangs tells you less than one that fails; the step BEFORE it had no such guard. **Fixed**: accept polls `select` with a fifty-millisecond tick against a ten-second deadline, and the destructor sets a stop flag before joining, so a server whose client never arrived reports "no client connected within ten seconds" instead of waiting for the CI runner to time out. Break-verified by a case that stands a server up and never connects: it reports the deadline rather than hanging, and with the deadline removed that case is the hang |
| D019 | CI, 2026-08-20 | `engine/app/src/engine.cpp` used `std::sort` with no `#include <algorithm>`: it compiled on Windows and in the Tier-2 container by reaching the header transitively, and failed on the CI runner's libstdc++ | fixed | `main` was red for one push. Found because the run the milestone push cancelled had already reported it. Neither local tier can see this class of defect — the transitive graph differs by libstdc++ version |
| D020 | CI, 2026-08-20 | `rokit install` resolves every pinned tool through api.github.com unauthenticated, which is 60 requests an hour shared across the runner's IP; it returned 403 and killed a job whose build and 25 tests had already passed | fixed | `rokit authenticate github --token` with the workflow's own `GITHUB_TOKEN`, before `install`. Subcommand read off the pinned 1.2.0 binary's `--help` rather than from documentation for another version |
| D021 | agent, 2026-08-20 | A property that refuses a value for being out of RANGE reports the key for its TYPE: `PhysicsService.FixedTimestep = 1/10` raises "it takes a number" about a number | fixed | M6 -- the IDL gains `Property.ErrorKey`, a per-property override for `errKeyOnInvalidSet`, and twenty ranged properties carry one. A setter still answers with a bool, so the key states the whole DOMAIN rather than the failure: "it takes a tick length from 1/240 to 1/30" is equally true of `"fast"`, which is why the override subsumes the type key rather than competing with it. Seven shared keys by shape, not one per property. Break-verified -- with the override ignored, five conformance cases and one seam case go red |
| D022 | agent, 2026-08-20 | A `Part` never reaches the solid renderer: only a `MeshPart` does, so every primitive in the world -- crate, ramp, floor, character -- draws as a debug wireframe box | fixed | **M6**, by human decision on 2026-08-20 — and this row said M7.5 until 2026-08-21, which is the correction. The roadmap and the M6 brief had carried it as M6 scope since `53e0af7c`, hours before this row was written, so the two sources disagreed: that is the state `Transparency` was in for a milestone, and it is worse than either answer. The reasoning for M7.5 was sound — it moves every render golden, and M7.5 owns how this engine renders — and it was outweighed by what deferring costs: `examples/04-obby` ships this milestone and M7's whole streamed world would be built and reviewed in wireframe. True since M2 and unchanged by M4, which shipped mesh rendering; found by looking at the M5 deliverable's first screenshot, where a whole physics playground is outlines. The fix is a built-in primitive mesh per `Enum.PartShape` scaled by `Size` **Closed in M6**, which is where the roadmap had scheduled it: every `Enum.PartShape` has a built-in primitive mesh scaled by `Size`, and `examples/04-obby` shipped on it. This row read `scheduled` until 2026-08-21 -- a ledger that says a defect is still coming after the milestone that closed it was signed off is worse than no ledger, and the ledger in `PROGRESS.md` had it right the whole time. |
| D023 | CI, 2026-08-20 | `scripts/gates/clang-format.sh` was committed without the execute bit, so the CI job that runs it directly failed with exit 126 while both local tiers were green | fixed | `4efa55a3` — `git update-index --chmod=+x`. `localgate.ps1` invokes every gate script as `bash <script>`, which does not care about the mode; `ci.yml` runs them the way a shell script is run. A `chmod +x` on a Windows checkout is not the same thing as a mode in the index, and the two look identical in `ls -l` |
| D024 | CI, 2026-08-20 | The determinism gate compared a PHYSICS scenario's committed trace against a build made by a different compiler, and went red on the CI Windows runner at tick 600 of 3,600 | fixed | ADR 0025's guarantee is same-BUILD, and every scenario before M5 was integer and tree state that happens to survive a compiler change. `scenario.json` gains `sameBuildOnly`: such a scenario carries no trace and is run three times, which is the roadmap's own wording for the M5 gate. The behaviour half is covered by seven tolerance-based assertions inside the scene. `churn` is anchored to keep its cross-build trace, since it was never a physics test |
| D025 | human, 2026-08-20 | A `CharacterBody` ignores `CollisionGroup`: a wall in a group its group is set never to collide with still stops it, and two characters in such a group still block each other | fixed | Reported as "two characters pass through each other", which did not reproduce — each character carries an inner rigid body and that is what the other one's sweep finds (Finding 15). The real defect was underneath: `CharacterVirtual::ExtendedUpdate` was handed a default-constructed `ObjectLayerFilter`, which accepts every layer, so the one thing a player controls was the one thing outside the collidability matrix. It now uses the world's own `GetDefaultLayerFilter`/`GetDefaultBroadPhaseLayerFilter` against the character's layer. Four seam cases and two conformance cases, verified by breaking both the filter and the inner body |
| D026 | agent, 2026-08-20 | The capture gate records an `upload`'s SIZE and not its contents, so every vertex a frame draws is invisible to the blocking render gate | fixed | Found while recording M6's UI goldens: the two resolutions differ in their scissors and viewports, and would have been byte-identical if only the quads had moved. **The same shape as M4.5's finding** -- that gate recorded the size of every uniform block and never its contents, and the fix reached `bindUniforms` and not `upload`, which was carrying debug-line geometry even then. A content digest is the obvious fix and is not free: raw float bytes can differ in the last bit between compilers, and a cross-BUILD comparison of them is exactly what D024 was. What holds the line meanwhile: `luaug_ui_tests` asserts exact rectangles, the two goldens catch anything that moves a scissor or a viewport, and the gate record carries a screenshot **Fixed 2026-08-21, and the row's own warning was right.** An `upload` and an `uploadTexture` now record what they carried. The obvious fix -- an FNV of the quantized words, the same digest `bindUniforms` has used since M4.5 -- was written first and the Tier-2 run refused it, on exactly the buffers whose contents are COMPUTED rather than read from a file: the primitive-shape meshes and the UI vertices. **A hash has no tolerance**, so a value one bit apart on either side of a quantization boundary hashes to two unrelated numbers, and over a few thousand floats something always lands on a boundary. Coarsening the grid moves the boundaries rather than removing them; it was tried and it made a third buffer fail. What ships is continuous instead: the MEAN of the float words and the mean step between consecutive ones, on the four-decimal grid the rest of the stream already uses, beside an exact hash of the words that were never floats. Means are what buy the margin -- the two compilers disagree by less than 1e-4 on a whole buffer, and moving one quad by four tenths of a pixel moves the mean by 1.4e-2, which is the break test. **And the bits cannot tell you which words are floats**: a `UiVertex` colour with alpha 127 reads as a perfectly ordinary normal float of 1.7e38, which overflowed the printed sum and made the two tiers disagree about what an out-of-range conversion produces. The split is by MAGNITUDE, and packed data is bit-identical on every compiler, so sending it down the exact path costs nothing. Four tests in `rhi_capture_tests.cpp` pin all of it. 
| D027 | human, 2026-08-21 | A character standing on a moving platform does not move with it: `examples/04-obby`'s waver slides out from under the player, who stays where they were | fixed | **Two halves, and the second is wider than the platform.** (1) `GetGroundVelocity` — Jolt's answer to "how fast is the body I am standing on moving" — appears **zero times** in this engine; `ExtendedUpdate` is handed only `mWalkStairsStepUp` and `mStickToFloorStepDown`, so a character never inherits its ground's motion. (2) And it would read zero if it were called: `jolt_physics.cpp:668` moves kinematic bodies with `SetPositionAndRotation`, which TELEPORTS. Jolt derives no velocity from a teleport; `MoveKinematic` is the call that takes a target and a delta and computes one. So **every script-moved kinematic body in the engine currently has zero velocity**: a closing door does not push, a piston does not launch, a conveyor does not carry. Scheduled M6, where it was found — the roadmap named the obby's moving platforms "a deliberate integration stressor" and this is the stress showing. The origin is M5's seam rather than M6's work | **Fixed 2026-08-21**, both halves. `setBodyTransform` records a kinematic body's target and `step` spends it through `MoveKinematic` against the tick the simulation is about to take -- the fixed timestep and never a wall clock (R10) -- so a moved kinematic body now HAS a velocity; and `moveCharacter` adds `GetGroundVelocity()` to the character's own, while grounded only, because carrying the last platform's velocity through a jump would launch it. Three seam cases: a kinematic body arrives with a velocity, a capsule rides a platform there and back without asking for a metre of it, and a capsule in mid-air inherits nothing. Break-verified -- with the inheritance removed the rider stays at zero while the platform reaches six
| D028 | agent, 2026-08-21 | `Touched` does not fire for a `CharacterBody`'s SIDE contacts: a character that walks into a wall, a flagpole or a trigger it does not stand on fires nothing | fixed | Found building `examples/04-obby`, whose finish was a pole the player pressed against and which therefore never fired. The ground half is fixed in the same commit -- `writeCharacters` diffs the surface under the character's feet against last tick's and fires `Touched`/`TouchEnded` on both parts, which covers checkpoint pads, hazards and pressure plates. The remainder needs the character's non-ground contacts, which are not on the `IPhysics3D` seam: `CharacterState` carries the ground body and nothing else, and `CharacterVirtual::GetActiveContacts` would have to reach it. That is a seam widening rather than a fix, so it is a row rather than a commit. What holds the line meanwhile: the obby's finish is a PAD you stand on, and that reads better than a pole anyway **Fixed 2026-08-21, and the row was wrong about what it would cost.** It said a seam widening; the seam did not move at all. `CharacterVirtual::GetActiveContacts` is read after the step, each contact turned into a pair keyed by our own handles, and the SAME diff `buildContactEvents` makes for rigid pairs decides Began and Ended. `ContactEvent` is unchanged, and so is the scene glue that consumes it -- `publishContacts` reads only the two user-data fields, which is exactly what a character has. The ground half MOVED here with it, and that is the point rather than a side effect: two mechanisms for one signal is how the two come to disagree, so `writeCharacters` no longer diffs the surface under the feet and keeps only `Landed`, which is about ground STATE. Two details that are not obvious: a predictive contact -- one the sweep found ahead of the character and never reached -- is filtered on `mHadCollision`, and the character diff makes NO sleep exception where the rigid one must, because a `CharacterVirtual` is never put to sleep by the solver and its inner body is created with `mAllowSleeping = false`. Three seam tests and one conformance case, each verified by breaking: with the collection removed, exactly those three go red. |
| D029 | human, 2026-08-21 | A `UIListLayout` with `HorizontalAlignment.Center` does not centre its column: every child in `examples/04-obby`'s menu sits 16 px left of centre | fixed | The cross-axis metric is the wrong one. `layout.cpp:275` computes `lineCross` as the WIDEST CHILD in the line, and `:294` then centres each child against that -- so a column whose children are all the same width gets `lineCross - cross == 0` and the whole column is placed at `content.min.x`, the left edge. The menu's children are `UDim2.new(1, -32, ...)`, 32 px narrower than the panel, which puts their centre exactly 16 px left of the panel's: the observed number is half the inset. The main axis is correct and uses `lineLimit`, the container's own extent (`:282`), which is what the cross axis should use when the layout does not wrap. **`Wraps = true` needs deciding rather than discovering**: whether wrapped lines centre within their own band or within the container is a real choice and this fix has to state which | **Fixed 2026-08-21**: the cross axis uses the container's own extent when the layout does not wrap, exactly as the main axis already used `lineLimit`. **`Wraps = true` is decided rather than discovered** -- a wrapped line centres within its own BAND, because wrapped lines stack along the cross axis and centring one against the whole container would put every line on top of every other. Four cases, including the wrap one and a horizontal row, whose cross axis is the other one and which a half-fix would have missed. Break-verified -- with `lineCross` back as the metric, three go red
| D030 | human, 2026-08-21 | `UICorner` does nothing: every frame and button draws square, and `CornerRadius` has no consumer anywhere | fixed | `components.h:452` declares `cornerRadius` and that declaration is its ONLY occurrence outside the generated accessors; neither `engine/ui` nor `shaders/src/ui2d.hlsl` mentions a radius, a corner or a rounding. The class doc promises the opposite -- "rounds its parent's corners; it changes the DRAWING and not the layout or the hit test". **And the marker built to make this state impossible was not applied**: the property is not `Inert`, so nothing in the inspector or the api-dump says it is stored-and-unread. That is the finding worth more than the fix -- `Inert` (M4.5) still depends on somebody remembering, which is the one thing it exists not to depend on. The drawing fix is a rounded-rectangle signed distance in `ui2d.hlsl`; the process fix is a check that a property with no reader is either consumed or marked | **Fixed 2026-08-21**, both halves. The drawing: `DrawQuad` carries a radius, `UICorner` is read where the background quad is built, and `ui2d.hlsl` measures a rounded-rectangle signed distance and antialiases it over one pixel of its own gradient. The geometry stays four vertices and a radius of zero costs one compare, so a rounded panel is the same draw as a square one -- and the hit test stays rectangular, which `UICorner`'s own doc promises. **The process half is `tools/repo/inertcheck.luau`**, in the Luau gate: every component field has a reader or its property is marked `Inert`. Run against the whole tree it found five more -- `CollisionFidelity` (which claimed a reader in prose that did not exist), `ScaleType`, `SliceCenter`, `ScrollBarThickness`, all now marked with M7 named -- and one field, `TextInputComponent::caret`, that named a feature nobody had written and is deleted
| D031 | human, 2026-08-21 | D027's fix is never reached: a tweened `Anchored` platform is a STATIC body, and `MoveKinematic` on a static body does nothing, so the character still does not ride it | fixed | Confirmed by playing a build newer than D027's fix. `physics_sync.cpp:122` reads `isDriven(id) ? Kinematic : anchored ? Static : Dynamic` — `Kinematic` is chosen only for a part an active `Weld` drives, so an `Anchored` part a TWEEN moves is classified `Static`. Jolt neither moves a static body nor derives velocity from one, so `MoveKinematic` receives a target and nothing happens. The example states the intent that the classification does not implement: "anchored parts whose `CFrame` a tween writes. That IS the kinematic case." **Fix, chosen by the human 2026-08-21 (option A of two):** an anchored part becomes `Kinematic` when its `CFrame` is written and returns to `Static` after N ticks without a write. Option B — `Anchored` meaning "kinematic always" — was rejected on cost: `jolt_physics.cpp:89` splits the broadphase into `NonMoving` and `Moving`, and the static layer exists precisely so it is never re-fitted; making every floor and wall kinematic would put a world of never-moving bodies into the layer that is updated each tick. Option A moves only what is written, which is the count that must be moving anyway. **Two things to get right rather than discover:** hysteresis, because a part written every other tick would otherwise oscillate between broadphase layers; and the transition cost, which this project measures rather than estimates — record it in `perf-baselines.md` with N platforms transitioning, beside the physics rows | **Fixed 2026-08-21** as the human chose: an anchored part is `Kinematic` while something is writing its `CFrame` and returns to `Static` twelve ticks after the last write. Twelve covers any driver writing at five hertz or faster and costs at most a fifth of a second of a stopped body in the moving layer; it is a TICK count, because a body's broadphase layer must not depend on how fast the machine is running (R10). **Two costs the measurement found and reasoning did not**, both fixed before the row was written: writeback went 0.03 -> 9.9 ms because Jolt reports every kinematic body as active and the mirror was copying six thousand solver transforms a tick back into components the script owns (a kinematic body is not written back now); and apply went 1.60 -> 6.96 ms because the pending-move list was deduplicated by scanning it, which is quadratic. `churn10k` settles at 7.32 ms against 4.96 before, and that increase is honest -- two thirds of its anchored parts are written every tick and are now simulated as the moving bodies they always were. `tests/bench/platforms200` and two rows in `perf-baselines.md` carry the numbers. Break-verified -- with the classification back to `Static` the conformance case reads zero
| D032 | agent, 2026-08-21 | The audio underrun counter counted the NORMAL case: a sixty-second soak on a real device reported 1,348 underruns and every one of them was the mixer working correctly | fixed | Found by running M6's own audio gate on a machine with speakers, which is the half CI cannot do. The counter incremented whenever a device callback arrived with no NEW voice frame published since the last one -- and that is most callbacks: the device asks for buffers at about 82 Hz and the simulation publishes at 60, so roughly a fifth of them legitimately mix the same voices again. **A gate that reports a catastrophe for the ordinary case is worse than no gate**, because the first thing anybody does with it is stop reading it.
| D033 | human, 2026-08-21 | `examples/05-streaming` loses frame rate steadily: 100 fps at the start, 35 fps after five minutes of flying | fixed | Reproduced before anything was touched, headless, counting instances rather than trusting the frame rate: `workspace` goes 2804 -> 4784 -> 8186 -> 13538 over 165 s of sim and never falls, across whole laps of a 60 s circuit. So it is not a hitch and not the renderer -- it is a world that only grows, and the smooth decay is the renderer iterating it. **`StreamingHost::setWorld` rebuilt the `StreamingGlue` EVERY FRAME.** The frame loop calls it at the safe point so a hot reload's new world is picked up; the glue is where the record of which instances belong to which resident chunk lives. So each frame threw that record away. The manager went on believing chunks were resident and called `evict(id)`; the newborn glue looked the id up, found nothing, and returned. Nothing was ever destroyed, and every re-entry into a chunk built a fresh folder of instances beside the last one. The fix is an early return when the world and root are unchanged -- and, on a real change, `StreamingManager::forgetResidency()`, because otherwise the first frame after a reload asks a new glue to evict chunks it never materialised: the same silent no-op, one frame wide | **Fixed 2026-08-21.** After: 1737 / 1528 / 1531 / 1685 instances, repeating exactly lap after lap -- the world became a function of the circuit position, which is what a streamed world is. Median frame 9.26 -> 2.31 ms, worst 36.08 -> 7.85 ms. **The gate that would have caught it now exists**: `ctest -R streaming_soak` flies the five-minute circuit and asserts a frame-time histogram, a declared memory ceiling, and -- the one the roadmap did not name -- that the INSTANCE COUNT FLATTENS. D033 broke neither of the first two: no frame passed 33 ms and no ceiling anyone would have set was crossed. Break-verified both ways: with the early return removed the gate is red, with it restored green
| D034 | agent, 2026-08-21 | With `--rhi=null` the streamed world never loads: 289 chunks become eleven instances, and the soak gate passes in 0.17 s | fixed | Found while wiring D033's gate, and it is the worse of the two even though nobody could see it. Content mounting, the pack mount and `streaming.load` all lived inside `ensureDebugPass`, which returns early when `device->caps().shaderFormat == Unknown`. Nothing in that block reads a shader, a format or a device: content addressing and streaming residency are decisions about files and distances. The consequence was not a broken picture but a VACUOUS PASS -- flat frame times over an empty world are exactly what a leak detector wants to see, so the gate reported a clean bill of health on a run that had done nothing | **Fixed 2026-08-21**: the block moved out of the shader gate into the boot path. The same coupling would have silently emptied the world on any machine whose shader load failed. `--soak-min-instances` landed in the same change, so the gate has to be TOLD what "the world loaded" means for a scene and cannot pass on an empty one again
| D035 | agent, 2026-08-21 | `TcpStream::connect` waits out its whole timeout on Windows when the connection is REFUSED: ten seconds to discover that nothing is listening on a loopback port that answered instantly | fixed | Found because `@std/net`'s first request took ten seconds to fail against a dead port, and the ten was suspicious -- it is the request timeout to the millisecond, which means nothing was ever detected and the deadline simply expired. `tcp.cpp:145`'s `waitFor` passes only `writefds` to `select`. That is correct on POSIX, where a resolved connect is writable whichever way it went. **Winsock reports a FAILED non-blocking connect in `exceptfds` and NEVER in `writefds`**, so the one case the code was written to detect -- ask `SO_ERROR` which way it went -- could not be reached at all. Present since M3 and invisible until now: the one caller was the dev-server connect (ADR 0035), where a slow failure looks like a server that has not started yet. The header even states the intent it was failing -- "the OS default for a refused-then-retried connect is tens of seconds of nothing" | **Fixed 2026-08-21**: `waitFor` passes an except set as well when waiting to write. Harmless on POSIX, where a descriptor turning up in both sets is still one `ready > 0` and `SO_ERROR` still says which. Break-verified against a LONG timeout and a loose bound -- twenty seconds against eight -- because how fast a refusal arrives is the OS's business (about two seconds on the machine this was found on, where the firewall drops the SYN rather than answering it) while the BUG makes the answer exactly the timeout on every machine
| D036 | agent, 2026-08-21 | A `@std/net` request that completed on the socket never came back to Luau: the worker filed nothing, forever | fixed | A fairness bug rather than a logic one, and only a frame loop could have produced it. `AsyncClient::take` is called once per frame for every outstanding ticket, and a headless run on the null backend has no frame pacing at all -- a hundred thousand lock/unlock pairs on the same mutex the worker needs for the single instant it takes to file a finished result. `std::mutex` is not fair: a thread reacquiring in a tight loop can hold it essentially continuously. The worker printed "done" and then never got back in; the run ended, `shutdown()` set the stop flag, and it returned having filed nothing. Worth noting what made it hard to see: every part in isolation was correct, and the trace showed the request completing | **Fixed 2026-08-21**: an atomic completion counter read WITHOUT the mutex, so a poll that finds nothing takes no lock at all and there is nothing left to starve. Release/acquire pairs the counter with the insert that precedes it. Break-verified by a test that is that caller -- a tight `take` loop around a real request -- which does not finish without the early out
| D037 | agent, 2026-08-21 | `luaug_jobs_tests` hangs intermittently: the whole local gate stops with the process alive, at zero CPU, and never finishes | fixed | Caught in the act rather than reasoned about. The stuck process had exactly TWO threads, both `Wait`, both at zero CPU -- and a jobs test with a four-worker pool should have five. Two means the main thread plus ONE worker, which points at `join` and not at the work. A minidump confirmed it before anything was touched. **`shutdown` stored `running = false` -- half of the worker's wait predicate -- without `p.mutex`, the very mutex its `condition_variable` waits on.** A worker that had evaluated the predicate (still running, nothing ready) and had not yet atomically released the lock and registered on the variable missed the `notify_all` entirely, slept through the shutdown, and left the main thread in `join` forever. One instruction wide, and it had been there since M7's Phase 0. **`enqueue` had the same bug and is fixed in the same commit**: `readyCount` is the predicate's other half and it too was an atomic incremented outside the graph lock. That one was found by inspection while chasing this, and honesty requires the distinction -- the shutdown race reproduces on demand and the enqueue race did not, in ten thousand attempts. It is the same defect by construction, and a condition variable whose predicate reads state changed outside its mutex is wrong whether or not a test has caught it yet | **Fixed 2026-08-21**: both stores move under `p.mutex`. The cost is one uncontended lock acquisition per enqueue and one per shutdown; the graph lock was already taken twice per job. Break-verified, and this is the strong kind: with the shutdown fix reverted the new regression case hangs FIVE runs out of five, and with it restored it passes in 0.29 s. **The comment that was there made this harder to find.** It argued that an increment before a notify meant a job "cannot be missed", which is true of the window it was thinking about -- between a failed steal and the wait -- and says nothing about the window between evaluating the predicate and registering on the variable. A confident comment about the wrong window reads as a settled question
| D038 | agent, 2026-08-21 | The M7 soak gate fails on the Tier-2 container: twenty frames spend more than 33 ms INSIDE STREAMING, the worst 130 ms | fixed | Found by the gate itself on its first honest run, and only because the hitch check had just been changed to measure time attributed to streaming rather than whole frames -- a whole-frame check on a shared runner would have been dismissed as noise. Measured rather than guessed, in four passes: the cost was not IO completion (0.02 ms), not eviction (0.00 ms), not the rebase, but the LOAD path; inside that, `beginRead`; inside that, two separate things. **The first is `SDL_LoadFileAsync`, which opens the file SYNCHRONOUSLY and only reads asynchronously.** On a local disk that open is microseconds; on the container's bind mount it measured 5 to 141 ms, on the frame thread, inside the streaming pump. An async IO service whose submit path can block for a tenth of a second is not one, and the machines where that bites -- a network share, a phone's storage, a cold cache -- are exactly the ones a streaming world exists for | **Fixed 2026-08-21**: admission moved to a dedicated submitter thread. `readFileAsync` queues and returns; the submitter picks the most urgent request, RELEASES the mutex, and does the blocking open; `pumpIo` only harvests. `platform::shutdown` now stops the service -- nothing did, and a joinable `std::thread` in a static is `std::terminate` at exit rather than a leak. Three async-IO tests had to say what they had been assuming: with admission on its own thread, "submitted" and "in flight" are two moments with a scheduler between them, so a case reasoning about QUEUE ORDER now waits for the first read to be admitted before queueing the rest. Worst streaming pump went 130 ms to 68 ms; D039 is the other half
| D039 | agent, 2026-08-21 | Every streamed chunk is read from disk TWICE, and the first read is synchronous on the frame thread | fixed | The remainder of D038's measurement, and the worse of the two. `ContentMounts::resolve` decided whether a loose file existed by calling `platform::readFile` -- **reading the entire file** and throwing the bytes away -- immediately before the caller started an asynchronous read of the same file. The comment above it was right about why it could not use `std::filesystem::exists` (inside an APK the content directory is zip entries and no path a C runtime can stat) and drew the wrong conclusion from it: existence has to mean "can it be OPENED", not "can it be read". Twice the IO, the first half of it synchronous, in the pump | **Fixed 2026-08-21**: `platform::fileExists` opens and closes through SDL, which keeps the APK case working at a fraction of the cost. And the pump does not resolve at all any more -- `StreamingHost` resolves every chunk in the index ONCE when the index is read, because whether a file exists was settled when the project was built and is not a question for a frame. A missing chunk is now reported at boot rather than three minutes into a fly-through. After both fixes the Tier-2 soak passes: no frame spends more than 33 ms inside streaming, p99 whole-frame 4.6 ms. **And the crash after the fix was M6 Finding 17 for the THIRD time**: `StreamingHost` gained members, some object file did not get rebuilt, and an access violation landed in code that had nothing wrong with it. `--clean-first` was the whole answer, again
| D040 | agent, 2026-08-21 | Incremental Windows builds silently reuse objects compiled against an OLD header: a header change rebuilds nothing at all | fixed | **This is what M6 Finding 17 always was.** Four times across two milestones an access violation landed in code that had nothing wrong with it, immediately after a struct gained members, and four times `--clean-first` was the answer -- recorded each time as a pattern to remember rather than a defect to fix. The fifth time, somebody touched `physics_sync.h` and ran the build: `ninja: no work to do`. Ninja learns which headers an object depends on by matching a PREFIX against `/showIncludes` output, and CMake writes that prefix into `rules.ninja`. On this machine it reads `msvc_deps_prefix = Observacao: incluindo arquivo:` -- a LOCALISED MSVC -- written as UTF-8 (`43 C3 A7`), while the compiler emits it in the console codepage. The two never match, so every `/showIncludes` line is treated as ordinary output and **not one header dependency is ever recorded**. Every incremental build on a localised toolchain has been unreliable for the whole project. `VSLANG=1033` does not help: forcing English needs the English language pack, and the installed one is Portuguese | **Fixed 2026-08-21**: the build runs under `chcp 65001`, which makes the compiler emit the same UTF-8 CMake wrote. Verified directly -- with the codepage set, recompiling and then touching `physics_sync.h` rebuilds `physics_sync.cpp.obj`; without it, `ninja: no work to do`. `scripts/localgate.ps1` sets it, `bootstrap.ps1` prints it beside the vcvars line, and CLAUDE.md carries it in the list of things that waste an hour. **The lesson is not about codepages.** A recurring symptom recorded four times as a pattern is a defect nobody has looked for, and "remember to use --clean-first" is what a workaround sounds like when it has been mistaken for an explanation
| D041 | agent, 2026-08-21 | A clustered light is clipped into hard horizontal bands: the pool of light on a floor stops at a straight line and resumes at the next one | fixed | Found by LOOKING at the frame, and it could not have been found any other way: the cluster assignment numbered clusters slice-major while the shader read the grid texture row-major, so every fragment looked up a cluster belonging to some other part of the screen. Both sides were internally consistent, so every unit test passed -- the ninth light was assigned, the runs were contiguous and ascending, the tile-Y flip was right. What was wrong was that two correct pieces disagreed about a layout neither of them named. `clusterIndexOf` is now that name, it is `constexpr`, and a test asserts it against the exact expression the shader computes. The general shape: **when two sides derive the same index independently, the index is the thing to write down, not the derivation.** |
| D042 | human, 2026-08-21 | The obby's animated `Waver` renders as a dark square from behind and a downward-pointing triangle from the front, instead of as a bending bar | fixed | **Every skinned mesh in the engine had every joint but index 0 reading past the end of its palette, since M6.** The skin stream stores joint indices as `VertexFormat::Float4`, because `rhi::VertexFormat` has no integer format and the enumeration is frozen (ADR 0037) -- and both skinned shaders declared their input `uint4`. That does not CONVERT the value, it reinterprets the bits: `1.0f` arrives as 1,065,353,216. Zero was the only index that survived, because zero has the same bits either way. Found by a human looking at the picture, then cornered by differentials rather than by reading: the palette was logged and proved to be a correct affine matrix; a step rig with no blended vertices collapsed exactly the half bound to joint 1; and forcing every vertex onto `JointMatrices[1]` in the shader produced a perfect rigid rotation, which put the fault in the INDEX and nowhere else. Fixed by declaring the input `float4` -- which is what the stream holds -- and converting explicitly at the call. **`capture_gate_skinned` could not have caught it**: it watches the joint palette, and the palette was right; what was wrong was the index a vertex used to read it. A gate that watches an input cannot see a defect in its consumer. The asset was under-specified too and is rebuilt by `examples/04-obby/tools/make_waver.py`: it was a zero-thickness plane, so back-face culling made it vanish from behind, and it declared no material, so it took glTF's own default of a perfectly rough metal. |
| D043 | human, 2026-08-21 | The instanced path draws nothing: `tests/perf/horde` renders 2,000 enemies as an empty field with only the player in it, at 22 draw calls and 3.7 ms | fixed | **The frame M7.5 measured its headline number on was drawing almost none of the scene.** `core::Mat4` stores `m[column][row]`, so the four per-instance attributes are the model matrix's four COLUMNS -- and HLSL's `float4x4(a, b, c, d)` constructor fills ROWS. Both instanced shaders handed it four columns and got the transpose, so `mul(M^T, p)` wrote a dot product of the translation into `w` and every instanced vertex left the frustum. Fixed by transposing, and by reading the cofactor basis straight off the attributes instead of out of a matrix whose indexing had just changed meaning. **Every gate the milestone had was satisfied**: draw calls fell from 15,250 to 22, which is exactly the gate the roadmap asked for; the capture goldens matched, because the command stream was correct; and the frame got eight times faster, because it was drawing nothing. Found by rendering a NEW scene of eight identical spheres and seeing an empty floor -- then confirmed on the horde, whose picture nobody had looked at. Proved by differential: with the fix, the instanced frame is BYTE-IDENTICAL to the same frame with instancing disabled; before it, 87% of pixels differed. `screenshot_gate_instanced` is now that check, standing. |
| D044 | human, 2026-08-21 | The sun's shadows break into hatched dashes as they stretch, and the pattern crawls frame to frame instead of sliding | fixed | Reported twice, with a screenshot, and reproduced exactly: `examples/02-meshes` at frame 2100, a sun 24 degrees up -- nowhere near the horizon. **It is the shadow term and nothing else**: forcing it to 1 removes the pattern completely. The flicker and the dashes are one defect, because the pattern crawls as the light moves. **What it is NOT, each ruled out by a build and a measurement**, against the shadow term isolated from the frame (0.548 as shipped): the constant depth bias at 0.3 m (no change) and at 5 m (0.410, and by then it is detaching every shadow in the scene); a slope- and texel-scaled bias, which is the correct FORM and is not the cause (0.399 at twelve texels, 0.306 at twenty-four -- where the shadow itself is gone); receiver plane depth bias, Isidoro 2006, the textbook answer (0.549, identical with and without); twice the shadow-map resolution (0.549); `kShadowDistance` halved (no change); the split lambda at 0.70 and 0.60 (0.543); ten times the caster margin (no change); the cascade blend band from 0.12 to 0.90 (0.546); a slope-adaptive filter radius (0.527, and it costs contrast); bypassing the `Gather` bilinear reconstruction (worse); switching the shadow pass off front-face culling (0.604, much worse -- that cull is earning its keep); and the content-fitted cascade committed alongside this row (0.549); and the texel-sized filter that followed it. **What the filter change DID fix is a second thing the same human reported** -- that the gradient between shadow and lit grows as the shadow stretches, and loses its resolution doing it. That one was real and is closed: the far cascade was filtering 0.8 texels, nine taps inside a single texel, so its gradient was a raw texel step 21 cm wide beside a 3.4 cm soft one in the near cascade. **The instrument that made all of that possible is worth more than the list**: binding the shadow atlas to the resolve pass and looking at it. That is what showed the cascade was spending 500 by 90 texels of a 1024 by 1024 tile on the scene -- a real defect, fixed -- and it is what proves the map itself is clean, so whatever produces the dashes happens at sampling time. What has not been tried: reading the stored depth and the receiver's depth for one dashed texel and printing the difference in metres. **The measurement has since been made and it reframes the row.** The probe reports, per pixel, how far the receiver sits behind what the shadow map stored for it, in metres. The answer is not small and it does not oscillate: the streaks are regions where the receiver is more than a METRE behind the stored depth, the lit floor beside them is more than a metre in front, and the boundary between the two is one pixel wide. **Acne oscillates around zero; this does not.** The streaks are real shadows with a real depth step, and what reads as broken is their EDGE -- a silhouette quantised onto the shadow map's texel grid, seen at a grazing angle, with no penumbra to hide it. That also explains why every bias failed: there was never a comparison to correct. **What is left is a penumbra wide enough to cover a texel step**, and the two commits either side of this row made that harder rather than easier -- the content-fitted cascade shrank the texel to about a millimetre and a half, so the texel-sized kernel that followed it spans a few millimetres of world. Widening it to four and eight texels was measured and does not close the gap; a 3x3 kernel cannot span the thirty-odd texels a five-centimetre penumbra now costs, which is the clamp M7.5 had for a reason. The next change is the KERNEL -- more taps, or a rotated pattern -- not another constant. **And the reason shader-side visualisation kept failing was not the automatic exposure**, which this row said twice and both times wrongly. Replacing the forward pass's colour outright leaves its uniforms and textures unused, dead-code elimination drops them, the reflection then reports different resource counts and the pipeline stops being creatable -- so the frame that came out flat was a frame with no pipeline, not a frame with a bad exposure. The M7.5 brief already records that exact mechanism from the per-feature sweep. Keeping the original expression live (`color = probe + color * 1e-8f`) and pointing the resolve at the HDR target rather than the tonemapped one gives a working probe, and that is how the measurement above was taken **Fixed 2026-08-22 by changing the KERNEL, which is what the probe pointed at.** Nine taps cannot draw a penumbra: spread a 3x3 far enough to cover a texel step and its own three samples show as bands, which is why the radius was clamped to three texels and why the edge stayed hard however the bias was set. Twenty-five taps cover the same reach with the spacing filled in, so the radius can go back to being a number of METRES -- clamped to [2, 4] texels, which is now a floor that guarantees the step is always covered rather than a ceiling that stopped it ever being. **It costs 0.54 ms at 1080p** and that is recorded in `perf-baselines.md` rather than buried: 1.51 to 2.01 on `examples/02-meshes`, a third of that frame, and the tap count is the dial if it is judged too much. **Three designs and each one failed for a reason worth keeping**: world-constant radius with a clamp the chain defeated at both ends; a texel-sized kernel, which fixed the far cascade and made the near one razor-sharp once the fitted cascade shrank its texel to a millimetre and a half; and this one, which works because the kernel finally spans what the radius asks for. The unit test was rewritten with each and says so. |

Redefined to what starvation actually is here: the simulation holding the voice list when the device needs it. The callback `try_lock`s and, if it loses, counts one and emits the silence the buffer was already cleared to -- which is what an underrun sounds like. **It also fixes a real hazard**: the callback used to take a blocking `lock_guard`, so a stall in the simulation would have stalled the audio device with it, and an audio callback must never wait on a game thread. The same soak now reports **0** in two of three sixty-second runs on a real device and 1 in the third, and that one is a single contention in five thousand callbacks -- inaudible, and structurally removable with a lock-free publish when M7 gives audio real buffers to stream |
| D045 | agent, 2026-08-22 | `luaug new` cannot find its own template: it fails inside `listDirectory` on a path with `../..` in the middle of it, and has done since the CLI's commands moved into `commands/` | fixed | Found by USING it -- M8 needed a scaffolded project to package, and the first `luaug new` of the milestone failed outright. `cliRoot` counted two directories up from its own source file, which was right while the command lived at `tools/cli/new.luau` and stopped being right the moment it moved into `tools/cli/commands/`: two levels up from there is `tools/`, so it looked for `tools/templates/starter`. Nothing caught it. The CLI's own suite covers `toml` and `version`; `luaug new` had no test at all, and the M3 gate that signed it off ran it by hand before the move. **A count is a fact about a directory layout that no check holds still.** The fix walks UP from the command's own source looking for `templates/starter`, which is self-correcting under any layout, and `tests/packaging` now scaffolds with the real `new.run` on every Windows gate run -- so the next move breaks a gate instead of a user's first command. |
| D046 | agent, 2026-08-22 | Two gates depend on a generated world that nothing generates: on a fresh clone `streaming_soak` and `asset_determinism` compile an empty content directory | fixed | Found while wiring M8's own soak gate: the flagship's world was deleted to prove the new gate would rebuild it, and the M7 gates beside it turned out never to have rebuilt anything. The chunk SOURCES are generated and gitignored on purpose -- 4.8 MB of JSON from a hundred committed lines -- and `run.bat` regenerates them for a HUMAN. Nothing regenerated them for a gate, so both tests passed here because a hand-run had left the world on disk. **Neither had ever run in CI to say so**: the last all-green run across all three tiers is at M5, before either gate existed, and Actions has executed zero steps since 2026-08-21. Fixed by `tests/support/ensure_generated_world.cmake`, included by both drivers: absent world, `lute <generator>` from the repository root, and a named failure when lute is not on PATH. Break-verified by deleting the world and running the gate, which rebuilt 289 chunks and passed. **The general shape is the one this project keeps meeting**: a gate whose input is produced by a step nobody automated is a gate that reports on whatever the last person left behind. |
| D047 | human, 2026-08-22 | `examples/10-open-world` shakes: with the character walking, the whole world vibrates | fixed | **The engine has no render interpolation, and the scheduler computes the factor for it that nothing reads.** Measured on the reference machine rather than guessed: a windowed run of the flagship reports about 100 render frames for every 60 simulation ticks (`PROBE render=608 sim=6.100`), so a camera advanced on `PreRender` moves on every frame while everything it looks at moves on two frames out of three. At a walking pace of 7 m/s that is up to 0.12 m of alternating lag between the camera and the world, which is what "it vibrates" looks like. `Frame::alpha` is computed by `frame_scheduler.cpp` and its own header says it is "passed to `render::extract` so a 144 Hz display shows smooth motion from a 60 Hz simulation" -- `architecture.md` §3 says the same -- and grepping the tree finds no consumer: every other `alpha` in `render` is transparency. So the promise is documented, the value exists, and the interpolation was never built. **The scene half is fixed**: the flagship advances its camera on `Heartbeat` now, with the world, which is what every earlier example already did and what removes the symptom. **And the engine half is fixed too.** `render::TransformHistory` captures every part's and camera's transform at the START of each tick, so once the tick has run it holds the state the frame interpolates FROM and the world holds the state it interpolates TO; `render::extract` evaluates the whole frame -- camera included -- at one fractional time. It lives in `render` rather than in `scene` deliberately: a previous transform is not world state, must not enter the world hash and must not travel in a replay, so `scene`'s components are untouched and every recorded determinism trace stays valid. Something that streamed in has no previous and is drawn where it is; a reused ECS slot is caught by the generation. **Headless passes alpha zero by construction** -- a headless run drives exactly one fixed step per frame, so its frames ARE the ticks -- which is what makes every golden in the repository still match byte for byte, and all 43 tests did. The remaining caveat is written down rather than left to be found: a game that drives its camera on `PreRender` now has that camera blended slightly towards the last tick, which is why every example drives it on `Heartbeat`. |
| D048 | human, 2026-08-22 | Looking down in `examples/10-open-world`, something dark on the ground keeps changing shape from second to second | fixed | **Reproduced exactly as reported**: ten screenshots a second apart with the camera pointing straight down, then differenced. With the character HELD STILL and the sun FROZEN, and the scene proved identical between the two frames -- 58 draws, 12 visible objects, 1,320 triangles in both -- 10,658 pixels of a 960x540 frame still differ, and the diff traces every tile-seam shadow and the outline of a boulder's shadow. So it is the shadow path, not the content and not the streaming. **Three contributors, each isolated by a flag or a build:** the caster-fitted cascade (M7.5) accounts for about 3,000 pixels -- disabling the fit and falling back to the sphere leaves 7,617; automatic exposure accounts for about 5,000 more -- `--no-auto-exposure` on top leaves 2,540; and the remaining 2,540 is a one-pixel dotted line along every shadow edge, which is the cascade stepping by ONE TEXEL as the camera crosses a half-texel boundary. That last one is inherent to a snapped cascade and is the technique working, not failing. **And the biggest contributor was not in the frame at all**: the demo ran a full day in four minutes, ninety times faster than the real sun, so every shadow visibly swings while a player stands still. That is the part a person notices, and the day is ten minutes now. **Fixed in three places, and the third one is the root.** (1) The day, above. (2) **The cascade fit remembers its box**: `ShadowCascades` carries the box it chose, in WORLD space, and a cascade keeps that exact centre and extent -- and therefore that exact texel lattice -- for as long as it still covers what casts into the slice. A fresh fit is grown 25% past its contents so there is something to keep. It refits when the content leaves the box or has shrunk below half of it, and a settings change or a resize drops the memory because a new atlas is a new texel size. (3) **The character was standing on a four-tile corner and sliding off it forever**: the flagship spawned at the world origin, where four 32 m ground tiles of slightly different heights meet, so it crept about three centimetres a second -- which moved the CAMERA, which moved the whole shadow lattice, which dithered every shadow edge in the frame. Spawned on a tile centre it is still to nine decimal places, and the `CharacterBody` itself is not at fault: the same probe drifts at a corner and does not on a flat tile. **The chain, measured end to end on a scene proved identical between two frames: 10,658 differing pixels of 518,400 became 43 -- and 3 with automatic exposure frozen, which is floating-point noise.** The forty that remain are the exposure still converging by a hair, which is the stabiliser doing its job. Three goldens moved and were re-recorded: the change is a few texels of shadow edge on the two most distant spheres, which is what the diff shows and what a kept box should cost. |
| D049 | human, 2026-08-22 | `InputService.PointerLocked` does nothing: the cursor is never locked, never centred and never hidden, so a mouse look control stops at the edge of the screen | fixed | Found by asking for mouse look in the flagship and discovering there was nothing to switch on. **The property was stored and read by nothing** -- `setInputServicePointerLocked` wrote `engineState().pointerLocked` and no platform code ever consumed it; `PointerVisible` was the same. It is the shape `Inert` exists to make visible (D030), and `inertcheck` could not see it: that check sweeps COMPONENT fields, and these two live in `EngineState`, which is the world's own struct rather than a component pool. Fixed with `platform::setPointerLocked` over `SDL_SetWindowRelativeMouseMode` -- which centres and hides the cursor and reports motion instead of position, the only arrangement that lets a look control keep turning past the edge of the screen -- applied by the frame loop on CHANGE, windowed only. **And the camera turned the wrong way**, reported in the same breath: the rig's forward is `(-sin y, 0, -cos y)`, so increasing the yaw rotates towards -X, which is left. A positive input has to subtract, and both `Turn` and `Look` now do. |
| D050 | human, 2026-08-22 | Shadows flicker continuously as the sun moves: the edges of the tree's and the character's shadows jump a few pixels between frames instead of sliding | fixed | **A different defect from D048 and with a different cause**, which is why it survived it: D048 was about a STILL sun, and this is about a moving one. Isolated by holding the character still, letting only the sun turn, and freezing the HUD -- the last of which mattered more than it should have, because the clock digit ticking over was two thirds of what the first measurement counted. **The shape of the numbers is the diagnosis**, not their size. With the old snap, the difference between frames 600 and 601 is 201 pixels and between 600 and 602 it is 1,312 -- a jump of six and a half times for one extra frame, then flat at 1,553 and 1,809. That is a discrete jump, not motion. With the fix: 224, 570, 1,029, 1,571 -- near-linear, which is what sliding looks like. **The cause**: the texel snap rounded `R(t) * centreWorld`, an ABSOLUTE position in a light space that turns with the sun. With the box half a kilometre from the world origin and a ten-minute day, that coordinate sweeps several texels per frame on its own, so the rounding flipped constantly and the whole shadow map jumped a texel at a time in a direction nothing in the scene explained. **The fix is to round the box's MOVEMENT rather than its position**: the delta from where the box was last frame is quantised, so the lattice is carried forward frame to frame instead of re-derived from an absolute that is sliding underneath it. That is also the stronger statement -- temporal stability asks that this frame's grid line up with the last frame's, and a grid fixed in the world stops being fixed the moment the light turns. **And the fit's memory (D048) had never engaged in this scene**: the ground fills the slice, so the content's light-space bounds ARE the slice's own, and the coverage test failed by one hundredth of a metre -- the snap's own displacement -- every single frame. One texel of slack fixes that, which is invisible: it is one texel at the very edge of a cascade, inside the blend band to the next. Two goldens re-recorded; the diff is a scatter of dots on shadow edges. |
| D051 | human, 2026-08-22 | Objects resting on the ground have a band of lit floor between their base and the start of their shadow, so a capsule and a boulder both look like they are hovering | fixed | Reported with a close-up, which is what made it findable: **the same objects at `tests/screenshots/specular`'s framing show that gap as about one pixel**, so a milestone whose shadow gate looked at that scene could not have seen it. `tests/screenshots/contact` exists now and is a standing golden -- three metres from a capsule, a ball and a box, with the frame number sweeping the hour so both ends of the range are one flag away. **The first hypothesis was wrong and a picture said so.** The shadow pass culls FRONT faces, which stores the far side of a solid object and detaches the contact by its thickness, so the obvious fix is to cull back faces instead. That was built and measured: the contact attaches, and a regular hatched acne appears across every lit floor in `examples/02-meshes` -- D044's own signature, and the confirmation of its note that the cull "is earning its keep". **What was actually wrong is that the two biases were paying for that acne a second time.** With front faces culled there is very little for a normal offset or a depth bias to correct, and what they do instead is push every shadow away from the thing casting it. `kShadowNormalOffsetFilters` was `sqrt(2)` -- a square kernel's diagonal, which is the right scale for a bias that has work to do -- and `kShadowDepthBiasMetres` was two centimetres, which under a character IS a visible band of bright floor. **Measured at three sun elevations**: at a quarter of a filter the contact is perfect and the capsule's own base begins to speckle; at half it is attached at every hour with no speckle anywhere, and the acne frame is clean. Shipped at half and eight millimetres. Three goldens moved. |
| D052 | human, 2026-08-22 | Shadows in `examples/10-open-world` lose resolution at a moderate distance: a tree's shadow stretching towards the camera is drawn in visible blocks, and the tile seams across the ground break into dashes | fixed | Asked as a question -- "is this normal?" -- and the honest half of the answer is yes: a cascaded shadow map picks its cascade from where the shadow LANDS, not from where its caster stands, so everything past the third split is drawn at that cascade's texel size and nothing about a distant caster makes its shadow coarse on nearby ground. The other half is that ours was much coarser than it needed to be, and the flagship's own project file is why. **Measured** by dumping each cascade's fitted box: at the file's `shadow_distance = 180` on the preset's 1024 tile, the far cascade covers everything from 44 m out with a half-extent of 266 m -- **0.52 m per texel**, which is a half-metre grid for a tree's shadow at fifty metres and is exactly what the report described. A caster fit cannot help there: in an open field the ground casts everywhere, so the box is the slice's own sphere to the centimetre. The file now asks for `shadow_resolution = 2048` and `shadow_distance = 140`, which measures **0.20 m per texel** in the same cascade, and the cost is nothing -- 2.81 ms median against 2.89, over 2,400 frames at 1080p, run twice each in alternating order because a sequence of GPU runs is not a sequence of measurements (M8 Finding 5). **Two things found while measuring it.** The Ultra preset spent its four-times atlas on range rather than density: 220 m gave 0.32 m per texel against High's 0.35, so a preset called Ultra was as blocky as the one below it for anything past thirty metres. It is 160 m now, which measures 0.23. And `--quality=low` did not fully win over a project file: the preset was replaced but the file's per-key entries were then applied on top, so a machine that asked for less would have been handed this file's 4096-pixel atlas. A file's per-key graphics entries refine the level it names and are dropped with it; `engine/app/tests/project_config_tests.cpp` is new and says so, the layering having had no test of its own before. |
| D053 | human, 2026-08-22 | The world is temporally unstable from about eleven in the morning of the in-game clock: with the camera and every object still, the ground pulses and shadow edges tremble and jump between frames, where earlier in the cycle the same shadows slide smoothly | fixed | **Two defects under one symptom, and the report's own detail -- that it starts at an HOUR rather than after a duration -- is what separated them.** Reproduced on a still probe scene under the flagship's clock rate, one screenshot per frame, subtracting consecutive frames. **The first and larger one is not a shadow at all: it is the diffuse ambient, and it was arriving in steps.** The irradiance SH rode on the specular chain's level zero, which is rebaked only when the sky drifts past `kEnvironmentRebuildCosine` -- about one frame in a hundred under a ten-minute day -- so the light every matte surface in the world receives held perfectly still for a hundred frames and then jumped by about a per cent. Measured: on the bake frame **68% of the ground changed** while the sky beside it did not, at an energy of 297,810 against 17,000 for its neighbours. The specular threshold is right for what it guards (a degree of staleness in a mirror is a step nothing rougher than a mirror can show) and wrong for the diffuse half, which costs one projection onto nine numbers rather than six texture bakes. The diffuse half now has its own threshold four times tighter, and what the shader reads walks towards it a twelfth per frame -- **peak-to-mean 6.99 became 1.27**, at +0.2 ms. A sun that jumps further in one frame than the specular threshold allows is treated as a CUT and taken whole, which is what keeps a clock-scrubbing fixture (and `tests/screenshots/contact`, which steps a quarter of an hour per frame) from fading into every shot. **The second is the shadow edge, and it is inherent rather than broken**: a cascade's lattice is defined in the light's own rotating frame, so a fixed world point drifts across it at a rate set by the light's angular speed -- measured at 0.02 texels per frame at dawn against 0.50 near noon, which is why the report names an hour. What that drift produces is a shadow edge that steps one texel at a time, and what hides it is a penumbra wider than the step: `kShadowFilterMinTexels` is 3 rather than 2, which halved the amplitude of the worst step (channel delta 20 to 9) and leaves the near cascade -- where D051's contact lives -- untouched, because its filter was already above three texels. The rest is texel size, which is D052. |
| D054 | human, 2026-08-22 | The shadow flicker under a moving sun is reduced but not gone, and it scales with CAMERA DISTANCE: close to a caster the shadow slides smoothly, far from it the edges go back to jumping between frames | fixed | The third report about this edge, and the distance relation in it is the diagnosis. A cascade's lattice lives in the LIGHT's rotating frame, so a shadow edge cannot slide continuously -- it advances one texel at a time. What changes with distance is the frequency, not the size: measured on a still probe, the near cascade drifts about half a texel per frame near noon and steps every other frame, while the far cascade drifts a twentieth and holds still for twenty-three frames before moving. **Frequent small steps read as motion; one step every four hundred milliseconds reads as a jump**, which is exactly what the report describes and why the near field looked fine. The step cannot be removed without a temporal filter this renderer does not have, so it is hidden instead: the penumbra has to be several texels wide, and in the far cascades the authored 5 cm penumbra is a fraction of one, so the texel-band FLOOR is the only thing speaking out there. It was two texels, then three; it is six now. What made six affordable is the second half: **the 5x5 kernel is rotated per pixel** by Jimenez's interleaved gradient noise, which is a function of the pixel's coordinates alone and therefore perfectly stable in time. A fixed grid spread that wide puts its twenty-five taps a texel and a half apart and every pixel in a region samples the same five rings, which is the banding the old ceiling of four existed to avoid; rotating each pixel's grid spends the same taps on different rings and the banding becomes fine noise. **Measured on a still scene with the casters at forty-five metres, consecutive frames subtracted**: pixels changing by more than four levels fell from 62 per frame to 14, those changing by more than two from 227 to 80, and the worst single change from 37 levels to 14. The near cascade is untouched -- its texel is millimetres, so the world radius lands inside the band on its own -- and contact was re-measured at three sun elevations on `tests/screenshots/contact` because a wider filter scales the normal offset with it, which is what D051 was about. Seven goldens moved: five capture streams, whose diff is one line each recording that the PBR fragment shader grew by 2,520 bytes, and two screenshots. **The instrument for the next one is `tests/screenshots/contact`'s sibling trick, and it came from the report too**: a camera looking straight down at the ground turns a shadow edge into a shape whose motion is unmistakable. **Second round, from the same reporter**: the worst hour moved from noon to nine in the morning, which is a low sun and therefore long shadows over the flagship's terraced ground. Measured properly this time -- a burst of 120 consecutive frames from ONE run, and the shadow edge's sub-pixel position tracked along a scanline by where the profile crosses the midpoint between its lit and dark ends, which is invariant to the ambient drifting: **the edge held perfectly still for five to twenty-five frames and then jumped 3.6 pixels, twice reversing direction.** The reversal is the tell: the sun turns one way, so a backwards jump is the rasterisation flip-flopping rather than tracking. The cause is the filter's own arithmetic -- it returns a COUNT of taps, so the smallest change it can express is one tap, and with twenty-five of them one texel of the map flipping moves the edge by a twenty-fifth of a six-texel penumbra. **Seven by seven rather than five by five over the same radius quantises four times as finely: the worst single step fell from 3.58 pixels to 0.75**, and the flagship's median frame did not move (2.98 ms against 3.09), because at 1080p this scene is not bound by shadow taps. Seven goldens moved again, and the diff is again one shader-size line each plus a hair of edge. **One thing ruled out while looking**: the dark dashed lines along the flagship's tile seams are not a shadow artifact at all -- with `--shadow-cascades=0` they are still there, because they are the vertical faces of the steps between tiles seen nearly edge-on. |
| D055 | agent, 2026-08-22 | `inertcheck` sweeps component pools only, so every property belonging to a service with one instance per world -- half the API surface -- was never checked for being stored and unread | fixed | Named in M8's own gate record as the thing the milestone measured and did not fix, then asked for. The blind spot was the size of a service: a knob like `FixedTimestep` or `PointerLocked` lives in `scene::EngineState` rather than in a component pool, because a service with one instance and no hierarchy has nothing a component would buy. That is how D049 survived from M6 to M8. The lint now runs the same sweep twice -- once over `components.h` for `*Component` structs, once over `world.h` for `EngineState` -- with each sweep excluding its own declaring header, and an explicit table maps the fields whose property name is not just their name in PascalCase (`streamingLoadRadius` is `StreamingService.LoadRadius`). **The first run found three, and none of them was marked Inert.** `DebugService.OverlayVisible` was stored and nothing applied it: the overlay was toggled by F3 alone, so the property was a lie in both directions -- it is synced both ways now, because a game that draws its own hint from it would otherwise go stale the moment somebody pressed the key. `UIService.DisplayScale` says in its own comment that the host writes it every frame and the host never did, so it was 1 on a doubled display and `SafeAreaInsets` was zero on a device with a notch -- both are written from the window now, which is what `platform::windowDisplayScale` and `windowSafeAreaInsets` are for. And `StreamingService.PauseOutsideLoadedArea` had a reader **waiting** for it: `StreamingManager::minimumRingResident()` exists, its comment says it "is what `StreamingService.PauseOutsideLoadedArea` reads", and nothing had ever called it -- so a game that turned the property on got no pause and a character walking into unloaded ground fell through it. **Break-verified**: with a minimum ring nothing can satisfy, 240 frames produce zero ticks with the property on and ticks with it off. It does not weaken R10 -- what it changes is WHEN a tick runs, never what a tick computes. |
| D056 | agent, 2026-08-22 | The `shipping` profile has not compiled for an unknown length of time, and nothing anywhere builds it | fixed | Found by acting on a reconnaissance claim rather than by a gate, which is the point: `cmake --build --preset win-msvc-shipping` fails with two `fatal error C1083: cannot open include file 'luacode.h'`, at `engine/script/src/modules.cpp:9` and `engine/script/src/runtime.cpp:18`. The cause is not a missing file -- it is that `shipping` is the one profile that sets `LUAUG_LUAU_COMPILER=OFF` (`cmake/luaug_options.cmake:16-18`, "loads precompiled bytecode only", ADR 0002) and no `#if` was ever written around the two sites that need the compiler's header. **Nothing exercises it**: `win-msvc-shipping` is in `configurePresets` and `buildPresets` and NOT in `testPresets` (`CMakePresets.json:57-62`), the string does not appear in any workflow under `.github/`, and `scripts/localgate.ps1` builds `win-msvc-dev` only. So the profile that the option file describes as the one that "cannot accidentally carry" the compiler cannot accidentally be built either. **A second failure is stacked behind this one and is still untested**: `engine/app/src/debug_overlay.cpp` closes its `#if LUAUG_DEBUG_UI` at line 784 and then defines `DebugOverlay::captureLog()` at 786 using `ConsoleLog`, `console()`, `core::setLogSink` and `std::lock_guard`, all of which exist only inside the block -- so that translation unit should fail too, and the build never reached it. **This is what D057 is made of**, and it is why a broken profile nobody builds is not a harmless one. **Fixed 2026-08-22, and it took three fixes rather than the two that were visible.** The `luacode.h` includes are guarded, and the absence is made HONEST at runtime rather than silent: `loadChunk` is the single point where source would have become bytecode, so a shipping host boots, mounts the scripts and reports `script.err.no_compiler` per file with a non-zero failure count -- which means `luaug test` on such a build fails loudly instead of passing over nothing. `captureLog` was real and failed exactly as predicted once the first errors cleared. The third was new: `LUAUG_BUILD_TESTS` defaulted ON in `shipping` and `sandbox_tests.cpp` calls `luau_compile` directly, so the option is dependent now -- a suite that drives the engine through Luau SOURCE cannot run in a profile with no compiler, and `CMakePresets.json` already agreed by leaving `win-msvc-shipping` out of `testPresets`. **The gate builds and LINKS the profile every run** (`scripts/gates/shipping-build.sh`, Tier-2, ~30 s warm), linking rather than compiling because half of ADR 0011's claim is a link-time one: a call site that survives an `#ifdef` sweep is an unresolved symbol, not a compile error. It caught its first defect on its first real use, in the editor work landing beside it -- three private fields nothing reads when ImGui is compiled out, one of them dead. **What it does not fix is D057**, and the two are cleanly separable now: the profile compiles and still cannot run a packaged game, because ADR 0002 gives it no compiler and ADR 0045 ships source |
| D057 | agent, 2026-08-22 | `luaug build` packages a `dev`-profile host, so the released v1.0.0 game binary carries the ImGui debug overlay, the inspector and a Luau REPL against its own VM | fixed | Found while investigating D056, an hour after the v1.0.0 release was published with this binary attached. `projectlib.findEngine` searches `LUAUG_BUILD_ROOT` for `win-msvc-dev`, `linux-clang-dev`, `macos-clang-dev`, `win-msvc-debug` (`tools/cli/project.luau:126-143`) -- **`shipping` is not in the list**, so `luaug build` cannot select it even when it builds. Verified against the shipped artifact rather than inferred: reading `examples/10-open-world/dist/win64/LuauG-Open-World.exe` as bytes finds the literals `debug-overlay`, `ImGui` and `stored and read back faithfully` -- the last of those being the inspector's own inert-property tooltip from `engine/app/src/debug_overlay.cpp:436-445`. So F3 in the released game opens the engine's debug shell, and its console evaluates arbitrary Luau in the game's VM through `ScriptRuntime::evaluate`. **Impact is a promise broken rather than a machine harmed**: this is the player's own copy of a single-player game, so the REPL is a cheat console and not an escalation -- the sandbox (R4) is intact and `luaL_sandbox` is on in every profile.
