What it contradicts is ADR 0011:14, "dev-facing only, compiled out of shipping builds", and the release notes, which describe a player and do not mention a debug shell. **The two candidate fixes are not equal and the choice is a human's.** Making `shipping` build and packaging it would ship a profile that has never run a test in this repository's history (D056), which is worse than shipping the profile with 34 passing ctest targets behind it. Making the promise true instead -- a packaged game built from a profile that compiles the overlay out -- needs the bytecode packaging ADR 0045 declined, because `shipping` has no compiler and a packaged game ships source **Fixed 2026-08-23 by the third exit this row named.** `LUAUG_DEBUG_UI` and `LUAUG_LUAU_COMPILER` were independent options that only ever shared a profile STRING, so a profile that takes one and not the other costs a condition. `player` is Release, links the Luau compiler -- which a packaged game needs, because `luaug build` ships its Luau as SOURCE (ADR 0045) -- and declares no ImGui target at all, which is what makes "this artifact contains no overlay" a link-time fact rather than a promise (ADR 0011's own argument, applied to the profile that actually ships). **`luaug build` now REFUSES a host that is not one**, rather than warning: a warning in a build log is a warning somebody scrolls past, and what it would be describing is an artifact already written to disk and about to be sent to people. The message names the two commands that fix it. `--dev-host` is the door, because handing a tester a package they can open the inspector in is a real thing to want, and a rule with no door gets worked around by setting an environment variable instead -- which says nothing. So `LUAUG_HOST` is deliberately NOT consulted when choosing what to package (it is what `luaug dev` uses, and "I pointed the dev server somewhere" must not decide what a release contains); `LUAUG_PLAYER_HOST` is its own name for its own job. **And the gate builds and links it**, beside `shipping`, in the stage that exists because a profile nothing builds is a profile nobody knows is broken (D056) -- which was this row's own objection to simply shipping the `shipping` profile, and would have been a poor thing to reintroduce. The packaging suite asserts the refusal where no player host exists and says so out loud where one does |
| D058 | human, 2026-08-22 | The editor opens with the game already running and has no play, pause or stop, so there is nothing to edit against -- whatever you change is overwritten by the next tick | fixed | Reported at E1's review, in the sentence that decides whether the milestone is an editor: "só não gostei que o jogo já começa em execução e não tem onde dar pause play stop". It is right and it is not a missing feature so much as a wrong default -- a world that is simulating is a world whose properties are being written by scripts every sixtieth of a second, so a person typing into the properties grid is arguing with the game and losing. **The fix is cheap and the precedent is already in the loop**: `waitingForGround` (D055) gates `frame.simTicks` to zero for streaming, and its comment already makes the R10 argument this needs -- what a gate changes is WHEN a tick runs, never what a tick computes, so the same ticks happen in the same order with the same inputs and the hash after n ticks is the hash after n ticks. **And pausing at tick zero shows a built world rather than an empty one**, because `WorldHost::boot`'s drain runs every entry script before the first frame and advances no clock, which `world_host.cpp:318-327` says in its own words. **What cannot honestly ship with it is STOP.** In an editor, stop returns the world to what it was before play -- to the EDITED state, not the scripted one -- and this engine cannot remember an edited state yet, because nothing can serialize a world (E3). A button labelled Stop that silently discarded somebody's edits would be worse than no button **Closed 2026-08-23**, and by E1 rather than by anything here: the editor opens in EDIT mode, the toolbar has play, pause, resume, stop and step, and stop is honest because `World::snapshot` and `World::restore` arrived with it -- what it puts back is the world as it was before play, which is exactly what this row said could not ship yet. Left open in the register while it was being read as a scope note rather than as a defect; the state is what the register is FOR |
| D059 | human, 2026-08-22 | The mouse cursor disappears while the editor is open, so there is nothing to click the panels with | fixed | The same wrong-owner mistake D058 was, one device over: `examples/10-open-world` sets `InputService.PointerLocked = true` and `PointerVisible = false` at file scope for its mouse look (`init.luau:386-387`), and `WorldHost::boot`'s drain runs file scope before the first frame -- so an editor opened on that project had no cursor from the very first frame it drew, and every panel in it was unclickable. **The rule is the one Unity and Unreal use and the one the transport already applies to time**: while the world is paused the editor owns the device; pressing play hands it back. **Fixed 2026-08-22** by overriding rather than overwriting -- `engineState.pointerLocked` still holds what the script wrote, so a script reading the property back sees its own value and a replay of a game that locks its pointer stays legal; only what reaches the WINDOW is the editor's while paused. Reported by a person opening it, which is the third defect in this milestone found that way and the third that no test in this repository could have found |
| D060 | human, 2026-08-22 | A sound plays while the editor is open and the world is not running | fixed | Reported alongside D061 in one sentence, and it is the same wrong-owner mistake as D058 and D059 one device further out: `examples/10-open-world` starts its ambient wind at file scope, `WorldHost::boot` runs file scope before the first frame, and `AudioSystem::update` pushes voices on the RENDER clock rather than the tick -- so a paused world was inaudible in no sense at all. **Fixed 2026-08-22** with `AudioSystem::setSuspended`, a HOST control no script can reach: while suspended the mixer is handed an empty voice list and **not one `Sound` is touched**, so `Playing` and `TimePosition` are exactly where they were and pressing play resumes rather than restarts. That distinction is the difference between pausing and stopping, and getting it wrong would make every ambient loop jump back to its start every time somebody tested a change. `SoundService.Volume` stays the game's control; this is the tool's |
| D061 | human, 2026-08-22 | After pressing play and then stop, the editor camera flickers continuously | fixed | Two authors for one transform, and the pause did not separate them because it only gated the TICK. `RunService.PreRender` fires on the render clock, `examples/10-open-world` drives its follow camera from there, and the editor writes `CurrentCamera` at the frame's safe point -- so on every frame the script put the camera behind the character and the editor put it back, and the view alternated between two answers sixty times a second. It appeared only after a play because the editor camera is not adopted until then. **Fixed 2026-08-22 by making pause mean what it says**: a paused world runs no script phases, not even the render-rate one. Arbitrating who wins the camera was the other candidate and it is the wrong one -- a phase that still fires is the game still running, and no amount of deciding who wins would make that untrue. The same reasoning already governs the tick (D058), the cursor (D059) and the audio (D060), and the four of them are one rule: **while the editor is paused the tool owns the machine, and pressing play hands it back** |
| D062 | agent, 2026-08-22 | The game's input still reaches it while the editor is editing, so WASD would drive a character and the fly camera at once | fixed | Found by looking for the shape of D058, D059, D060 and D061 rather than by hitting it: those four are one architectural mistake appearing four times -- the editor inheriting the game's decisions instead of taking them -- and input was the fifth device with no owner declared. It had not been REPORTED because a paused world runs no ticks, so no action resolved; the moment somebody pressed play on a project with keyboard shortcuts, WASD would have gone to two places. **Fixed 2026-08-22** by the rule the other four now share: while the editor is editing the game receives no input at all, and `InputSystem::releaseAll` runs once on the way in so a key held when play stopped is not still held when play starts. That is the alt-tab case `releaseAll` was written for, arriving through a different door. ImGui is unaffected -- it takes the untranslated SDL stream of its own (`debug_overlay.cpp`), so the shell stays fully live while the game is not |
| D063 | human, 2026-08-22 | Holding the right mouse button to turn the editor camera hides the cursor but does not hold it: it moves, and reappears somewhere else | fixed | Reported twice, the second time with the detail that decided it -- "I do not see it but it moves". That separates two failures that look alike: hiding and holding are different calls, and only the first was working. **The log said the second one was working too**: `Pointer held.` and `Pointer released.` with no refusal, so `SDL_SetWindowRelativeMouseMode` was accepting. **The cause is SDL doing exactly what it documents.** In relative mode `SDL_mouse.c` accumulates a LOGICAL position from the motion -- `mouse->x += xrel; mouse->y += yrel;` clamped to the window -- and on the way out `SDL_SetRelativeMouseMode(false)` warps the real cursor to it: "Put the cursor back to where the application expects it". For a game whose camera is the mouse that is right; for an editor where the pointer is a tool it is not, because a look that turns the camera around leaves the cursor against a window edge instead of under the hand that was holding the button. **Fixed 2026-08-22 by anchoring**: the position is remembered while the pointer is FREE -- recording it during the hold would anchor to wherever the camera took it -- and `platform::setPointerPosition` puts it back when the hold ends. **And the diagnostic that found it stays**: `setPointerLocked`'s result was being discarded with a `(void)`, so a refused lock and a granted one looked identical from outside, and the difference only shows as a camera that stops turning at the edge of the screen -- a thing somebody reports and nobody can explain |
| D064 | human, 2026-08-22 | Right-clicking anything in the editor does nothing: no context menu appears | fixed | Reported twice, and the two reports had different causes. **The first was a condition I wrote wrong.** The camera's look test read "over the viewport OR dragging", and ImGui's `IsMouseDragging` becomes true a pixel after a press ANYWHERE -- so a right-click in the explorer with a tremor of the hand became a camera turn: the pointer locked, motion stopped reaching ImGui (D063's fix), and the menu that click was asking for never appeared. Latching on the PRESS fixes it, and the press is the only moment the question can be answered -- once the pointer is held it stops reporting a position. **The second was worse and was a process failure.** The patch that added the two item menus asserted mid-way and never wrote the file, because it writes at the end; only the folder menu -- applied separately -- had landed. It compiled and every test passed, because the signature it would have changed had not changed either, so nothing could disagree. I reported the menus as done on the strength of having RUN the patch rather than having read the result. The scripts that edit this repository now re-read the file from disk after writing and fail if what they added is not there: **an edit that cannot be verified is an edit that did not happen** |
| D065 | human, 2026-08-22 | The editor lets you delete, duplicate and rename the engine's own services | fixed | Asked as three questions in a row -- "I should not be able to delete the basic services", "the Workspace counts too, right?", "renaming as well, I think" -- and the three have three different reasons, which is why each is stated rather than covered by one sentence. **Deleting** leaves a world that cannot answer a call every script makes. **Duplicating** makes "one per world" false, so every `GetService` becomes a question with two answers. **Renaming** breaks `game.Workspace`, which is a lookup BY NAME and not a keyword, in files the editor cannot see. **Fixed 2026-08-22 by asking the IDL**: a class tagged `Service` carries `ClassFlags::Service` into the generated descriptors, so `Workspace`, `Lighting`, `UIService`, `AudioService`, `RunService`, `TagService` and `ScriptService` are covered with no special case and a service added later joins the rule on its own. A list of names would have gone stale the first time somebody added one. **The rule lives in `Editor`, where the world changes, and the menu only reflects it** -- greyed rather than refused afterwards; a rule that lived in the menu would be bypassed by every other path to the same action. **And the first version was wrong in a way the tests caught**: it read "has no parent" as "is the world's root", which would have refused to delete anything somebody had detached, because an instance parented to nil is a loose instance and not a root. The root is passed in now rather than inferred |
| D066 | agent, 2026-08-22 | `openworld_soak` fails its instance-growth check under machine load and passes immediately afterwards | quarantined | Failed once in E1's closing gate -- "the world grew from 4269 to 5385 instances between the second and the fourth quarter of the run; a streamed world flattens" -- and passed twice in a row when re-run alone, minutes later, on the same binary and the same tree. **Not quarantined**: `MASTER_PROMPT.md` §12 sets that bar at flaking twice, and this is once. Recorded so the second time is recognised as a second time rather than as a first. **The hypothesis the evidence supports, and it is labelled as one**: streaming's materialisation budget is denominated in MILLISECONDS rather than in chunks (`architecture.md` §10, M7). Under load fewer chunks materialise per frame, the resident set lags the focus, and the balance between materialising and evicting shifts across the quarters -- which is exactly the quantity this check compares. The failing run shared the machine with a parallel container build and an interactive editor; the passing runs had it to themselves. **If that is right the test is measuring the machine as well as the engine**, and the fix is for the check to be denominated in something load cannot move -- not for the threshold to be widened until it stops complaining, which would remove the only instrument that watches a streamed world for a leak. **Second flake 2026-08-22, and it was recognised as one because this row existed.** 4,269 to 5,306 instances in a gate run, then 4,269 to 4,222 on the very next run with the machine to itself -- same binary, same tree, and 5,939 frames both times, which is the part that settles it: the frame COUNT is deterministic and the resident set is not, so what differs is how far behind materialisation fell. **Quarantined per MASTER_PROMPT.md §12**, and quarantined rather than weakened: `SoakVerdict` gained a `quarantined` list beside `failures`, the check still runs, still measures, still logs at warn level and is still in the JSON report -- it no longer fails the gate. `soak_tests.cpp`'s D032 case now asserts exactly that distinction, so the complaint cannot go quiet without a test saying so. **What replaces it has to be denominated in something load cannot move**, and the honest candidate is a focus path that RETURNS to where it started: a resident set that does not come back with it is a leak whatever the millisecond budget did. |
| D067 | human, 2026-08-22 | Pressing play in the editor on `examples/10-open-world` prints `init.luau:566: [script.err.instance_dead]` once a frame and nothing happens -- the viewport only changes colour | fixed | **Reproduced byte for byte before it was touched, and the reproduction is the diagnosis.** With the project's scene named in `luaug.toml`, 120 headless frames of the flagship produce 89 copies of the reported line, from the reported file, at the reported line number; with the fix, zero. The chain is one ordering mistake. `WorldHost::boot` runs every entry script's file scope in its own drain BEFORE the first frame, so `init.luau` had already built the walker, the lantern post, the welds, the follow camera and the input contexts. `engine.cpp` then applied the boot scene AFTER that drain -- and a scene load REPLACES the contents of `Workspace` (`scene_file.cpp:559`, and it has to, or a second load would double everything), so `clearScene` destroyed every one of them. **The Luau VM is not restored by any of this**: it went on holding the references and its `Heartbeat` connection stayed connected, so the first tick that ran raised `instance_dead` on `lantern`, aborted the handler before the camera, the movement and the streaming focus, and left only the two lines above it -- `ClockTime` and `FogColor` -- still landing. That is precisely "the background changes colour and nothing else happens". The streamed world could not arrive either: `StreamingService:AddFocus(character)` had registered an instance the clear destroyed, so `collectFoci` dropped it and the resident set had nothing to follow. **The fix is the order ADR 0047 already specifies and `scene_file.h` already states in its opening paragraph** -- "boot reads it and then starts the scripts". `WorldHostOptions` carries `bootScene` and `WorldHost::boot` applies it after the project mount and the preserved restore and BEFORE `startScripts`, so there is nothing alive to invalidate and a script that builds its world builds it on top of the file. E1 shipped the inversion knowingly and said so; what it did not have was a project where the two orders disagree. **Two things it fixes that were never reported**: a hot reload used to drop the scene entirely, because the load lived outside the host the reload rebuilds, and it now travels in the options `reloadWorld` copies; and an entry script can finally see the world it was given, which is what makes a scene worth having. Break-verified: with the block moved back after the drain, three of the four new cases in `world_host_tests.cpp` fail |
| D068 | human, 2026-08-22 | Save Scene As wrote the flagship's scene into `content/content/scenes/`, where nothing will ever look for it | fixed | Found in the wreckage of D067 and it is the reason that project had a scene at all. The dialog labels its box `content/` and then resolved what was typed against the content root, so the natural thing to type -- `content/scenes/main` -- became `<project>/content/content/scenes/main.scene.json`, and `createDirectories` made the folder without a word. The forensics are on disk: a 5,093-byte full world at the doubled path at 10:52, and 32 seconds later a 170-byte empty one at the right path, which is exactly what `New Scene` followed by a second `Save As` writes. **Nothing validated the string at all** -- not a leading `/`, not a drive letter, not `..`, none of which `ContentTree::isUsableName` would have accepted from the New Folder box three hundred lines away. `Editor::normalizeScenePath` and `Editor::sceneNameIsUsable` are static and pure so the dialog previews the RESOLVED path while it is being typed and disables Save on a path that leaves `content/`, which is the half that makes the rule visible rather than surprising |
| D069 | human, 2026-08-22 | In play mode with the pointer locked, the mouse still reaches the Explorer and the Properties panel -- hovering rows and clicking things nobody is pointing at | fixed | **The other half of D063, and the worse half.** A locked pointer is SDL's relative mode: the cursor is hidden and does not move, and SDL goes on posting motion carrying a logical position it accumulates from the deltas. That position walks across the panels, and ImGui hovers and clicks whatever it walks over -- so a player turning their head is dragging through the explorer at the same time. D063 fixed this for the EDITOR's own right-drag and `handleEvents` has withheld motion while `lookInput().active` ever since; nothing covered the case where the GAME holds the lock, which lasts as long as somebody is playing rather than as long as a button is held. **Fixed 2026-08-22**: the frame loop already answers the question -- `wantLocked && !editorOwnsPointer` -- so it tells the overlay rather than leaving it to infer, and it tells it BEFORE `handleEvents` runs later in the same frame, so there is no lag on either edge. While the game holds it, motion, wheel and button PRESSES are withheld and the cursor is pushed off-screen with ImGui's own `-FLT_MAX`, because withholding motion alone would leave whichever row was under the cursor when play was pressed lit for the whole session. A RELEASE is always delivered, in both the editor's case and the game's: ImGui must never be left believing a button it was never told about is held. **And Escape stops play now**, which is what Unreal does and what this change made necessary rather than merely nice -- a project that never hands the cursor back would otherwise have had no way to reach the stop button, and the keyboard still arrives |
| D070 | human, 2026-08-22 | After play then stop in the editor, the flagship's character capsule flickers continuously in a world that is not running | fixed | **The code had already written down the obligation nobody met.** `world.h:424-428` lists `render::TransformHistory` among the caches that are "rebuilt from the tree rather than restored" and gives the order in so many words -- "safe order: restore, then rebuild". `TransformHistory::clear()` had no caller anywhere in production. So `Editor::stop`'s `world.restore` put the capsule back at its spawn while the history still held where it had walked to, and a snapshot preserves generations PRECISELY so an id means the same thing afterwards -- which is exactly what let the stale entry go on answering `previous()`. **The second half is the alpha**: `FrameScheduler` drains its accumulator every frame whether or not the editor let a tick through, so `frame.alpha` goes on sweeping the whole of [0, 1) at render rate. Interpolating between two poses metres apart with a factor that changes every frame is the flicker, and it was only ever the three instances that MOVE during play -- the walker, its capsule and the lantern post -- because `render_world.cpp`'s early-out skips a part whose two ends are identical, which is the whole anchored terrain. **Fixed 2026-08-22 in both halves.** A world that is not advancing has nothing to interpolate, so `renderAlpha` is zero unless `advancing(runState())` -- which also settles the smaller tremor a paused frame had against a history one tick old. And the history is dropped when `Inspector::worldGeneration` moves, which is the signal every world-replacing path already raises: stop, undo, redo, load, new scene and hot reload, all at once and without inventing a second notion of "this is not the world it was" for the two to disagree about. `editor_tests` holds that counter as a contract, because a path added later that restored without announcing it would bring this straight back |
| D071 | human, 2026-08-23 | Ctrl+Z collapses the whole Explorer | fixed | The tree drops its expanded set whenever `Inspector::worldGeneration` moves, and undo moves it -- so every undo also closed every row somebody had opened, on a scene where finding the thing again is the expensive part. **The counter was answering two questions with one number.** A reload or a scene load builds a DIFFERENT world: slot indices restart from zero, so a set keyed by instance index would hand a dead row's expansion to whatever moved into its slot, and dropping it is right. An undo, a redo and a stop are `World::restore`, which carries generations and the free list *precisely* so that an id means the same thing afterwards -- `world.h` says so where the snapshot is defined -- so every row the set names is still that row. **Fixed 2026-08-23** by splitting the two: `worldGeneration()` still counts every wholesale change of the world's VALUES and is what the frame loop's `TransformHistory` reads (D070 is what happens when that one is missed), and `worldIdentity()` counts only the worlds that are actually different, which is what anything keyed by an id reads. `onWorldRestored()` bumps the first and not the second, and unlike `onWorldChanged` it leaves the selection to `pruneDead` rather than clearing it -- so an undo now keeps what you had selected too, which was the same defect one panel over and had not been reported yet. `editor_tests` holds the distinction, because a path added later that restores through the wrong one would collapse the tree again and nothing else would notice |
| D072 | human, 2026-08-23 | Opening the content browser fills the log with `Calling PopStyleColor() too many times!` | fixed | Mine, and one edit old: the open scene's green name was pushed once and popped twice. When the browser's rows were restructured so the name is DRAWN on the row rather than carried as the selectable's label, the colour had to be popped after the name instead of after the selectable -- and the new pop was added without the old one being removed. ImGui caught it in the open, once per row per frame, which is the best version of this mistake. **Fixed 2026-08-23**; the pop that coloured nothing is gone |
| D073 | human, 2026-08-23 | Selecting one instance in `examples/10-open-world` makes others disappear -- every boulder and every tree canopy leaves the frame | fixed | Mine, and one commit old. The first cut of the selection silhouette excluded an outlined draw from instance batching, on the argument that a batch is drawn or skipped whole and selecting one of five identical crates does not select the other four. That argument is right and the place was wrong: a run of identical meshes is how a whole forest is drawn, and splitting one -- in `buildInstanceBatches`, which every pass of the frame depends on -- took the split mesh out of the frame entirely. Reported with two screenshots a second apart, which is what made it unmistakable. **Fixed 2026-08-23 by not touching the batching at all**: `instanceable` is back to transparent-and-skinned, and the OUTLINE pass ignores batching instead -- it treats every draw as its own, which costs a handful of calls because only what is selected is drawn there. The cost belongs to the tool's pass and not to the one the whole frame is built on. `outline_mask_instanced` went with it, and the capture goldens are re-recorded: the only lines that changed are the shader and pipeline creations |
| D074 | human, 2026-08-23 | Saving a scene in `examples/10-open-world`, closing and reopening leaves the world with two of everything -- two characters | fixed | **Not a regression: the honest cost of D067's fix, arriving where it always would have.** Once the scene loads BEFORE the entry scripts -- which is ADR 0047's lifecycle and what D067 corrected -- a project whose CODE builds the world has two sources for one world. The scene puts back the character it saved and then `init.luau` makes another, and the engine cannot merge them: it has no way to know that a `CharacterBody` the file names and a `CharacterBody` the script makes are meant to be the same one. **The engine now says so, with both numbers**, at boot and once (`scene.warn.two_sources`), because "the file gave you 6 and the scripts added 5" is actionable and "something may be duplicated" is not. **What it does NOT yet have is the mechanism a project needs to resolve it**, and that is the open half: a script has no way to ask whether the world it is about to build has already been authored, so the only resolutions today are to stop building that world in code or to give the project no scene. `examples/06-scene` is the shape ADR 0047 asks projects to become and it has neither problem. **Fixed 2026-08-23, the way Unity and Unreal have it** (human decision): the world is the FILE and the script is what it does with it. `examples/10-open-world`'s character, its capsule, its lantern, the two welds, its camera and its whole input map are now `content/scenes/main.scene.json`, `luaug.toml` names it, and `init.luau` FINDS them by the names it agreed to instead of making them -- ninety lines of construction became six lookups and an assert per name, so a scene missing something says which. Three things had to move with it. `luaug-host --save-scene=PATH` is the migration itself: a project whose world is in its code had no way to get that world into a file, and retyping it into JSON is not a migration anybody performs -- it boots, lets the scripts build, and writes through the same `writeScene` the editor's Save calls. `@luaug/camera` ADOPTS `workspace.CurrentCamera` when the scene already holds one, or every scene-first project would get a second camera that silently wins. And the cold-boot teleport to the spawn is gone: it was right while the script owned the world and is a save that did not save now that a file does. **The warning stays** -- it is what proves the fix, and it is what the next project to grow two sources will see |
| D075 | human, 2026-08-23 | A child added to a `Folder` in the Workspace never appears -- "porque em folder na workspace eu não consigo adicionar filho??" | fixed | **The plus worked; what did not was SEEING what it made, and the reported symptom is indistinguishable from the reported cause.** `Editor::createInstance` accepts a `Folder` parent -- it refuses only an unknown or uncreatable class and a `generated` parent -- and it selected the new instance, so the properties grid was showing it. The Explorer was not. The expanded set is only ever written by two things: the chevron somebody clicks, and the root at depth zero. **A row that has never been opened is not opened by gaining a child**, and an EMPTY row has no chevron at all to open it with -- so a `Part` created inside a fresh `Folder` was invisible EVERY time rather than intermittently, which is what made this read as "the button does not work". Diagnosed by reading, and the mechanism is deterministic enough that reading settles it: there is no state in which an empty folder could have been open. **Fixed 2026-08-23** the way every editor with a tree fixes it -- making a thing reveals it. `Inspector::reveal` is a one-shot request and `collectAncestors` is the way down; the panel drains it BEFORE it decides visibility, so the row appears on the frame the plus was pressed rather than one later. One-shot because a request that stayed set would re-open, every frame, a row somebody had deliberately closed; and dropped by `onWorldChanged` because a different world recycles slot indices and the id would name something else. **`createInstance`, `duplicateInstances` and `reparent` all raise it**: dropping something into a collapsed folder and watching it vanish is the same defect arriving through the other verb, and it shipped in the same session as the drag that makes it reachable. Break-verified by removing the one call and watching `editor_tests` fail |
| D076 | human, 2026-08-23 | A `UDim` or `UDim2` in the properties grid is two or four unlabelled boxes -- there is no way to tell scale from offset | fixed | **A box is for a value, and nothing on the screen said which value.** `x y z` has a convention to fall back on and a scale-and-offset pair does not, so `UIPadding.PaddingTop` drawn as two stacked boxes holding `0.000` and `10.000` is a widget nobody can read. The first fix put the word INSIDE the box -- `scale 0.000` -- and the human rejected it in one sentence, correctly: that is a box with a caption in it, and it spends the width the number needs. **Fixed 2026-08-23 the way every properties grid does it**: the name goes in the name column where every other name in this panel already is, and a composite value expands into a row per number -- `Scale` and `Offset` for a `UDim`, `X Scale` through `Y Offset` for a `UDim2` -- with the collapsed line carrying the same summary `formatValue` has printed since M6, so the two agree by construction. Open by default, because starting closed puts a click between a person and the property they opened the panel for. **What this found is worth more than the fix.** `inspector_fixture.h` has said "one property of **every** `ValueType`" since M4, with a `static_assert` on `std::variant_size_v<Value>` under it -- and counting is not covering. M6 appended `Vector2`, `UDim`, `UDim2` and `Rect` to the variant and no property in the fixture ever named them, so **four editor branches had been executed by no test at all**, including the one being rewritten. That is the M4 brief's entering risk 6 word for word, found exactly the way it predicted. All four are in the fixture now and the real-device overlay test draws every one of them |
| D077 | human, 2026-08-23 | The Explorer offers a plus INSIDE a streamed chunk -- `Chunk_-3_0_0/Ground` takes a child that the save skips and the next eviction destroys | fixed | **Reported as the opposite of what it is**: "the plus does not appear for a folder", then corrected on the next line -- it does not appear on the `Chunk_*` rows, and a folder made inside one had it. The chunk rows are RIGHT: nothing authored may live in what streaming materialised. What was wrong is everything under them. `Editor::authorable` walks the ANCESTRY and its own comment says why -- "a chunk marks its FOLDER and not its contents, which is exactly the economy that makes checking the instance alone wrong" -- and both the plus and `createInstance` asked `world.generated(id)` of the INSTANCE. So `Ground`, which streaming did not mark, wore a plus, accepted the create, and the thing somebody made was skipped by the serializer and destroyed by the next eviction without a word. The one row that looks dangerous was refused and the ones that do not were not. **Fixed 2026-08-23** by naming the rule once: `Editor::canParentInto` is the ancestry walk with no opinion about services -- a service IS a legal parent, `Workspace` holding a `Part` is what it is for -- and `authorable` is now that plus `!isEngineOwned`, so the two cannot drift. The plus, `createInstance` and `reparent` all read it. **And the chunks are hidden from the Explorer now** (`Window > Streamed Content`, off by default), which answers the question the human asked next: streaming pumps in EDIT mode as well as in play, deliberately -- a world you cannot see is a world you cannot edit, which is what Unreal's World Partition does in its own editor -- but a chunk is not part of the authored scene, the serializer skips it whole, and sixty `Chunk_x_y_z` folders standing between a person and the four things they wrote is the complaint the root's own hidden row already answered. A switch rather than a rule, because "what did streaming actually materialise" is a real question with no other way to ask it |
| D078 | human, 2026-08-23 | A part dragged by a manipulator arm does not stay on the arm -- "não segue exatamente a reta da movimentação, vai todo estranho para o sentido da seta" | fixed | **A comment that said the opposite of its own code.** `Editor::driveGizmo`'s translate branch read "Snapped in the GIZMO's frame rather than the world's, so a local drag lands on the part's own grid" and then called `snapTo(delta, step)`, which quantised each WORLD component independently. That is right for a world-axis drag -- the delta is axis-aligned, so rounding the three apart keeps it on the line -- and wrong for every other one: an arm in LOCAL space points diagonally through the world, so the three roundings pull the motion off the arm and the part wanders. Snapping is on by default at a quarter metre, so this was reachable the first time anybody turned a crate and dragged it. **Reproduced before it was touched**, and the reproduction is the shape of the test: a drag made DIAGONALLY on screen, because that is what a hand does and an arm is supposed to hold the motion to its line anyway, with the movement decomposed into "along the arm" and "across it". Across must be zero; it was zero in world space with and without snapping and non-zero in the part's own. **Fixed 2026-08-23** by doing what the comment promised: the delta is expressed in the gizmo's own basis, snapped there and turned back. An axis drag stays on its axis and a plane drag stays in its plane, because a component that was zero rounds to zero; in world mode the basis is the identity and the code is exactly what it was. **And the same question asked of the other two modes found a second defect** (D079). Rotate is right and was checked: the angle is a scalar, so snapping it cannot move anything off anything, and the turn is about the gizmo's own axis around the gizmo's own pivot |
| D079 | agent, 2026-08-23 | Resizing a rotated part with the world-space gizmo grows it along the wrong axis | fixed | Found by asking D078's question of the other two modes rather than by anybody hitting it, which is the cheaper way round. **A `Size` is three numbers in the part's OWN frame** -- there is no world-space size to change -- so `driveGizmo`'s scale branch projected the drag onto whatever axis the gizmo was showing and then wrote it into `size.x`, `size.y` or `size.z`, which are the part's. On an unrotated part the two agree and nothing looks wrong; on a turned one the arm points one way and the part grows another, and the handle is lying about what it does. **Fixed 2026-08-23**: `gizmoFrame` uses the part's own rotation whenever the mode is `Scale`, whatever the world/local toggle says -- which is what Unity's scale tool does and for this reason. The toggle greys out while resizing rather than reading "world" over local handles: a control that stayed live while changing nothing would lie twice, once by looking usable and once by naming the wrong space. Break-verified: three assertions fail with the rule removed |
| D080 | human, 2026-08-24 | Editing a stamp on its stage and saving does not move the linked instances already in the world -- "editei a stamp do content e não atualizou a stamp da workspace" | fixed | **Half of ADR 0051 was only true across a save and a LOAD.** "Changing a stamp changes every instance of it" was implemented where the format could see both trees -- the scene reader builds a stamped node from the file every time it opens one -- and nowhere else. A linked instance sitting in a running editor is an ordinary subtree carrying a mark, and nothing re-read the file while it sat there; `scene_file_tests` proved the rule on a round trip and the claim read as though it held generally. **Fixed 2026-08-24** with `scene::restamp`, which is the same arithmetic done live: what each instance has of its own is measured against the file it was built from, its children are rebuilt from the file as it stands now, and the overrides go back on top. **`before` is why the session carries the file's previous text** -- an instance that differs from the new file is either overridden or merely out of date, and only the text it was built from tells those apart. Rebuilt IN PLACE rather than destroyed and re-read: the instance keeps its id, its parent and its place among its siblings, so a reference held to it survives and an Explorer's rows do not jump on every save. A subtree whose shape has moved on is left alone and said out loud in the status line, which is the rule the writer already applied, from the other side. **Two limits, stated rather than found later**: a refresh is not an undo step (it is a change to a file, and the stage's history is what an undo here belongs to), and a reference held to something INSIDE a refreshed instance goes with the children that were replaced |
| D081 | human, 2026-08-24 | The content browser shows `charactertesting.stamp.json`, which reads as a JSON file rather than as a stamp | fixed | **`.json` is how the file is stored and `.stamp` is what it is**, and the browser is the one place that has to say the second. Reported alongside D080 and D082 in one message, all three about the same row. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: `ContentTree::displayNameOf` drops the trailing `.json` of a COMPOUND suffix -- asked of `extensionFor`, the one answer to "what part of this name says what it is", so a `.json` that is somebody's own data file keeps it. The name on disk is untouched, and the grid view's tooltip still shows it whole: this is a label, and every path, rename and load spells the whole thing |
| D082 | human, 2026-08-24 | A stamp in the content browser wears a generic icon; the same thing in the Explorer wears its class's, badged | fixed | Reported twice in one exchange and the second report is the better statement of it: not "the badge is missing" but **"o icone da stamp deveria ser o icone da instancia raiz"** -- a file of a character should draw as a character. The panel drew `class.Model` for every stamp, which was written as an honest stand-in for a `content.Stamp` drawing the set owes; the drawing is not what was missing. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: a stamp row wears `class.<its root's class>`, read from the head of the file at refresh and cached on the entry, with the kind's own icon as the fallback and the stamp badge drawn over it. **A prefix rather than a parse**, and the size is the argument: a stamp of a whole character is hundreds of kilobytes and a folder can hold forty of them. The badge is what now carries "this is a file one comes from" -- the same mark the Explorer puts on an instance, read the other way round -- so `content.Stamp` is no longer owed at all |
| D083 | human, 2026-08-24 | The editor opens on `stats` rather than `properties`, and forgets the manipulator's space and the window's geometry between launches | fixed | Asked for as a standing rule rather than as a bug -- "o layout em si do editor deve ser lembrado ... por padrão" -- and it is three things wearing one sentence. **The docking, the panel sizes and which tab is open were already remembered**: ImGui writes `.luaug/layout.ini` for `Shell::Editor` and that includes a dock node's selected tab, so this half was working and only its DEFAULT was wrong -- a tab node opens on whichever window was docked last, and `stats` was docked after `properties` by accident of call order. **The manipulator's mode and space, snapping and the browser's layout were remembered nowhere**, which is the half that was actually missing: they are somebody's answer to "how do I work", and an editor that asks it again every launch is one they answer by hand every morning. **The OS window's own geometry was remembered nowhere either**, and the layout file cannot hold it -- that one knows about the panels INSIDE a window and nothing about the window. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: the tab is chosen explicitly rather than falling out of dock order; the toolbar state and the browser's view go in `.luaug/editor.json` as WORDS a person can read (`"space": "local"`, not a flag); the window's placement goes there too, read by a static before the window is created so it OPENS at the right size rather than opening small and jumping. Three details that are the difference between it working and nearly working: the preferences are drained by one flag once a frame rather than written from inside each keystroke handler, the placement is asked for once a frame and compared rather than subscribed to (a move and a resize are several events each and none of them is the last), and a maximised window keeps the geometry it will have when it is NOT maximised -- SDL reports the screen it fills, and storing that hands somebody who un-maximises it a window the size of their display. The ini is also written on the way out now, because ImGui saves it on a five-second timer and an arrangement made just before quitting is one made twice |
| D084 | agent, 2026-08-24 | The partitioner leaves in the boot scene everything it moved into cells, so a partitioned world is loaded TWICE | fixed | Found while proving that `examples/10-open-world` really streams after its terrain moved into `Workspace` (ADR 0053). The boot log is right and the file is not: **"Partitioned the scene into 115 cell(s) holding 15363 instance(s); 204 stayed in the scene"**, and the scene it then writes and boots -- `.luaug/partition/<hash>/scene.json`, written at `partition_cache.cpp:229` and returned as `outcome.scenePath` -- still holds **15,568** instances, of which 15,541 are the `Scenery` folder the cells were made from. Both halves are live: a 3,000-frame soak reports `earlyInstances 25311 -> peakInstances 26498`, which is the whole scene plus a resident ring of cells materialised on top of it. **Streaming itself is healthy** and that is what makes this easy to miss -- `worstStreamingMs 1.712` against a 2 ms budget, `hitches 0`, and the resident count rises and falls as the focus moves. What it costs is the frame: median 16.1 ms with the duplicate against 10.3 ms measured on the same world before the autopilot's long leg, and roughly twice the instance memory. The duplicate is invisible because the copies are coincident -- a duplicated `Ground` tile is exactly under the streamed one, so the picture is right and only the counters say otherwise. **Mine, and the walk asked one question where it needed two.** `visitChildren` tracked whether a direct child had been REMOVED and nothing else, so a child that stayed and was itself rebuilt -- because something under IT left -- left the parent believing nothing had happened, and the parent then copied its own original text over the top of that rebuild. Every part of `Scenery` was correctly emitted into a cell, correctly spliced out of `Scenery`, and then the spliced `Scenery` was discarded when `Workspace` wrote itself out verbatim. A node now reports which of three things it did -- removed, verbatim, or rewritten -- and a parent rebuilds for either of the last two reasons. **Every test in the suite had its streamable parts as direct children of `Workspace`**, which is the shape the flagship stopped having when its terrain moved into a folder; three tests now put a part one, two and three folders down, and a fourth asserts the property this defect really wants -- partitioning an already partitioned scene moves nothing, byte for byte. Measured after: the boot scene holds 204 instances against 15,568, and the flagship runs at a median 4.80 ms against the 16.1 ms recorded here. **Confirmed independently by the reporter** on a cold partition and a second 3,000-frame soak of the same world: median **16.1 -> 5.90 ms**, p99 **23.6 -> 9.18**, worst **64.9 -> 11.07**, peak resident 181 -> 168 MiB. The instance count still rises and falls with the focus (11,102 -> 7,807), which is the streaming this defect was found while trying to prove |
| D085 | human, 2026-08-24 | Pressing F3 in `examples/10-open-world` turns the whole window black | fixed | Reported while trying to watch streaming happen. **Not reproduced here and it cannot be**: the ImGui shell does not render headlessly and SDL does not accept injected input (E1's brief), so there is no way from this side to press F3. What follows is what reading ruled out and what it did not. **Three obvious causes are eliminated.** A saved layout with a huge window: `io.IniFilename` is null for every shell but the editor (`debug_overlay.cpp:3499`), so the panel is 420x520 on every launch. The overlay clearing the frame under itself: its pass is `loadOp = LoadOp::Load` with a comment saying why (`:3641`). A dockspace covering the viewport: `DockSpaceOverViewport` is inside `drawEditorShell`, and a game shell calls `drawShell`, which opens one window. **What is left is the instance count, and it is very likely self-inflicted.** `drawShell` calls `drawExplorer` with `showGenerated = true` -- deliberately, to show the world as it IS rather than as it was authored -- and the Explorer's row pass is `collectTree(world, root, g_rows)`, the WHOLE tree every frame, with `g_visible` filtered from it afterwards. The flagship's terrain moved into `Workspace` the same day (ADR 0053) and D084 duplicates it, so that world now runs at roughly 24,000 instances against the few hundred this panel was built against. A frame that stops being presented is a black window. **The discriminating test is thirty seconds**: press F3 in `examples/06-scene`. Working there and not here is the instance count; black in both is the overlay and predates this  **The discriminating test was in the log, and it says EDITOR.** `luaug.log` from the session carries `81 icon(s) in a 1024-square atlas`, and the icon atlas is built behind `options.editor` and nowhere else (`engine.cpp:2019`); it also carries `Loaded a scene of 15567 instance(s)` with no `Partitioned the scene into...` line, which is the editor skipping the partition on purpose. So `drawShell` was never running and the instance count is not this -- the reporter pressed play in the EDITOR and then F3. **And in the editor F3 hides the only thing that draws the world.** The editor renders into an offscreen TEXTURE sized to the viewport panel, the screen itself is only cleared to `{0.06, 0.06, 0.07}` as a backdrop (`engine.cpp:2116`), and `DebugOverlay::render` returned early on `!visible_` -- so hiding the panels left that clear as the entire picture. A black window, exactly as reported, and true since E1 rather than anything ADR 0053 did. The overlay's own header calls F3 "the cheapest way to look at the world without the furniture", which is the one thing it could not do. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: the editor shell now draws while hidden, and what it draws is the viewport alone -- a borderless window over the whole screen, sharing `drawViewportBody` with the docked panel so the aspect ratio, the picking ray and the image agree whichever is up. A window of its own rather than the docked one undocked, because the layout somebody arranged is theirs and a keystroke that rearranged it would cost more than it showed. **What this rules out is worth keeping**: the Explorer walking the whole tree every frame in `drawShell` is a real cost at twenty-four thousand instances and it is not what happened here; it gets its own row when somebody hits it. |
| D086 | agent, 2026-08-24 | The released v1.0.0 binary reports its version as 0.0.1: `project(LuauG VERSION ...)` was never bumped | fixed | Found while writing the packager (ADR 0054), which names the folder after that declaration and produced `LuauG-0.0.1-win64` for an engine tagged `v1.0.0` and released on 2026-08-22. It is not a stale copy -- ADR 0031's rule held perfectly, and everything downstream reported the number faithfully: `LUAUG_VERSION_STRING` in the generated `build_info.h`, `luaug --version`, `api/api-dump.json`, and the engine's own boot line. **What had no owner was the declaration itself.** A version that only ever moves when somebody remembers to move it is exactly the shape this project keeps designing against, and the reason nobody noticed for a whole release is that the one artifact that would have put the number in front of a person -- a folder named after it -- did not exist until now. **Fixed**: bumped to 1.0.0 with the api-dump regenerated beside it. What is NOT fixed is the mechanism: nothing checks that a `vX.Y.Z` tag and the declaration agree, and the honest next step is a docs-gate rule comparing the newest tag against `CMakeLists.txt` |
| D087 | human, 2026-08-24 | `luaug edit examples/10-open-world` opens a black window | fixed | Reported the day the packaging milestone landed, and caused by it: `scripts/package.ps1` builds the `player` profile, and `findEngine` had been trying player presets FIRST since D057 -- which cost nothing for as long as no machine in the world had a player build. The moment one did, every `luaug edit` and `luaug dev` launched a binary with `LUAUG_DEBUG_UI=OFF`, so there is no ImGui target linked into it at all and `DebugOverlay` degrades to a class whose methods do nothing. In edit mode the world renders into an offscreen texture and the screen receives only the backdrop clear, so the panels are the only thing that draws anything: no panels, no picture. It ran, it exited zero, and it was black. **Reproduced before it was diagnosed**: the CLI's own resolution reported `win-msvc-player/engine/app/luaug-host.exe`, which is D085's window with a different cause -- the same symptom, the second time, from the opposite direction. **Fixed** in two parts, because ordering alone would only move the luck: the player presets are now the LAST thing `findEngine` will take, after dev and after editor; and `luaug edit` asks the binary what it is rather than inferring it from where it was found -- `hostProfile` parses the profile out of `--version`, and a host with no debug UI is refused by name with the two commands that fix it. `luaug dev` warns instead of refusing, because a player host does run a project; what it cannot do is F3, the inspector and the REPL. Break-verified with `LUAUG_HOST` pointed at the player build, which is now refused rather than launched. **The general shape**: a preference that is right only because nothing has exercised the alternative is a preference nothing is holding. |
| D088 | human, 2026-08-24 | A blank project opened in the editor shows a background cycling through colours instead of a world | fixed | Reported minutes after E6 shipped the launcher, and reachable for the first time BECAUSE of it: until there was a New Project button, nobody opened a project that had no scene and no camera in it. The rainbow is `pulseColor` -- the M1 "the engine is alive" clear, which `engine.cpp` falls back to whenever `snapshot.camera.valid` is false, and which every example since M2 has hidden by having a camera. **The chain is four links and each one is reasonable.** `editor.adoptCamera` is seeded from `Workspace.CurrentCamera` so that opening a project does not teleport the view (D061's design); a project with no scene has no camera to adopt, so `cameraAdopted()` stayed false forever; `useEditorView` requires it, so `extract` received no `ViewOverride`; and `extract` with neither a world camera nor an override leaves `out.camera.valid` false, which is the pulse. **Fixed**: with nothing to adopt, the editor adopts its OWN default frame, so the editor always has a camera -- which is what ADR 0046's "the tool owns the machine while it is editing" already implied, and the adopt-if-present path is untouched. **The empty world itself is not the defect**: a project whose world is built in script is empty in the editor until play, which is ADR 0047 working as designed. What was wrong is that emptiness rendered as a rainbow. |
| D089 | agent, 2026-08-24 | `core::JsonDocument::parse` refuses a UTF-8 byte-order mark, so an engine JSON edited in Notepad is ignored in silence | fixed | Recorded as a finding by the other session while building the appearance file and fixed here, because it is `core`'s rather than the shell's: **every** JSON this engine reads goes through one parser -- the scene format, the chunk index, the partition manifest, the project list, `appearance.json` -- and all of them are files a person may open in an editor. Notepad writes a BOM by default, so does Windows PowerShell's `Out-File` for UTF-8, and so does Visual Studio; the parser saw three bytes where a value belongs and answered `expected a value at byte 0`, which every caller here turns into "there is no such file" and carries on. **RFC 8259 leaves this to the implementation** -- it says a parser MAY ignore a BOM -- so the old behaviour was not wrong, it was useless against the files people actually have. **Fixed**: the prefix is skipped at the start of `parse` and nowhere else. A BOM in the middle of a document is a zero-width no-break space in somebody's data and is left alone, and a document that is nothing but a mark is still not a document. Break-verified: with the skip removed the first case reports `expected a value at byte 0`, which is the symptom itself. |
| D090 | human, 2026-08-24 | Double-clicking the packaged `luaug-host.exe` opens a console that closes immediately | fixed | Reported against a folder that was HALF DELETED, and the packager did that: `removeTree` let `removeDirectory` walk the tree and fail when it reached `luaug-host.exe`, which Windows will not unlink while it is running. What was left had the binary and none of the content -- so the host started, found no icon theme and no fonts, could not bring the shell up, and exited. **A half-deleted installation is worse than one that was not cleared at all**, because it behaves like a defect in the engine rather than like a step that failed. **Fixed**: the binaries that can be locked are removed FIRST, one at a time, so a failure there has deleted nothing and says which file is in use and what to do. The earlier half of this -- the stack trace from inside `fs.remove` -- was already replaced by a sentence in the same session; this is the half that mattered. |
| D091 | human, 2026-08-24 | Pressing play in a project with no camera shows a background cycling through colours | fixed | The other half of D088, and it survived that fix because the fix was scoped to editing. `useEditorView` required `editing(runState)` -- deliberately, because pressing play must show the GAME through the game's own camera rather than a tool's idea of it -- so play mode dropped the override, a project whose scripts never made a `Camera` had nothing to render through, and the frame fell back to the M1 "the engine is alive" pulse. **Fixed**: the editor's camera is the fallback and never the winner. A game with a camera is still shown through its own; a game with none is shown through the editor's, which is what every editor in this shape does and what was asked for in those words. The breadcrumb rectangle reported in the same message is the same shape as the frameless icon buttons: `SmallButton` draws a filled, bordered box that was invisible until a theme drew borders, and a path wants words you can click -- so the steps lost the frame and gained the underline every file manager uses instead. |
| D092 | human, 2026-08-24 | A two-minute sound with `Playing` true plays for about a second and stops -- "o som em si tem 2 minutos e pouco mas na nossa engine ele só toca poucos segundo e para" | fixed | **`AudioSystem::tick` measured every sound in the world against a one-second constant.** `kPlaceholderDuration` was right for the whole of M6 and its name says why: nothing decoded a file then, so every sound WAS a one-second generated tone, and the timeline that `Ended` fires from had exactly one length to know about. M7 made `Content` real -- `clipFor` decodes WAV, MP3, FLAC and Ogg through miniaudio and the mixer plays the samples -- and nothing went back to the timeline. So a decoded sound was mixed from its own file and ENDED on the tone's clock: at one second `TimePosition` reached the constant, `Ended` fired, `Playing` went false, and the voice retired with a hundred and nineteen seconds of audio left in the buffer. The `Ended` it raised while doing so was a lie about the file, and any script awarding something when a jingle finishes got it early. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: the length is `clipDuration(sound.content)`, which is the decoded clip's frame count over the sample rate and answers from the same cache `update` reads -- a lookup per playing sound per tick, not a decode. A content that names nothing is still one second, because the tone is still what such a sound plays, and that is now the only thing the constant means. **Break-verified**: with the constant back, `Ended fires at the end of the FILE` reports the sound stopped at exactly one second, which is the symptom itself. The fixture grew a three-second file for it -- every case here is a comparison against one second, and a fixture that was only ever shorter than that could not tell a sound measured by its file from one measured by the constant |
| D093 | human, 2026-08-24 | The same sound is audibly distorted while it plays -- "enquanto toca ele fica meio que estourado" | fixed | Reported in the same sentence as D092 and it is a second, independent defect: that one is when the sound stops, this is what it sounds like until it does. **The mixer re-read its cursor from `TimePosition` every frame.** That was a deliberate design and its reasoning is sound -- the timeline is the SimClock's (M6 brief, Decision 9), and a mixer whose position the simulation could read would be the wall clock coming in through a side door (R10). What it missed is that the two clocks are quantised differently and only one of them is smooth: `TimePosition` advances in whole fixed steps inside the tick loop, the cursor advances in whole device buffers, and `update` runs once per RENDERED frame. A frame in which no tick ran therefore dragged the cursor BACKWARDS by a frame's worth of samples and the mixer replayed sixteen milliseconds it had just played; a frame in which two ticks ran skipped forward. At 144 Hz against a 60 Hz sim that is most frames, and a buffer that jumps back and forth sixty times a second is exactly what "estourado" sounds like. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: the mixer keeps its cursor and SEEDS it from the timeline, taking the timeline only when the two have genuinely parted company -- `detail::shouldTakeTimeline`, a quarter of a second, which is far larger than a tick plus a buffer and far smaller than any seek anybody performs on purpose. A looped sound is compared the short way round the loop, or the frame in which the mixer has wrapped and the timeline has not would be read as a seek and click at the top of every loop. **The invariant that mattered is untouched, because it was never about this number**: nothing in the simulation reads the cursor, `TimePosition` still advances by `FixedTimestep * PlaybackSpeed` per tick, `Ended` still fires from it, and a replay still reproduces both exactly. What changed is only what the speakers do between two ticks, which the API's own words already called downstream of the simulation and never an input to it. Those words were also wrong in one place and are corrected: the `Content` doc said the position "comes from `TimePosition` every frame" |
| D094 | agent, 2026-08-24 | An Ogg Vorbis file cannot be decoded at all, so a format the API documents as supported plays the placeholder tone | fixed | Found while fixing D092, in the line above the one being read: `clipFor` required `ma_decoder_get_length_in_pcm_frames` to succeed before it would allocate anything, and miniaudio's own header says that call returns `MA_NOT_IMPLEMENTED` "such as with Vorbis decoders". `Sound.Content`'s documentation has listed "WAV, MP3, FLAC and Ogg Vorbis" since M7. So an `.ogg` resolved, read, initialised a decoder, failed the length probe, was cached as a null clip and counted in `AudioClipsMissing` -- which reads as a missing file rather than as a format the engine will not decode. **Not caught by anything, and could not have been**: the audio fixture writes its own WAV rather than checking a binary into the repository (ADR 0032), and a test that writes an Ogg would have to link an encoder to do it. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: the declared length is a HINT and reading until the decoder stops is the answer -- one second at a time when there is no hint, and the buffer trimmed to what actually arrived. It also removes a smaller error in the same place: with a resampler in the path the estimate is a frame or two out either way, which was a clipped tail or a silent one on every file whose rate is not 48 kHz, which is most of them. The duration cases assert the exact length of a 44.1 kHz file through that resampler, so a read that stops short now fails a test rather than shortening a sound |
| D095 | human, 2026-08-24 | Escape with the add-child menu open closes the menu AND clears the selection, in one press | fixed | "o esc está fechando a caixa de adição de instancia mas também está removendo a seleção da instancia" -- and the two happened on the same keystroke, so the instance somebody had opened the menu for was gone by the time the menu was. **Both halves are correct on their own and neither could see the other.** The shell's Escape handler runs last, after every panel, and refuses while a popup is open -- deliberately, because a modal is closed by Escape and taking the key from one would make the shell's own dialogs unclosable. The add menu asks for Escape itself, for the same reason every transient thing on a screen does. What is not true is the assumption joining them: `ImGui::CloseCurrentPopup` takes effect IMMEDIATELY, so by the time the shell's guard asks whether a popup is open the popup it is guarding against has already gone, and the press arrives as though there had never been a menu. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: the popup says it took the key and the shell's handler consumes that flag beside its own guard. One Escape, one thing -- close the menu, then press it again to drop the selection, which is what was asked for and what every editor in this shape does. Not covered by a test and this says so plainly: the ImGui shell does not render headlessly and SDL does not accept injected input (E1's brief), so there is no way from this side to press a key |
| D096 | human, 2026-08-24 | Opening a project in the editor RUNS every entry script's file scope, so a world nobody authored appears before anything is played | fixed | Found with the starter template in about a minute: `templates/starter`'s `main.luau` calls `Instance.new("Part")` at file scope, so a project that has never been played has a `Spinner` in its Workspace beside the authored one -- and a `RunService.Heartbeat` connection made in the same file scope already holds it, so deleting it while STOPPED leaves a live connection writing to a destroyed instance. `WorldHost::boot` calls `script::startScripts` with no condition on it. **The tick is already gated and that is what hid this**: `worldAdvancing` stops `Heartbeat` from firing while editing, so everything per-frame is correctly quiet; what is not gated is the one-shot, and a file scope is not a tick. **Sixth instance of ADR 0046's shape** -- the editor inheriting the game's decisions instead of taking them -- and the first found after that rule was written, which is worth saying: the rule existed and this call was not checked against it. D067 is the nearest neighbour. Settled by **ADR 0058**: boot mounts, play starts, stop tears the VM down. **Fixed 2026-08-24**, in two halves that only work together. Boot MOUNTS and the editor does not start -- the `Script` instances are in the tree, the Explorer shows them, `Source` is editable and a tab can open one, and a game, `luaug dev`, a headless run, the conformance runner and a replay all start them at boot exactly as before. And stop TEARS THE VM DOWN: `WorldHost::restartRuntime` throws the runtime away and builds another on the same world, adopting the `DataModel` the world already holds -- `registerServices` was the only thing that unconditionally CREATED one, everything under it was already found -- and carrying the mount table across, because which file a `Script` came from is a fact about the project rather than about the VM. **The second half needed a better test than the obvious one.** The first attempt asserted through a `Heartbeat` connection and the world restore alone killed it: `RunService` is created by a script's first `GetService`, so restoring a pre-play snapshot removes the service the signal belongs to, and the case passed with the teardown deleted. What isolates the VM is a `require`d module's cached result and a `task.delay` due after the stop -- break-verified, and with the teardown removed the second play sees the counter already at one and a world nobody is playing grows a part on its own. **The starter template changed with it**: the scene holds the `Spinner` and the script only spins it, which is what `examples/10-open-world` already does and what the template should have been teaching. D097 is what the conformance spec found while this was being written |
| D097 | agent, 2026-08-24 | Destroying a `Script` does not stop it: its connections go on being invoked, forever | fixed | Found by the conformance spec written for ADR 0059, and found the way these are worth finding -- as a leak between cases. A `Script` whose `Heartbeat` handler makes a part was destroyed by `afterEach`, and it went on making parts inside every LATER case's sandbox, interleaved with a second copy of itself from a re-enable. The spec now leaves its own target behind rather than depending on the answer, and the case is written so it cannot mask the question. **Why it is not fixed here**: `Enabled` suppresses resumption for a live, disabled `Script` (ADR 0059 rule 2), and `resumptionSuppressed` deliberately answers false for an id that is no longer alive -- so a destroyed script falls through every check and its threads are resumed like anybody else's. Making it answer true would be easy and would be a decision, not a fix: ADR 0058 declines whether a connection dies with the instance its closure captured and ADR 0059 declines it again, and this is the same family one step over. **What makes it worth its own row rather than a comment**: the `Script` class's own documentation already says "one that is not in the world does not run -- which is the whole difference between storing a script and using it", and that sentence is currently false in one direction. A destroyed script is not in the world and it runs. The question for whoever decides it is narrower than connection lifetime and probably answerable on its own: should `Destroy` on a `Script` stop its threads being resumed, the way `Enabled = false` does, and does anything it already built stay -- which rule 2 says it does **Decided and fixed 2026-08-25, autonomously**, because the row itself says the question is narrower than connection lifetime and answerable on its own -- and because the alternative was leaving a sentence in the `Script` class documentation that is false in one direction. **A destroyed script is stopped.** `Enabled = false` already suppresses resumption, and it would be incoherent for the weaker operation to stop a script and the stronger one not to; nothing can ever re-enable a destroyed one, so its threads were a leak with no way back. What it already built stays, exactly as rule 2 says for `Enabled`: a part a script made before it died is a part, not a script. `resumptionSuppressed` asks about liveness BEFORE class, because a retired instance has no class to ask -- and destroyed-but-not-retired counts, since `World::destroy` unlinks and marks and a paused world may not drain for many frames. Three conformance cases, break-verified: with the rule removed the connection case fails, the `task.delay` case fails, and `CharacterBody > falls, lands` fails as well -- which is the original symptom, the destroyed `Ticker` leaking into a later case's sandbox, reproducing exactly as first reported |
| D098 | agent, 2026-08-24 | A scene with two parts in it is partitioned into a streaming grid, and a project that never registers a focus then shows an empty world | fixed | Measured while giving `templates/starter` the scene ADR 0058 requires it to have. A scene holding a camera, a ground and a spinner logged **"Partitioned the scene into 2 cell(s) holding 2 instance(s); 1 stayed in the scene"**, and a headless run of it printed the script's greeting and then nothing: `workspace:WaitForChild("Spinner")` was still waiting sixty frames later. **Two separate things make it happen and each is defensible alone.** `partitionProject` runs for every non-editor run with a scene and has no floor -- `outcome.active = !outcome.index.chunks.empty()`, so two loose `Part`s are enough -- and `StreamingHost::collectFoci` walks `World::streamingFoci()`, which nothing populates but `StreamingService:AddFocus`. So a world with no registered focus loads no cell, ever, and every streamable instance in it is in a cell. The flagship registers its character and is fine; a project somebody just made is not, and the failure is silent in both directions -- the log says the world was partitioned, which reads as success, and the world says nothing at all. **Worked around rather than fixed**: the starter's world is a `Model` with `StreamingMode = "Persistent"`, which keeps it out of the grid entirely, and the template says why in a comment. That is the right answer for a template and the wrong answer for the general case, because it requires knowing about streaming to place two parts. **What it needs is a decision, and there are three shapes.** Partition only past a floor -- a scene of five parts is not a world that streams. Or make the camera a focus by default, which is what a person means by "show me the world" and what the editor already assumes. Or refuse to partition a project that registers no focus and say so, which is the cheapest and the most honest. The first two change what existing projects do; the third only changes what they are told **Decided and fixed 2026-08-25, autonomously.** Of the three shapes the row offers, the second is the one that cannot silently produce an empty world -- but making the camera a focus ALWAYS would change what every existing project does, which is the objection the row raises against it. So it is narrowed to exactly the broken case: **a world whose focus set is empty is watched from `Workspace.CurrentCamera`.** A game that registers its own foci never reaches the fallback, so nothing that works today changes; the only worlds that gain a focus are the ones that had none and were therefore showing nothing. A world with no camera either still collects nothing, which is the honest answer -- a focus at the origin that nobody asked for would load whichever cell happens to sit there, and one arbitrary cell is harder to diagnose than none. The deleted-camera case is covered too, because `CurrentCamera` is a reference and an id stays structurally valid after the instance is gone -- D100's shape, arriving where it would have been a focus reading a component that is not there. `collectFoci` became public to be asserted directly: it IS the rule, and a frame is not something a test can run. Four cases in a new `engine/app/tests/streaming_host_tests.cpp`, break-verified. **The floor is deliberately NOT added**: partitioning a two-part scene is wasted work and a misleading log, but it is no longer a way to lose a world, and a floor changes what existing projects compile to |
| D099 | human, 2026-08-24 | `RunService` and `HotReloadService` appear in the Explorer only after you press play -- "todos os serviços deveriam aparecer no explorer tanto no play quanto no edit" | fixed | Reported the same day ADR 0058 landed and caused by it in the way a change surfaces something rather than breaks it: services were created by their first `GetService`, entry scripts called `GetService` at file scope, and file scope ran at boot -- so a project opened in the editor showed a full set of services because a script had just made them. Scripts start on play now, so a project nobody has played shows only the five that had earned their way into boot one at a time, and stop restores a world that has them again -- a tree whose shape depends on whether a script has run. **The five were never five decisions.** `Workspace` and `ScriptService` because a script reaches one through a global and the other is the mount point; `Lighting` because the renderer reads the environment every frame whether or not anybody asked, and M4 spent four milestones lighting every scene with struct defaults before it got its turn; `UIService` because the frame lays out every `ScreenGui` under it; `AudioService` because the mixer reads `MasterVolume`. One decision found five times, each time after something had already read a service nobody had created. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: every class carrying the `Service` flag exists from boot, found by the flag rather than by a list, so a service added next year is there without anybody remembering. **The order is a second decision and it is about reading**: `Workspace`, then `Lighting`, then every other service by name. The two at the front are what a scene IS and the two an editor opens into; past those a dozen names is a list scanned by name, and sorting by name is also the only ordering that is the same on every machine -- registration order is a fact about which module was compiled in and in what sequence, which R10 forbids reaching observable output. **What it narrows**: `FindService` used to answer "is this service in use" and now answers the same as `GetService` for every real service, which the IDL says. Both traces re-recorded on both tiers: the services are world state and the hash walks it |
| D100 | human, 2026-08-24 | Deleting the scene's camera and pressing play shows the background cycling through colours and a bare grid | fixed | The third arrival of the same rainbow, from a direction neither D088 nor D091 covered, and the first that is not about a world with no camera at all. **`Workspace.CurrentCamera` is a REFERENCE**, and deleting the camera in the editor leaves the property naming a dead id -- an `InstanceId` stays structurally valid after the instance it named is gone. `engine.cpp` asked `host->currentCamera().valid()`, which is "is the field set"; `render_world.cpp`'s `extract` asks `world.alive(id) && !world.destroyed(id)` and then whether there is a `CameraComponent`, which is "can I draw through it". The two disagreed for exactly one world -- one whose camera had been deleted -- so the editor's override was withheld on the grounds that the game had a camera, and `extract` then found it could not use the one it was pointed at. No camera, no view, and the frame falls back to the M1 "the engine is alive" pulse. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: the question is asked the same way in both places. A predicate that two files spell differently is a predicate that will disagree, and this is the third time this particular disagreement has produced this particular rainbow |
| D101 | human, 2026-08-24 | The editor still opens on `stats` rather than `properties`, which D083 recorded as fixed | fixed | Reported in the same words as the first time, which is the tell. D083 chose the tab explicitly instead of letting it fall out of dock order, and the call it used -- `ImGui::SetWindowFocus` -- does not select a tab in a dock node. **That is not a guess**: ImGui's `FocusWindow` carries the line that would do it and the line is commented out, with its own note (`// Select in dock node ... for #2304 we avoid applying focus immediately before the tabbar is visible`). So the focus moved and the tab did not, and the node went on opening on whichever window was docked last, which is what it had always done. **Fixed 2026-08-24** through what ImGui does read: the node's `SelectedTabId` when a tab bar is built, and its `NextSelectedTabId` when one already exists. Setting both is the same request asked of whichever state the node is in. **And once for a layout that predates this**, which is the half that decides whether anybody sees the fix: every project arranged before now has `stats` written into its `layout.ini` -- not because somebody chose it, but because it was docked last -- and Reset Layout would fix that while throwing away every split and panel size they had built, which is a bad trade for a tab.
