So `.luaug/editor.json` carries a `layoutRevision`, the new default is applied once against a layout that predates it, and a person who chooses `stats` afterwards keeps it. **The general shape**: a fix verified by reading the call site and not by watching the pixel is a fix that can be recorded as done while doing nothing |
| D102 | human, 2026-08-24 | The audition button on a `Content` row is bigger than the field beside it, so that row is taller than every other row | fixed | Reported as "o tamanho das box checkbox play etc.. tao meio zuado" against a `Sound`'s properties, with a screenshot showing the green triangle pushing past the column. Mine, from the day before: `iconButton` sizes the PICTURE and the button then adds `FramePadding` around it, so asking for `GetFrameHeight()` worth of icon produces a button taller and wider than a frame -- the one thing every other widget on a property row is. **Fixed 2026-08-24**: the glyph is what is left of a frame after its padding, which is the font size, and the width the row reserves is what the button will actually measure. One row, one height. The checkboxes reported alongside it are `GetFrameHeight()` squares already and are left alone: a checkbox is small because a checkbox is small, and stretching one to the width of a text field would be a control that looks like it takes a value |
| D103 | human, 2026-08-25 | Remove does nothing on a launcher row whose folder was deleted: the click reports "is not there any more" instead | fixed | Reported with a screenshot of six orange rows and the message the ROW produces sitting under them, which is the tell -- the button's click was landing on the row underneath it. **Two independent halves, and either alone would have looked like the same nothing.** The button was submitted after a `Selectable` covering the whole row and without `SetNextItemAllowOverlap`, so ImGui gave every pixel to the selectable: Remove was drawn, never hovered, and the press went to the row, which for a missing project says exactly what the reporter saw. It was also emitted only while the row was hovered, which is the Explorer's plus-sign defect from the other side -- an item that comes into being on the frame a row becomes hovered is an item whose first press the thing underneath has already taken. **And the list was only ever written when a project was OPENED**, so even a working button would have removed the row until the launcher closed and every one of them came back. **Fixed 2026-08-25**: the overlap is allowed, the button is emitted on every row every frame and drawn at zero alpha when the pointer is elsewhere -- alpha is a drawing property and not a hit-testing one, and the rows where it is invisible are the rows nobody can press it on -- and the hover is asked of the row's RECTANGLE rather than of the selectable, because with the overlap allowed the selectable stops reporting hover the moment the pointer reaches the button, and a button that vanishes when you point at it cannot be clicked by anybody. The save moved into `applyProjectDecisions`, drained by the loop like every other decision this shell takes. **That function exists because of D101's own lesson**, which was written the day before: the loop needs a window, a GPU and somebody clicking, so "the loop calls save" was a claim nobody could check -- break-verified now, and with the drain returning false the row is back in the file. The click half is not covered and this says so plainly: the shell does not render headlessly and SDL does not accept injected input (E1's brief). A message says what happened, including that the folder was not touched |
| D104 | human, 2026-08-25 | Ctrl+S in the script editor flashes the whole screen, and before that lost the panels, the tabs, the caret, the Explorer's expansion and the selection | fixed | **Six reports, six patches, and one wrong decision underneath all of them.** Ctrl+S wrote the file and then rebuilt the world, so that a running VM would pick the change up. Each symptom was fixed as it arrived: the panels were put back (`overlayVisible` is world state and a fresh `EngineState` says false), the tabs were re-opened by chunk name, the caret and scroll were carried across, the Explorer's expansion was preserved, the selection was re-applied. **The patches were the tell.** A fix that has to put six unrelated things back is not a fix, it is a receipt for the wrong operation -- and the sixth report, a full-screen flash, is what a world rebuild looks like when there is nothing left to lose. Saving a text file must not destroy a world, and it never needed to: the pane writes `Source` on the instance as somebody types, so the world already holds the new text, and ADR 0058 makes PLAY what compiles `Source`. In the editor there is no running chunk to refresh. **Fixed 2026-08-25**: Ctrl+S is a `writeTextFile` and a `markSaved`, and five of the six patches were deleted with the reload that made them necessary. The sixth stays, because it was real on its own -- the dev server has had it since M3 and nobody noticed, since `luaug dev` starts with the overlay hidden anyway. Breakpoints moved to the play/stop cycle, which is what owns the VM they are patched into. Verified by driving the real window twice: the editor after Ctrl+S is pixel-identical to the editor before it |
| D105 | human, 2026-08-25 | Autocomplete in the script editor never offers anything from the project: `Workspace.` lists class members and no instances | fixed | Reported together with four others found by using the editor, and this is the one that matters. **Completion knew every class the engine ships and nothing at all about the project open in front of it**, so the one name somebody types most -- something in their own Explorer -- was the one name it could not produce. That is not a limit of the design (ADR 0057 chose the engine's reflection over `Luau.Analysis` for compile-time reasons that still hold); it is a source that was never read. The tree is a pointer away. **Fixed 2026-08-25**: a dotted path is walked instance by instance from the DataModel and offers that instance's children beside its class's members, a name inside `WaitForChild("` or `FindFirstChild("` is completed from the same walk, and `GetService("` from the service flag. There is **no list of service names in the completion code at all** -- a service is a child of the root -- so one registered by a module this build has never heard of resolves for free. One row per name, because six parts called `Ground` insert the same six characters; and a string that is nobody's argument now offers nothing, where before it popped every keyword in the language over whatever somebody was writing. The four found beside it: the completion popup was **measured in Inter and drawn in Cousine**, so its text spilled out of its own highlight -- every ASCII string hides that by a different amount, and the rest of the pane multiplies cells by one advance for exactly this reason; a pane holding ImGui's active id made **every other item in the frame unhoverable** (`ItemHoverable`, `imgui.cpp`), so the first click on the Explorer, the Viewport, the Properties grid or another tab did nothing and only the second landed -- released at the top of the frame, because by the time the pane draws, whichever panel came earlier has already answered that it was not clicked; the Properties grid **drew every line of `Source`**, making the panel as tall as the file, fixed with a `Code` flag in the IDL rather than by testing whether the string has a newline in it, because which properties are code is a fact about the CLASS -- the argument `contentKind` had already made in its own comment; and the central node's tabs now carry their class icons, so a `ModuleScript` tab and a `Script` tab are told apart at a glance |
| D106 | human, 2026-08-25 | Double-clicking in the middle of a word in the script editor selects only the half before the pointer | fixed | Reported as "double-clicking in the middle of `require` selects up to the `u`", which names the cause precisely: the selection ends where the pointer is. `wordAt` was right and the frame after it was wrong -- **`IsMouseDragging` with a zero threshold answers true for as long as the button is held**, so the double-click set the word and the next frame's drag put the caret back under the pointer, leaving whatever was left of it. The zero threshold is deliberate and stays: a selection drag that has to travel five pixels before it starts is a selection drag people fight. **Fixed 2026-08-25**: a drag knows what it is extending BY, and a word-wise drag grows a word at a time in either direction -- which is what every editor does and what makes the gesture worth having rather than merely not-broken |
| D107 | human, 2026-08-25 | `game:GetService("AudioService").` offers nothing: a path through a call is not walked | fixed | The first line of most Luau files ever written, and the completion walk stopped dead at the closing bracket -- so a file that assigns its services at the top completed nothing from the line after it. Two halves. **A call that NAMES something is a step in a path**: `GetService("X")`, `WaitForChild("X")` and `FindFirstChild("X")` each answer the instance their own argument names, so `X` is the step. Only a bare string literal counts -- `game:GetService(name)` is an expression, and reading one would be the type inference ADR 0057 says this does not do, so it stops rather than guesses. **And a step may be a local somebody assigned earlier**, read the way a person scrolling up would read it: one shape, `local NAME = <path>`, spliced in front of the path being resolved, three hops deep. Nothing is evaluated and no function is followed. **A third thing fell out of it**: a dot with nothing readable behind it used to fall through to the keyword list, so `t:GetChildren().` offered every reserved word in the language -- it now offers nothing, which is a state of its own |
| D108 | human, 2026-08-25 | The script editor has no zoom, and no way to move a line | fixed | Two gestures a code pane is expected to have, asked for together. **Ctrl and the wheel** size the text, read from the HOVER rather than the focus because a wheel belongs to whatever is under the pointer -- and the percentage appears in the pane's own corner with `Ctrl+0` beside it for as long as it has just changed, because **a zoom with no readout is a state somebody can get into and not out of**. **Alt+Up and Alt+Down** move the lines the selection covers, and the selection travels with them, which is what makes pressing the chord four times mean anything. The text half lives in `ScriptDocument::moveLines` rather than in the panel -- it is an edit and not a drawing, which is the split that file exists for and the reason it has tests -- and it is ONE `replace` over both blocks rather than a delete and an insert, so Ctrl+Z takes the whole move back. A move somebody has to undo twice is a move that will eat the line underneath it the first time nobody is watching. **One found while testing it**: the join asked `rebuilt.empty()` whether to write a separator, which cannot tell "nothing yet" from "a blank line" -- so moving a line into the empty last line of a file swallowed the final newline, and every file ends in one |
| D109 | human, 2026-08-25 | With a script tab focused, the first click on the Explorer, the Viewport, a property or another tab does nothing: everything takes two clicks | fixed | **Reported twice, because the first fix was a reading of ImGui rather than a measurement of it.** The code pane holds ImGui's active id for as long as somebody is typing -- that is what a caret is, and what makes the shell's `!IsAnyItemActive()` guards keep its shortcuts off the letters being typed. `ItemHoverable` refuses to hover any item while another is active (`imgui.cpp`), so nothing else in the frame could be clicked. The first fix released the active id at the top of the frame, before any panel is submitted, which is necessary and **was not sufficient** -- and the picture that was supposed to prove it was misread: the row it showed was HOVER-highlighted, not selected, and the old selection was still sitting two rows down in the same shot. **The second half was found by instrumenting the real window** and printing what ImGui thought on the click frame: the row was hovered, the active id was zero, the mouse key had no owner -- and no press happened anyway. The reason is one line in `ItemHoverable`: an item carrying `ImGuiItemFlags_AllowOverlap`, which every Explorer row does, is hoverable only if it was ALREADY the hovered id on the PREVIOUS frame. While the caret lived in the pane no row could hover, so on the frame the block was lifted no row had the history it needed either. **The cost was never one frame, it was one click.** **Fixed 2026-08-25** by declaring what is actually true -- `ActiveIdAllowOverlap`, "this widget is active and another may take that from it" -- so hover goes on being resolved underneath the caret and the row is already hovered when the press arrives. The top-of-frame release stays: it is what hands the caret back when a click lands somewhere no item takes it. **The general shape**: a fix whose only evidence is that the source reads correctly is D101's lesson arriving a second time, and the screenshot that seemed to confirm it is worse than no screenshot |
| D110 | human, 2026-08-25 | Autocomplete knows nothing about Luau itself: `typeof`, `math.floor` and `string.format` are not offered | fixed | The completion had the engine's reflection and the project's tree and not one thing from the language it is an editor for -- so `math.` offered nothing and `typeof` was a word you had to remember. **Fixed 2026-08-25** with a list in `engine/script/include/luaug/script/stdlib.h`: Luau's free globals and its ten library tables, every name carrying what `type()` answers for it. **Beside the sandbox rather than beside the editor**, because what a script can name is a fact about the VM -- the script module owns the sandbox, a test there can boot one, and `engine/app` already depends on it. **And written down, which needs an argument**: `api/defs/libraries.api.luau` refuses to declare Luau's libraries because "re-declaring them would make this file a second source of truth for something the pin already fixes", and that is right -- the difference is that the editor has to OFFER these names and cannot boot a VM to answer a keystroke. So the drift is caught instead of prevented: `sandbox_tests.cpp` stands a real sandboxed VM up and checks the list **in both directions**, so a Luau bump that gains a function fails a test rather than quietly leaving the editor a version behind. **It caught two mistakes on the first run**, which is the argument in miniature: the list was transcribed from `third_party/luau/VM/src/l*lib.cpp` rather than remembered, and that missed `math.e`, `math.nan`, `math.phi`, `math.sqrt2` and `math.tau` -- set with `lua_setfield` rather than listed in the registration table -- while offering `buffer.readinteger` and `buffer.writeinteger`, which are in that file inside a block this pin does not build. **The surface is this engine's, not stock Luau's**: `os` carries three names because `removeUnsafeGlobals` takes `difftime` off, and `getfenv`, `setfenv`, `newproxy` and `loadstring` are not offered because they are not there |
| D111 | human, 2026-08-25 | A ModuleScript nested inside another module cannot reach its own children: `require` of it errors | fixed | Reported as "I cannot require a ModuleScript that is a child of a Script", and reproducing it split the report in two. **Dot-access to a child is not the defect**: `script.Nested` is divergence #26 in api-design.md, decided deliberately -- an `__index` fallback to children is untypeable under a 100%-strict surface (ADR 0018) and is the habit that made `WaitForChild` load-bearing -- and the supported `script:FindFirstChild("Nested")` worked for a module under a Script and for one inside a Folder. **What was really broken is a module inside a module.** `evaluateModule` runs a module on a fresh sandboxed coroutine, and `luaL_sandboxthread` gives that thread a globals table whose `__index` is the creating thread's -- so a module inherited the `script` of whatever REQUIRED it. A nested module asking for its own children asked the outer Script's and found none, which from the outside reads exactly like "this module cannot be required". **Fixed 2026-08-25**: `script` inside a module is the module's own instance, and nil rather than inherited when a module has no instance at all (`@luaug/…` is not a script). Set BEFORE the load, which is not a preference -- `luaL_sandboxthread` marks the globals safeenv, so the compiler resolves globals at load time and anything assigned afterwards is invisible to every `script.X` in the chunk; the same ordering `startScripts` already documents. A file required by path gets the instance the mount made for it, so the same module body means the same thing whichever way it was reached. Three conformance cases cover the three nestings a person actually builds: under a Script, under a Folder, and under another module |
| D112 | human, 2026-08-25 | Deleting a script in the Explorer leaves its editor tab open | fixed | The tab went on drawing a document that could no longer be saved anywhere -- there is no instance to write `Source` to and no file behind a scene script either. **`ScriptEditor::forgetDestroyed` had existed and been tested since the editor was built and nothing ever called it**, which is the shape D087 recorded: code that is right and unreachable fails the moment something finally reaches for it. **Fixed 2026-08-25** by asking it once a frame at the safe point, after every command that can destroy an instance -- rather than wiring it to the delete, because an instance also goes when a scene is loaded, when a stamp session opens, and when an undo takes a create back. One question in one place answers all of them; a list of the ways would have missed the next one somebody adds. The close is said out loud in the status line, because a tab vanishing is something that happened TO a person and the text went with the instance it belonged to |
| D113 | human, 2026-08-25 | A script created inside an open stamp shows the wrong name in its tab and sometimes does not save | fixed | Both symptoms, and a third nobody had hit yet, are one cause: **an `InstanceId` is a handle into ONE world's slotmap and this editor has two**. A stamp is edited in a world of its own (ADR 0049), and the two allocate independently -- the first instance in each has the same handle. `openScript` read `host->world()` unconditionally, so a row double-clicked in the stamp's Explorer was resolved against the scene's world and answered about whatever instance happened to share the slot: a name from the wrong instance, a `Source` from the wrong instance, and -- the third one -- **every keystroke written into that wrong instance**, silently, in the project's own scene. Ctrl+S then took the `tab->file.empty()` branch and saved the SCENE, which is why the stamp "sometimes did not save": it never did, and the scene got the write. **Fixed 2026-08-25** by making a tab record which world it came from -- `ScriptOrigin`, an enum and not a `World*`, because a stage's world is destroyed when the stamp closes and a stale pointer compared against a fresh allocation that reused the address is a worse version of the same bug. Opening reads `authored()`, typing writes into the tab's own world, Ctrl+S on a stamp's script saves the stamp, `indexOf` is keyed on the pair so opening a stamp's script does not focus a scene tab that shares the id, and a stamp tab closes when its session does -- there is nowhere left to edit it. A stamp script's chunk name is its place in the stamp's tree, which needed `script::treePathOf` to take a world rather than a `lua_State`: it is a fact about a world, and the stamp's has never been seen by a VM. **The case is now a test rather than a reading**: two fixture worlds are asserted to hand out equal ids, and the tabs are kept apart anyway |
| D114 | human, 2026-08-25 | The script editor is not intelligent enough: it never says a name was used without being declared, nor that one was declared and never used | fixed | Asked for with Roblox Studio as the bar, and with the standard the request itself set: **responsive, and not in the developer's way.** **Fixed 2026-08-25** with two lints over the AST the parser already builds for the syntax errors -- `Luau.Analysis` carries a linter and is deliberately not built (35% of a cold build; ADR 0018, ADR 0057), and neither of these needs it. The PARSER already resolves scope, which is the hard half: a name it could bind to a local comes out as `AstExprLocal` and one it could not comes out as `AstExprGlobal`. **An unknown global here is a fact rather than a style note**, which is what makes it worth drawing: `sealGlobals` freezes the globals table (R4), so a name outside the sandbox's surface will be nil at runtime -- and the set it is checked against is the same one the completion offers, so it can never underline a name the editor itself just suggested. **What it deliberately does not report** is the whole of "not in the way": a file that does not parse is not linted at all, because a half-typed one would put a warning under every name somebody is mid-word on; a function's parameters and a loop variable are never reported, because both are unused constantly and on purpose; and a leading underscore is honoured, because that is how a person says "I know". A warning is drawn in the warning colour under the NAME rather than in red to the end of the line -- an error means this will not compile and a warning means it will, and drawing them the same is how a panel teaches somebody to ignore both. Costs nothing per keystroke: it runs in the parse that already happened one frame after the text came to rest |
| D115 | human, 2026-08-25 | A material being edited draws with none of its maps: the preview sphere ignores a diffuse, a normal and a roughness map all set on it | fixed | Reported as "the stamp preview is not showing the texture", and it was two walls one behind the other. **The first is this row.** The frame loop ran `syncPrimitives`, `syncTextures` and `sync` over `host->world()` and nothing else, while `extract` has known about the stamp stage since a stamp began opening onto a world of its own -- so a `Material` that existed only on the stage had no texture loaded, and every `MeshPart` on a stage had no mesh uploaded either. **Fixed 2026-08-25** by loading for every world that can be drawn rather than for the game's: both, not whichever is on screen, because the libraries are keyed by content and shared, and keeping the game's meshes resident is what makes closing a stamp instant instead of a frame of missing geometry. The second wall is D116, and fixing this one alone changed nothing anybody could see -- which is worth recording, because it is exactly the case where a correct fix reads as a wrong diagnosis |
| D116 | agent, 2026-08-25 | Every `Material` instance in the engine ignores every map it is given: the texture is bound, sampled, and multiplied by zero | fixed | Found while looking for why D115's fix changed nothing. The shader never branches on whether a map is present -- every slot always has a texture, a 1x1 stand-in when the material has none -- so `textureFlags` is what decides whether a sample counts: it multiplies the difference between the map and the factor. `materialOf` assigned the four handles and left the four flags at their zero default. `fillEntry`, forty lines away in the same module, wrote both, which is why a mesh out of a glTF has always looked right and made the difference impossible to see from the symptom: a surface drawing its factor alone is indistinguishable from a surface whose map was never set. **Fixed 2026-08-25** by making the maps and the flags one verb, `RenderMaterial::setMaps`, used by both paths -- stating a fact twice is what let one copy go stale, and no amount of remembering to write the second line is a fix. **Why it shipped**: every material case in `render_world_tests.cpp` checks a NUMBER, and a number arrives through the uniforms whether or not the flags say anything. Nothing had ever handed a `Material` a texture. Two cases now do, break-verified: deleting the four flag writes fails both and nothing else in 49,369 assertions notices |
| D117 | agent, 2026-08-25 | Dragging a panel edge in the editor stalls the whole GPU, once per frame, for as long as the drag lasts | fixed | Found while auditing for what makes the editor feel heavy, and the code said so itself: `ViewportTarget::resize` called `device.waitIdle()` before freeing the texture it was replacing, and its own comment noted that "the stall is real and it is paid while somebody drags a splitter, which is the one moment nobody is measuring frame time". It is also the one moment somebody is looking hardest at how the tool feels. **Fixed 2026-08-25** with the retirement queue `MeshCache::beginFrame` already uses, down to the two frames of slack: a replaced target is put aside and freed once no command list still in flight can name it. `destroy()` keeps the wait, because shutting down is not a frame and there is no next one to drain the queue. Asserted through the CAPTURE device, which records every call -- the null device forgets and a real one cannot be interrogated -- so "was this freed yet" is a question a test can actually ask. Break-verified. **What this is not**: it is not the reported "everything reloads when I change a property", which is still unreproduced. Measurement ruled out the two obvious suspects -- an undo snapshot of a 30,000-instance world costs 0.81 ms, and a headless frame of an idle editor costs 0.037 ms -- and the windowed numbers that looked bad were vsync |
| D118 | human, 2026-08-25 | "Anything I do in the engine looks like everything reloads -- I change a property on something and it seems to reload" | fixed | **Measured rather than guessed at, after two other suspects were ruled out the same way.** An undo snapshot of a 30,000-instance world costs 0.81 ms and a headless idle editor frame costs 0.037 ms, so neither the snapshot nor the frame loop was it; the windowed numbers that looked bad were vsync. What it is: `MeshLoader::syncTextures` reads, decodes and uploads every missing map it finds **in one frame, with no budget at all**, on the frame thread. An ordinary 1024-square PNG out of a texture pack costs **14 to 36 ms to decode** -- measured on the reporter's own textures -- so pointing a part at a three-map material froze the frame after the write for a tenth of a second. A 4K source is sixteen times worse. On a 240 Hz display that is eight to seventy dropped frames on exactly the frame somebody changed something, which is how a tool whose frame times all look fine comes to feel like it reloads the world whenever you touch it. **Fixed 2026-08-25** with the three-stage shape `StreamingHost::pump` already uses: `platform::readFileAsync` reads, the job pool decodes and resamples as `Domain::AssetIo`, and only the upload happens on the frame, because only the frame has a command list. Bounded at four in flight, and a queue that cannot be joined falls through to the synchronous read rather than failing -- deferring is a way of doing the work, not a permission to skip it. **Off by default**, and that default is what keeps every golden byte-identical: a capture records the frame it was told to, and a texture arriving two frames later is a different picture. The editor turns it on and says why. **Confirmed end to end by A/B on the reporter's own textures**: the same scene, the same three maps, three runs each, warm -- 278.8 / 278.4 / 276.5 ms on the load frame with the synchronous path against 163.8 / 164.0 / 160.8 ms with the deferred one. **114 ms**, which is the three decodes plus their reads. **The first version of this measurement was wrong and is corrected here rather than quietly restated**: it ran the host without a project path, so the scene never loaded and there was no material to decode -- the 90 ms it reported was a cold file cache, and the run that looked like the fix working was a run in which nothing loaded at all. Caught by probing whether the texture actually ARRIVED instead of trusting a frame time, which is the only reason it is not still in this row as a fact. **What is still open, named so it is findable**: two more callers read and decode on the frame thread. `MeshLoader::sync` parses a 3 MB glTF there -- left synchronous on purpose, because a mesh arriving late is collision arriving late, and a character falling through a floor for three frames is a worse trade than a hitch. And `UiText::loadPendingImages` does it for every `ImageLabel`, where the `ui-*.png` goldens depend on the timing the way the capture goldens do. The content browser's thumbnails were given this same shape in the same session, for the same measurement |
| D119 | agent, 2026-08-25 | Opening the editor freezes for a fifth of a second, and 131 ms of it is decoding the icon atlas one file at a time | fixed | Found by instrumenting the frame that the `--frame-stats` warning had been naming since the first run of this audit: "Frame took 279 ms". The atlas is built on the first frame that has a command list -- an upload is one, so it cannot happen earlier -- and it decoded eighty-eight PNGs and downscaled each to four sizes in a single serial loop. Whatever that costs is a window that has not appeared yet. **Fixed 2026-08-25** by splitting it into three phases: reading stays serial, because the files are a few kilobytes each and the job pool is explicitly not allowed to block on IO; decoding and downscaling go through `jobs::parallelFor` at a grain of one icon; and packing stays serial and in id order, so the atlas is byte-identical however many cores ran it. A layout that depended on scheduling would make two machines disagree about where an icon is. **131 ms to 12 ms**, and the opening frame from 279 ms to 160 ms. The `load` status line now carries the number, because a cost nobody can see is a cost nobody improves -- and reading a wall clock to report elapsed time is the one thing R10 allows it for. **What the REST of that frame is, measured afterwards with a throwaway stopwatch through the loop**: 109 ms of it is the renderer's first `submitWorld`, which is the GPU driver compiling pipeline state the first time it sees each one. That is not engine waste and it does not move by doing it earlier; SDL_GPU exposes no pipeline cache to warm. Recorded so the next person looking at a 160 ms opening frame knows what is left in it and does not go hunting. Cold, the atlas itself reads 528 ms rather than 12 -- the build stages `content/icons` and Windows scans each file on its first read, so the first run after a build is never the number anybody lives with |
| D120 | agent, 2026-08-25 | The physics step silently drops contacts, and the message says to go and look at something the log does not contain | fixed | Found by reading a `luaug.log` left in the repository root by the gate itself: `Jolt reported a broken assumption ... errors == EPhysicsUpdateError::None An error occurred during the physics update, see EPhysicsUpdateError for more information`, four times, each beside a 200 ms frame -- **in a stage that was passing**. `m_system.Update`'s return value was discarded, so the only report was Jolt's own assert, which names no error and does not exist at all in a build with asserts off. Every value of `EPhysicsUpdateError` means the same thing: contacts were dropped, which reads as parts sinking through each other rather than as an error. **Fixed 2026-08-25** in two halves. The return value is read and reported by NAME, once per distinct kind -- a full buffer stays full, and a line a tick turns a log into a wall. And the message says what to DO: the first draft told the author to raise the body budget, which nothing in the engine can set -- `WorldDesc::maxBodies` has one writer and it is its own default -- so it pointed at a knob that does not exist. It now names the cause instead. **What it found the moment it could speak**: `tests/hotreload`'s heavy fixture creates five hundred `Part`s and positions them from a `Heartbeat`, so until the first one ran all five hundred were stacked at the origin -- a hundred and twenty thousand collision pairs against a budget of thirty-two thousand. Its own comment had always claimed "each with a name, a colour and a position" and the position was the one it did not give. Giving it took the gate from four dropped-contact frames of 200 ms each to none, and the reload benchmark it lives inside from 3.6 ms to 2.9 ms -- it had been measuring a physics pathology instead of a reload |
| D121 | agent, 2026-08-25 | Three directory walks throw out of boot or out of a draw call if the project holds a folder this process cannot enter | fixed | Found by sweeping for the shape of the breadcrumb crash (D136, `currentFolder`) rather than for the crash itself: something that takes the window with no message. `std::filesystem::recursive_directory_iterator(root, ec)` in a range-for looks defensive and is not -- **the error-code overload is a CONSTRUCTOR, and `operator++` still throws** -- so a junction, a permission-denied subtree or a directory that disappears mid-walk raises `filesystem_error` from inside `WorldHost`'s script mount, from the conformance spec mount, and from `ContentTree::filesOfKind`, which a property picker calls while drawing. An uncaught throw out of any of those is a process that dies during boot or during a frame, saying nothing about what it was reading. `tools/assetc/src/compiler.cpp` already used the `it.increment(ec)` form and was the model. **Fixed 2026-08-25** in all three. And `filesOfKind` gained the dot-folder skip `collectStamps` already had: it lists what a `Content` property may name, `.luaug` holds the pack and the import cache, and offering a compiled copy of a texture beside the texture is worse than offering neither -- they look the same in a list and only one is the file anybody edits |
| D122 | agent, 2026-08-26 | The split-sum BRDF table is baked serially on the first frame that renders: 33 million importance samples, 85 ms | fixed | Found by a scouting pass over what was left of the editor's opening frame after D119 took the icon atlas out of it. `bakeBrdfLut` runs 512 importance samples per texel over a 256-square table on the first frame the renderer draws -- and it is not editor-specific, it is every shell that has a camera. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by copying the shape the environment prefilter beside it already had: a job struct and a row function through `jobs::parallelFor` at a grain of eight rows. There is no shared accumulator -- each texel is written once from its own `x`, `y` and sample index -- so the partition is a function of the DATA and the output is bit-identical by construction. **That is not a nicety here**: `tests/rendercapture/*.jsonl` record a content hash of these 32 KB and the capture gates carry no `gpu-golden` label, so they run in CI on both tiers -- one ULP of drift reddens four blocking gates. Asserted the only way it can be, by baking once with the pool in its documented SERIAL mode and once with four workers and requiring the bytes to agree, plus a check that it baked something rather than agreeing about zeroes. **85 ms of an editor opening frame**, which with D118 and D119 takes that frame from about 280 ms to 78 |
| D123 | agent, 2026-08-26 | Three live faults in the physics mirror: a body the backend refuses is asked for again every tick for ever, a refused rebuild is never retried, and a record keeps a span it is not allowed to keep | fixed | Found by a scouting pass done as a pre-requisite for deferring mesh loads -- async loading is what makes all three fire routinely, and none of them had ever fired because a mesh used to finish loading inside the frame that created the part. **One:** `applyBody`'s create branch did `record = BodyRecord{}` on a refusal, which zeroes the generation -- so the next tick saw an empty slot and asked again, once per tick, for ever, burning a body generation each time and saying nothing. **Two:** `updateBody` returned void and the record's shape was assigned unconditionally, so a rebuild the backend refused -- a degenerate hull is the real case -- was recorded as done and never retried. **Three:** `ShapeDesc::points` is a `std::span` whose documented lifetime is "the `createBody` call and no longer", and `BodyRecord` kept the whole desc across ticks; the vector behind it is replaced whenever a mesh loads, so the mirror was already holding a dangling span in the current tree. **Fixed 2026-08-26** as one change, because each without the others is worse: `ShapeDesc` gains a `pointsRevision` counted up wherever a point cloud is replaced, `sameShape` compares it and the scale rather than the spans, records store the description with the span CLEARED, a `live` flag says whether the backend actually holds a body -- read by `retireUnseen` and `bodyHandleOf`, both of which would otherwise act on a body that was never made -- and `updateBody` reports refusal so the record can say what it ATTEMPTED. One attempt per description, and only a description that genuinely differs is worth another. Six cases, break-verified one fault at a time -- the first two masked the third until it was isolated, which is the reason to break them separately. The fake backend now copies the hull rather than storing the span, and two existing cases that read `desc.shape.points` after the fact were reading exactly the span this row is about |
| D124 | agent, 2026-08-26 | Every frame any mesh finishes loading, the whole mesh library's vertex positions are copied to the physics mirror | fixed | Found while making mesh loading deferrable. The frame loop pushed collision points by walking EVERY library entry whenever `sync` reported a non-zero count -- a full `std::vector<Vec3>` copy of every loaded mesh, and a free of the previous one, on every frame anything landed. Synchronous loading hid it inside one or two frames; spreading N completions over N frames turns it into N(N+1)/2 copies of everything. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by having `sync` append the content name of each mesh it actually put in the library, and pushing exactly those. A count cannot answer "which", which is the whole reason it walked the library in the first place |
| D125 | agent, 2026-08-26 | A frame loads every mesh it is missing, with no budget: five models dropped into a project is one frame of about a second | fixed | Measured on the model E9 opened for -- 2937 KiB of glTF, 60,688 vertices, 677 joints -- at **21 ms to read and 191 ms to parse**, and `MeshLoader::sync` had no budget at all. It is not an editor problem either: a `MeshPart` a script creates mid-play takes the same path. **Fixed 2026-08-26**: deferred, one mesh lands per call. **A budget of one whole mesh rather than a millisecond count**, because a parse cannot be split -- any budget needs a floor of one, and one already exceeds a frame. What it buys is N frames instead of one frame N times as long, which is the difference between a tool that hitches and a tool that stops. Wired as `!options.headless` rather than `options.editor`, and the texture switch beside it was widened the same way: a shipping game hitches on identical code, `--headless` is what every capture, screenshot and determinism driver runs under so no golden can observe it, and `options.editor && !options.headless` was identically `options.editor` because `main.cpp` refuses the two together. `two_worlds.cpp` owns a second loader and now says synchronous out loud rather than inheriting a default. **What a part collides as while it waits**: a Box of its `Size`, which is deliberate and tested -- with the honest caveat that the box is a superset of the hull only when `MeshSize` was authored correctly and the mesh is centred on its origin, so a rare pop is possible. It never falls through. **What is NOT deferred and why**: the parse itself stays on the frame thread. `jobs.h` rule 1 forbids blocking IO on a worker and `importGltf` reads a glTF's external `.bin` and `.png` itself as part of parsing -- so the pool is not available to it until the importer can be handed buffers somebody else read, which is a change to `asset`. E9's own direction, a loose `.gltf` ceasing to work and everything arriving compiled, retires the caveat rather than fixing it |
| D126 | agent, 2026-08-26 | The skeleton pass reads every PNG beside every model, in the tick, to throw them away -- and rescans a list to decide it has already done so | fixed | `WorldHost::syncSkeletons` runs for every `MeshPart` in the world on every tick and stops before a single texel is looked at, including for the meshes it is about to conclude have no skeleton at all -- but it asked fastgltf for `LoadExternalImages` anyway, so every image beside every model was pulled off disk to be discarded. **Fixed 2026-08-26**: `skeletonOnly` no longer asks for them. The buffers stay, because a skin's joints and its animation samplers are accessor data and accessor data is what a `.bin` holds. Asserted through a file whose image is not there, which is the one way the difference is observable from outside -- a pass that reads it fails and a pass that does not read it does not care -- and break-verified. **And the memo of what it had already tried was a `std::vector` searched linearly**, so the pass cost `meshParts x distinct skeleton-less meshes` per tick, for ever, long after every parse was done: a world of five hundred rigless props paid a quarter of a million comparisons a tick to conclude nothing. A set now, whose iteration order reaches nothing observable. **Deliberately NOT changed**: this site stays synchronous and stays inside the tick. `WorldHost::tick` is called with no render loop from the replay, bench and two-worlds harnesses; animation retires into the world inside the tick and `replay.cpp` hashes it at checkpoints, so hoisting it removes skeletons from every replay and making it async makes the world hash a function of disk speed (R10) |
| D127 | agent, 2026-08-26 | The streaming pump's completion drain is outside its own budget: eight chunk decodes on one frame with no way to stop | fixed | `StreamingHost::pump` samples a clock and accounts for it inside `m_manager.tick(budget)` -- but the loop ABOVE that, which takes finished reads and materialises them, was bounded by nothing except `initIo`'s `maxInFlight`. Worst case is every in-flight chunk decoding on one frame with no interruption point, which is exactly the shape of the hitch M7's gate claims there are none of. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by checking the deadline at the top of the drain. What is left in `m_reads` stays there and the loop resumes next frame where it stopped, which it was already written to do |
| D128 | agent, 2026-08-26 | Every UI picture a frame newly names is read and decoded in that frame, unbounded -- and the whole state machine had no test | fixed | The last of D118's class. `UiText::loadPendingImages` reads and decodes every `ImageLabel`'s image on the frame it is first named, with no bound: a HUD that comes up with eight icons pays all eight at once, at the 14 to 36 ms per 1024-square PNG measured for D118. **Fixed 2026-08-26** with the same three stages -- `readFileAsync`, the job pool as `Domain::AssetIo`, and the upload on the frame -- off by default, `!options.headless` in the host, four in flight, and a fall-through to the synchronous read when the IO service will not take a request. **Three things the port had to change rather than move.** `pending`/`failed` were two booleans that could not say "in flight": `pending = false; failed = true;` assumed failure and took it back two statements later, which split across frames reads as permanently dead -- so the entry carries a named state now. The handle table is rebuilt where a HANDLE IS ASSIGNED and not where the queue is drained: rebuilding on the queueing pass writes an invalid handle, never rebuilds again, and the picture draws as flat tint for ever with nothing logged -- and the lengths agree, so no size check finds it. That one is break-verified. And `~UiText` was `= default` while jobs write into memory it owns, so it waits now, as `destroy` does. **`engine/app/tests/ui_text_tests.cpp` is new**: there was no test file at all and `layout_tests.cpp` stubs the provider out, so read, decode, upload and the little table `ui` indexes into were held together by nothing but adjacency |
| D129 | agent, 2026-08-26 | A sound's first play decodes the whole file on the frame that plays it | fixed | `AudioSystem::Impl::clipFor` reads and fully decodes into the mixer's format on the calling thread, and it is called from two places: `AudioSystem::update` on the render frame, and `clipDuration` inside `AudioSystem::tick`. **Deliberately not fixed, and this row is the reason.** The tick's half cannot be made asynchronous: it advances `timePosition`, wraps loops and fires `Ended`, all of which `replay.cpp` hashes -- an answer that depended on decode latency would be a world hash that depends on disk speed (R10). The shape that works is a background prefetch with a blocking JOIN in the tick, deterministic because the answer is the same every run, and free in practice because the prefetch started frames earlier. **What stopped it being written**: this repository contains no audio file at all, so the cost has never been measured and neither has the fix -- and every other item in this sweep was justified by a number. Building it on a guess is the thing the rest of this session was about not doing. Also worth recording beside it: a fully decoded three-minute Ogg is roughly 69 MB of resident f32, grown through a doubling `resize` because Vorbis supplies no length hint. Streaming music rather than decoding it is the real answer to that half and is a larger piece of work **Fixed in `d56fece1` and `60ecd6d8`.** The first is the prefetch: an asynchronous read and a decode job started from the frame, with the synchronous path kept as the floor, so a sound that exists before it plays is resident when it does. The second removes both things that row left: the tick reads a sound's length from its file's HEADER (`detail::probeFrames`, Ogg Vorbis read by hand from its last page), so a sound made and played on one tick decodes nothing on it; and a file longer than ten seconds STREAMS -- its encoded bytes are kept and each voice decodes a window just ahead of the mixer -- so three minutes of music costs the file's size, not 70 MB. `audio_tests.cpp` asserts both, sample for sample, through `AudioSystem::renderInto` |
| D130 | human, 2026-08-26 | "ainda nao consigo atribuir um material a uma part ou mesh part" -- the whole reference editor shipped inert | fixed | Reported after the picker, the Explorer drag and the content drag had all landed and all been tested. `editable()` returns false for every `EditorKind::InstanceRef`, and has since M4 -- deliberately, because reparenting from the panel was out of that brief's scope and `Parent` is an Instance property, so an editable reference widget would have been the one feature it excluded arriving by accident. The panel grew a real reference editor and **this predicate was not told**: `drawInstanceRef` reads it for `locked`, so the button came up disabled and the drop target was never installed. `BasePart.Material` -- a property whose entire purpose is to be set -- could not be set by any means. **Nothing caught it because every test reached the COMMAND behind the widget and none reached the one predicate that decides whether the widget is live**, which is the general shape: a feature can be complete, tested and inert. **Fixed 2026-08-26**, with the enum's own comment -- which still said "Displayed and never written" -- corrected beside it, because the stale rule was written down twice |
| D131 | agent, 2026-08-26 | Every failed asynchronous read leaks its slot, and at 512 all asynchronous IO stops process-wide | fixed | Found twice on the same day from two directions: another session left a `TEMP leak probe` in `async_io_tests.cpp` asserting that 600 consecutive missing-file reads all succeed, and it did not -- and an adversarial review of this session's new pipelines traced the same thing to their call sites. `pumpIo` releases a completed request's slot only when it carries a CALLBACK; a caller that polls `ioStatus` instead gets its slot back from `takeIoResult`, which releases one only for `Ready`. So every read that fails keeps its slot for ever, and the pool is a fixed 512. Past that, thumbnails, material textures and world streaming all stop at once, silently, process-wide -- and material textures stopping means the 14 to 36 ms frame stall D118 removed comes back. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by releasing the terminal-but-unconsumed request with `cancelIo`, which is what it is for -- `releaseSlotLocked` is exactly what it reaches on a request that is neither queued nor in flight -- at all three new call sites and at the pre-existing `StreamingHost::pump`, which had the same hole since M7. **Not fixed inside `pumpIo`**: releasing callback-less `Failed` requests there would turn an observable `Failed` into `Unknown` and break the contract `async_io.h` states and a test asserts. **The residual was never the leak, it was the SILENCE** (S8.5, fixed 2026-08-27): `readFileAsync` returns an invalid request when the pool is full and every caller falls through to a synchronous read, which is right and is exactly why nothing broke -- so a pool taken to 512 by any future leak would again stop every asynchronous path in the process at once with nothing said in any direction. It logs now, once per EPISODE: not per call, because a full pool is asked hundreds of times a second, and not once per process, because a pool that fills, drains and fills again has told you two different things. Logged outside the mutex, so a formatter that allocates cannot put the pump thread behind a logger. `async_io_tests.cpp` asserts the ceiling itself -- 512 allocate, the 513th is refused rather than hung, one release restores capacity, and the pool comes back WHOLE -- which is the assertion the original leak would have failed, break-verified by removing the release from `cancelIo` |
| D132 | agent, 2026-08-26 | `Instance.Destroying` stopped running for the script that owns it -- a regression introduced by D097 the same day | fixed | Found by an adversarial review of the session that introduced it. D097 made a destroyed `Script`'s threads stop being resumed, which is right -- and `World::destroy` MARKS the instance before it queues the `Destroying` fire, so a script's own `Destroying` handler is owned by an instance that is already destroyed. The engine's documented last-chance cleanup hook became unreachable for the one script that most needs it, with no diagnostic at all. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by giving the rule a REASON rather than a bool: `suppressionFor` answers `Retired | Destroyed | Disabled | None`, the queue entry built for `Destroying` is flagged, and that entry -- and only that entry -- lets `Destroyed` through. `Retired` and `Disabled` still suppress, and every OTHER signal a destroyed script had connected still suppresses, which is what keeps D097 fixed. Two conformance cases: the hook runs, and a sibling's `ChildAdded` connected by the same dead script stays silent. Break-verified |
| D133 | agent, 2026-08-26 | A material dropped on a part inside an open stamp is written as null, silently, and pushed into every instance in the world | fixed | A stamp is written from its root DOWN, so a reference to anything beside the stamp rather than under it is one the file cannot carry -- and `assignStampTo` placed the `Material` under the stage's WORKSPACE, beside the stamp being edited. The save dropped it to `null`, the next open showed an untextured part, and `restamp` then pushed that null into every instance of the stamp in the game's world. Silent in all three directions. **Fixed 2026-08-26** in three parts, because any one alone still loses something: the material is placed under the stamp's ROOT when a stamp is open (the SEARCH root stays the whole tree, or the search would stop finding a material that is already there and place duplicates); `saveStamp` now builds a `StampLibrary` and passes it to `writeStamp`, as `Editor::save` has since ADR 0051 -- without it a stamp containing a stamped instance wrote that instance in full and unlinked; and `saveStamp` reports `droppedReferences` and marks the status as a failure, the way `save` has always reported a scene's. Break-verified by placing beside instead of under: the reference is dropped and the reopened part has no material |
| D134 | agent, 2026-08-26 | Dropping the same material on a part that already wears it records an undo step that undoes nothing | fixed | The invariant is `editor.cpp`'s own rather than a row here -- it is written at every verb that can refuse (`paste`, `instantiateStamp`, `reparent`) and is why `UndoStack::depth` is exposed at all: **a step that undoes nothing eats a press of ctrl-Z** -- and worse here, because `UndoStack::record` clears the redo stack, so a step taken back afterwards has already destroyed a real redo future and leaves a junk one in its place. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by moving the search, which does not mutate, ahead of the record, and returning success without recording when every live target already holds exactly what was dropped. Preferred over undoing the step afterwards for the reason above |
| D135 | agent, 2026-08-26 | A refused rebuild is recorded as applied for motion and material, so an Anchored part keeps falling with its friction change lost | fixed | The other half of D123, found by review. The record takes what it ATTEMPTED, which is right for the retry gate -- but it took the WHOLE description, so a rebuild the backend refused left the mirror believing the backend had a motion type and a friction it had refused, and `describedDifferently` then went false for ever. **Fixed 2026-08-26**: a refusal commits only the three fields the retry is gated on -- shape, motion, density -- and leaves friction, restitution, the flags, the group and `record.written` alone, so the incremental branch pushes them through their own setters on the next tick and a `CFrame` written in the same tick as a refusal is delivered rather than recorded as delivered and swallowed. And `record.backendMotion` is a second field on purpose, because `motion` now has two meanings: `writeBack`'s kinematic skip and the kinematic re-target want what the backend HAS, not what it was asked for. **Also corrected**: two comments claimed a coplanar quad is a hull Jolt refuses. It is not -- `ConvexHullBuilder` accepts one and gives it a thickness -- and naming a case that does not happen is worse than naming none, because somebody writes a test around it |
| D136 | human, 2026-08-25 | Clicking a breadcrumb three folders deep closes the window with nothing in the console | fixed | **Filed 2026-08-26, for a defect reported and fixed on 2026-08-25 that was never given a row** -- which is the disappearance this register exists to prevent, and it survived a green gate because `docs-lint` excluded this file from its own cross-reference sweep: D121 could point at `D-` and nothing looked. `ContentTree::currentFolder()` returned a REFERENCE into the tree and `leave()` assigns to that very string, so the breadcrumb held a path across its own navigation -- clicking a crumb shortened the string in the middle of the loop, and the next step read `substr(begin, ...)` with a `begin` measured against the old, longer path. Three deep is the shallowest that reaches a second step after a click: `a/b/c`, click `a`, two `leave()` calls empty the string, and the next read asks for position 2 of nothing. An uncaught `std::out_of_range` is a process that dies saying nothing, which is exactly what was reported. **Fixed 2026-08-25 in `2e967cde`** at both ends: the accessor returns by value, which kills the whole class rather than this one caller, and the breadcrumb records where it was asked to climb to and climbs after it has finished drawing -- a chain that shortened halfway through would go on drawing crumbs for a folder it had already left. ImGui cannot be driven headlessly, so the case in `content_tree_tests.cpp` is the loop's ARITHMETIC over a real tree, break-verified by restoring both halves of the old shape |
| D137 | agent, 2026-08-26 | The toolbar's New button empties an unsaved scene with no prompt, where the same verb in the File menu asks | fixed | Found by audit. **Every other door onto this question asks, and asks in one place.** `drawMenuBar`'s `guard` (`debug_overlay.cpp:4010`) refuses to act while `editor.hasUnsavedWork()` and parks the verb in `dialogs.pending` for the "Unsaved Changes" modal to re-issue: New Project, Open Project, New Scene and Exit all route through it, `openSceneOrAsk` routes through it, and a close request sets the same pending. The toolbar's button (`debug_overlay.cpp:2876`) sets `commands.newScene` directly. **It cannot guard even in principle**: `drawTransport` (`debug_overlay.cpp:2737`) is handed `Editor`, `EditorCommands` and the icon atlas and nothing else, and so is `drawViewport` (`debug_overlay.cpp:3077`) which calls it -- neither has an `EditorDialogs&` to park anything in, though `drawShell` holds one and passes it to every other panel that can lose work. The tooltip is honest about the consequence -- "empty the scene and start over -- anything unsaved is gone" -- and that is the tell: the behaviour was written down rather than questioned. Nothing caught it because every test reaches the COMMAND and the command is right; what differs is which door sets it, and no test drives the toolbar **Fixed in `74e78189`**: the toolbar's New goes through `issueOrAsk`, the one gate every door that empties the scene shares, and `debug_overlay_tests.cpp` drives every door through it with and without unsaved work. |
| D138 | agent, 2026-08-26 | A stamp whose root is a `Model` is placed at the world origin instead of in front of the camera | fixed | Found by audit. `Editor::instantiateStamp` puts what it built in front of the view by writing `CFrame` through `setProperty` (`editor.cpp:1467-1476`), copying the shape `createInstance` uses -- and there that shape is right for the reason its own comment gives: a class with no `CFrame` "simply refuses and nothing is placed", which is what a `Folder` should get. **A stamp is not that case, and `Model` has no `CFrame`**: the dump gives it `PrimaryPart`, `Scale` and `StreamingMode` over `PVInstance`'s `PivotOffset`, and `scene/pivot.h` says it in words -- "a `Model` has no transform, so it borrows its primary part's". So `setProperty` returns `UnknownProperty`, the result is `(void)`-cast, and the subtree stays wherever the file authored it, which for a prefab built at the origin is the origin. `examples/10-open-world/content/stamps/lanternpost.stamp.json` is exactly that shape: a `Model` root over two parts at x=0, z=0. **Not a one-line fix**, which is why it is worth a row: the arithmetic that moves a model is `methodPivotTo` in `instance_binding.cpp:589`, a `lua_State*` function, so the only C++ spelling of "put this model there" lives above the editor and cannot be called from it. Nothing caught it because no test of `instantiateStamp` adopts a camera -- `m_cameraAdopted` is false, so the placement branch is never entered -- and no test anywhere asserts where a placed stamp landed **Fixed in `74e78189`**: placement goes through the pivot (`pivotTo`), which a `Model` has, instead of a `CFrame` it does not; `editor_tests.cpp` asserts a `Model`-rooted stamp lands in front of an adopted camera, subtree and all, and that a `Part` root still lands where it did. |
| D139 | agent, 2026-08-26 | `dev.err.not_implemented` is in no catalog and carries no namespace, and the lint built for exactly this cannot see it | fixed | Found by audit. The dev protocol answers a reserved verb -- `asset-changed`, `eval` -- rather than ignoring it, which `briefs/m3-kickoff.md` specifies and which is right: a caller that gets silence cannot tell "not yet" from "lost". It answers with `writer.field("key", "dev.err.not_implemented")` (`engine/app/src/engine.cpp:1955`), a raw string on a JSON writer. **There is no such key.** `i18n/en.json` has no entry for it, and every dev key the engine does own is namespaced -- `engine.dev.err.bad_url`, `engine.dev.info.attached`, `engine.dev.warn.bad_message` -- so the name is wrong twice over, and a client that resolves it gets the `[i18n:missing:%08x]` marker `i18n.cpp` degrades an unknown key to. **`tools/repo/i18nlint.luau` is structurally blind to it**, which is the part worth recording: it collects references with `LUAUG_TR%(%s*"([^"]*)"%s*%)` and lints four named sinks (`log`, `logText`, `makeError`, `raise`), and a key written as a field on a wire message is neither a `LUAUG_TR` use nor a sink argument. Its own header calls a key missing from the catalog "a build failure"; this one has never been one, and the gate reports the tree clean **Fixed in `74e78189`**: the key is `engine.dev.err.not_implemented`, it is in `i18n/en.json`, and `i18nlint` reads keys written as wire fields, not only through the macro. |
| D140 | human, 2026-08-26 | "corrige esses bugs aq q preciso testar rapido" -- the editor died on a project the owner was building, three times, and the dump could not be read | fixed | The crash was an UNCAUGHT C++ EXCEPTION and not a fault: the Windows record said `0xe06d7363`, which is Visual C++'s own code for a `throw` nobody caught. **It was introduced hours earlier by the fix for the instance-valued override loss**: `collectOverrides` decided by the property's DECLARED type and then called `std::get<core::InstanceId>` on the value, and every accessor in this tree answers `Value{}` -- Nil -- for an instance whose component is not there, which is the ordinary case and not an error. `std::get` on that throws `bad_variant_access`, and nothing between `collectOverrides` and `main` catches it. `setPrimaryPart`, three lines away in the same fixture, has always used `std::get_if`. **Fixed 2026-08-26** with `get_if` at both reads: ask what a value HOLDS, never what the schema says it should. **The second half of this row is why it took an hour**: the crash handler wrote a minidump and nothing else, and its header said that was deliberate because "the dump is for a developer with a debugger" -- there is no debugger installed on the machine this engine is developed on. It writes `luaug-crash-<pid>.txt` now, with the exception code, its plain name, the faulting address and a symbolised stack, plus a `std::terminate` handler for the throw case. `run_crash_gate.cmake` asserts the note's CONTENT two ways, and doing so found that on MSVC the runtime's own `__try` around `main` means `terminate` never runs for an uncaught throw -- so the note carries the stack at the throw site rather than the `what()` string, and the gate requires the file and line |
| D141 | agent, 2026-08-26 | `World::moveChild` reports a move for a child that did not move, so a reorder to where something already is eats a press of ctrl-Z | fixed | Found by its own test, which was written correctly and then not satisfied: the predicate was left as the literal `if (false) return MoveResult::Unchanged;` under a complete comment explaining why it had to be there. That is the exact invariant D134 records -- a step that undoes nothing eats an undo, and `UndoStack::record` clears the redo stack with it, so a step taken back afterwards has already destroyed a real redo future. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by walking the child list to the child and comparing its index with the one asked for; walked rather than stored because the hierarchy is an intrusive singly-linked list where a child does not know its own index, and a cached one would have to be maintained by every other verb that touches the list. The walk stops AT the child, so it costs the distance to it and not the length of the list |
| D142 | agent, 2026-08-26 | An instance-valued property set inside a PLACED stamp is dropped on save, silently, so the edit is reverted to whatever the stamp said | fixed | The placed-stamp twin of D133, and it ate the material workflow that shipped the same week: assign a `Material` to a part inside a placed stamp, save, reopen, and the part is bare. `collectOverrides` skipped every `ValueType::Instance` property outright, under a comment whose reasoning was true and narrower than the rule -- a reference INSIDE a stamp resolves inside that stamp, so it is not an override. That is right, and it is not a reason to skip a reference RE-POINTED at a different member, or one naming anything BESIDE the stamp. Neither reached the writer that counts a dropped reference, so `droppedReferences` stayed zero and nothing said anything in any direction. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by comparing instance-valued properties by PATH under each subtree's own root rather than by id -- equal paths mean the stamp's own reference untouched, which is the case the blanket skip was protecting and which must stay not-an-override or every placed stamp in a world would carry one for nothing. The loader needed no change at all: it has resolved instance properties through a deferred pass since the format existed, and only the writer skipped. **Reproduced first**, with three cases of which two failed. The fix then caused D140 by reading the variant with `std::get` |
| D143 | agent, 2026-08-26 | `World::clone` reads a freed instance record for every instance it copies, and has since clone existed | fixed | **Found by the first sanitizer run this repository has ever performed**, which is the whole argument for having turned them on. `clone` held `const InstanceRecord* record = m_instances.find(original)` and then called `create()` -- which inserts into that same slot map, and an insert that grows its vector moves every record in it. `record->classId` was read BEFORE the call and was fine; `record->name`, one line after it, was a read through a dangling pointer, so every clone of every instance in this engine was reading freed memory to decide what to name the copy. It never misbehaved because the freed page was still mapped and still held the old bytes, which is exactly the failure ASan exists to make visible and nothing else here could. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by copying the class and the name out before `create` rather than reading them after it. It is the same rule the deferred decode pipelines each learned separately -- nothing may hold a pointer into a container across a call that can grow it -- and what hid it here is that the container and the caller are the same object. Three more things had to be fixed before the sanitizers could report anything at all, and none of them were defects in this engine: the Tier-2 image had no sanitizer runtime, so Luau's `lmem.cpp` failed to COMPILE rather than to link; UBSan aborted the shader compiler at build time on a SPIRV-Cross downcast, at 124 of 1,263 objects; and `tools/cli/project.luau` did not list `linux-clang-asan`, so the suite ran entirely green and then failed with no output because `luaug test` could not find the engine |
| D144 | agent, 2026-08-26 | `CFrame.lookAt` straight up or straight down returns the identity rotation, so anything aimed at something directly above or below it points along -Z instead | fixed | Found while proving that `SpotLight.Shadows` changes a picture: a spot aimed at the floor from eight metres above it lit nothing at all, and the probe that asked why printed `CFrame.lookAt((0,7,0), (0,0,0)).LookVector` as `(0, 0, -1)`. **It was documented and tested behaviour**, not an oversight -- `api-design.md` section 2.3 said the identity is the answer where the direction is degenerate OR where `up` is parallel to it, and `math_tests.cpp` asserted both. The two are not the same situation. With `eye == target` there is no direction and the identity is the only sane answer; with a parallel `up` the direction is perfectly well defined and only the ROLL about it is undetermined, so answering with the identity throws away the part the caller actually asked for. Every implementation worth comparing against picks a fallback roll instead. **Fixed 2026-08-26** in `core::lookAt` and `core::lookAtCFrame` through one shared `basisUp`, which keeps the requested up where it is usable and falls back to `(0,0,1)` -- or `(1,0,0)` where the direction is itself mostly Z -- where it is not. The matrix form was worse than the CFrame form: it normalised a zero cross product rather than testing for it. A zero `up` hint is now the same case for the same reason, rather than a third answer to one question. The spec and the cases were rewritten to state the new rule and why the old one conflated two things |
| D145 | agent, 2026-08-26 | `buildRagdoll` holds a `PartComponent*` across the `create` calls that grow the pool it points into, so every limb after the first is placed from freed memory | fixed | **Found by the determinism case, not by a crash, and that is the whole point of having one.** The rig's transform was read through a pointer taken before any part existed; each `Part` created below adds to that same pool, and a `std::vector` that grows moves what everything pointing into it named. The read was RIGHT most of the time -- the freed bytes usually still held the old component -- so the ten cases asserting limb lengths, bone placement and joint frames all passed, every time. What failed was "two builds of the same profile produce the same instances", about one run in three, because the second build's allocator handed it a different neighbour. The leaf limb's direction came out zero, its capsule collapsed to a sphere at the wrist, and the world hash moved. **The same shape as D143** -- a pool pointer held across a mutation of that pool -- three weeks apart and in code written by somebody who had just fixed D143. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by copying the frame out by value before anything is created, and doing the same for the two pointers pass three holds across three `create` calls: nothing in those touches the parts pool TODAY, which is exactly the kind of true-right-now this row exists to stop relying on |
| D146 | agent, 2026-08-26 | Every `MeshPart` nobody set `CollisionFidelity` on collides as its BOUNDING BOX while the enum documents a hull, and the one that asked for `Box` gets a hull -- two opposite faults from one constant | fixed | **The mirror short-circuited on item ZERO.** Zero is `Default`, whose whole meaning is "the engine chooses" and whose documented choice is `Hull`; `Box` is item two. So the common case -- a mesh part somebody dropped in and never touched -- got a box a metre outside the rock it draws, and the rare deliberate case got the hull it was asking to avoid. **Neither is visible in a screenshot and neither raises**: what they produce is a character walking on air beside a rock, or falling into geometry it should have stood on. **The test suite agreed with the defect**, which is the part worth keeping: a case titled "CollisionFidelity Box is honoured exactly" set the property to 0 and asserted a box -- the test and the code held the same wrong belief about which item was which, so a case written specifically about this behaviour passed throughout. Found while reading `shapeOf` for S6.2, by noticing that the comment directly above the line describes the opposite of what the line does. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by naming the items in an enum rather than comparing against a literal, and by five cases that pin each fidelity to the shape it asks for -- break-verified, and the old constant fails three of them |
| D147 | agent, 2026-08-26 | The one test that enters the editor shell tore SDL down before the device it had built, and crashed in `VULKAN_DestroyDevice` the moment a Linux machine could actually run it | fixed | **Found by giving the Tier-2 image a screen** (S7.8). The Linux tier has had a software Vulkan device through lavapipe and no display at all, so `on a real device, F3 flips the panel` returned at its first line, reported a pass, and the editor shell was entered on exactly one of the two machines the gate uses. Installing `xvfb` and running `ctest` under `xvfb-run` made it run, and it segfaulted immediately -- inside SDL3's own `VULKAN_DestroyDevice`, from `~SdlGpuDevice`, at the closing brace of the test. The case called `luaug::platform::shutdown()` by hand at the end while `window` and `device` were locals declared AFTER it, so both were destroyed after SDL had already gone. `run`, `runLauncher` and `runTwoWorlds` each carry a `PlatformScope` declared before the window for precisely this, with a comment saying declaration order is shutdown order reversed; the test predated none of that and simply did not have one. **Fixed 2026-08-26** by giving all three cases in the file that scope guard -- the two on the Null backend included, where the destructor frees nothing today and the SHAPE is what bit -- and deleting the four hand-written `shutdown()` calls. **Nothing could have caught it**: it needs a display and a real driver at once, and no machine in this project had both until this change. The window is created VISIBLE now for the same reason -- a hidden one gets a backbuffer on Windows and none under lavapipe, so `.visible = false` was silently costing the Linux tier every assertion past the icon atlas |
| D148 | agent, 2026-08-27 | The world hash included the engine version, so bumping 1.0.0 to 1.1.0 broke every checked-in determinism trace at tick ZERO with nothing about any world having changed | fixed | **Found by cutting v1.1.0**, which is the only way it could be found: nothing else in the project's life had changed the version since the traces were recorded. `World::worldHash` reaches class-specific state through the generated accessors rather than reading components directly -- deliberately, so a property whose storage moves cannot silently stop being covered -- and that walk reached `DataModel.EngineVersion`. **The file already argued against exactly this** twenty lines above, about `ClassId`: hashed by NAME because "registration order is a property of the engine build rather than of the world". A version string is the same kind of thing and nobody noticed. **The churn is not the defect; what the churn hides is.** If a release is expected to move every trace, then a real determinism regression shipped in that release is indistinguishable from the expected movement -- and detecting precisely that is the only reason the traces are checked in. Re-recording would have been the obvious response and would have masked any R10 break in the same commit. **Fixed** with `HostFact` in the IDL: a property that describes the build or the machine rather than the world, carried to `PropertyDesc::hostFact` and skipped by the hash. Four are marked -- `DataModel.EngineVersion`, `DataModel.LuauVersion`, and `UIService.{SafeAreaInsets, DisplayScale}`, the last two being the same defect not yet biting: a trace recorded on a doubled display would not have replayed on an ordinary one. Nothing is lost, because a script that BRANCHES on any of them still moves the world and the branch's effects are in the ordinary hashed properties it wrote. **Break-verified** by bumping the version to 9.9.9, rebuilding and running the gate: it passes, where 1.0.0 to 1.1.0 failed at tick 0. The traces were re-recorded once, on both platforms, and will not move for a version again |
| D149 | agent, 2026-08-27 | `api-design.md` promised that the CLI warns when a project has `src/client` or `src/server`, the two folder names v1 reserved for the client/server split, and no such warning was ever built | fixed | **Found while opening post-v1 phase 4**, checking which of v1's reservations were real before building on them. Two were claimed. One is genuine: `Enum.RunContext` is in `api/defs/enums.api.luau` with both items, honestly documented as read by nothing, so code written today cannot come to mean something else later. The other was prose -- a repo-wide search for either folder name outside `api-design.md` finds nothing in `tools/cli/`, in `engine/`, or in the CLI's own message catalogue. **Not fixed by building the warning**, and that is the decision rather than the shortcut: phase 4 opened on 2026-08-27, so those folders stop being names to warn about and start being names the engine acts on -- a warning built now would be built to be deleted by the milestone that gives them meaning. The sentence says what is true instead, which is what the reservation actually bought. **The shape is the campaign's own recurring one** and worth the register row for that alone: a documented behaviour with no implementation reads as built, and the only reason this one was caught is that somebody was about to rely on it |
| D150 | agent, 2026-08-27 | Every `Unreliable` and `UnreliableSequenced` payload larger than one MTU was silently sent RELIABLY -- acknowledged, ordered and head-of-line blocking -- which is the exact behaviour those modes exist to avoid, and a state delta is precisely the payload that crosses the threshold | fixed | **Found by a reconnaissance pass over `engine/net` before building replication on it**, and it had been there since the transport landed. `flagsFor` (`engine/net/src/enet_transport.cpp`) mapped `Unreliable` to `ENET_PACKET_FLAG_UNSEQUENCED` and `UnreliableSequenced` to no flag at all, and neither set `ENET_PACKET_FLAG_UNRELIABLE_FRAGMENT`. **`enet_peer_send` checks for fragmentation FIRST**, before it dispatches on unsequenced-versus-reliable (`third_party/enet/peer.c:121`), and its fragment branch tests `(flags & (RELIABLE \| UNRELIABLE_FRAGMENT)) == UNRELIABLE_FRAGMENT`; with neither bit set that test fails and the `else` sends `SEND_FRAGMENT \| COMMAND_FLAG_ACKNOWLEDGE`. The threshold is `mtu - sizeof(ENetProtocolHeader) - sizeof(ENetProtocolSendFragment)`, about **1364 bytes** at ENet's default MTU of 1392. `transport.h` documents `UnreliableSequenced` as "what most state replication actually wants", so the one mode chosen for the job was the one that stopped doing it as soon as the payload got interesting. **Nothing could have caught it by observation.** ENet is linked PRIVATE so no test above that file may name its constants -- the CMakeLists calls that "the R17 rule holding in the build graph rather than only in the header" -- and over a loopback a wrongly-reliable packet arrives anyway, same bytes, same order. So the guard is five `static_assert`s beside the mapping: costs nothing, runs on every build on every platform, and reads as the contract. Break-verified by removing the flag, which fails the build naming D150. **A second gap went with it**: every other case in `transport_tests.cpp` sends four bytes, so multi-fragment reassembly had never run at all on any mode -- there is now a 5000-byte round trip with a position-dependent pattern, because a buffer of one repeated value passes even when reassembly drops or reorders a fragment. **One honest caveat**: ENet has no unsequenced-fragment command, so a fragmented `Unreliable` payload is delivered sequenced. Droppable-and-sequenced is far nearer that mode's contract than reliable-and-ordered, and the comment says so rather than leaving it to be rediscovered |
