| D151 | agent, 2026-08-27 | `updateBody` re-announced every contact on the body it reshaped: a script writing `part.Size` on something a character was standing on got a `Touched` for a contact that never physically broke, and never a matching `TouchEnded` | fixed | **Found while designing F1's terrain sculpting**, where dragging a brush reshapes the ground somebody stands on many times a second -- but nothing about the defect is terrain. `updateBody` is `RemoveBody` + `DestroyBody` + `CreateAndAddBody` and it called `forgetPairs` first, which erases every contact pair naming the body from `m_previousPairs`; `Began`/`Ended` are a DIFF against that list, so the contact was absent from the previous set, present in the next, and reported as new. **The call was copied from `destroyBody`, where it is right for the exact reason it is wrong here** -- that function's own comment says "the pair can never appear again", and a reshaped body persists: same record, same handle, only `record->id` changes. Removing it is self-correcting, because a reshape that genuinely ends a contact leaves the pair in the previous set and absent from the next, which is the `Ended` the diff exists to emit. **The reproduction is the part worth keeping.** The first version of the test settled a cube for 240 ticks and then reshaped the floor, and it PASSED -- because a sleeping body produces no contact callbacks at all, so `began == 0` meant "nothing was observed" rather than "nothing was re-announced". A test that could not fail is worse than no test. Jolt's sleep timer is half a second, so the case now steps only until the contact is announced, asserts the body is still awake, and reshapes on that tick -- and it fails by exactly one spurious `Began` against the unfixed engine. `updateBody`'s only other coverage was one constraint-lifetime case, so nothing else was watching this |
| D152 | agent, 2026-08-27 | A mesh that failed to load once was blacklisted for the life of the loader, so fixing the file on disk and reloading it did nothing until the editor was restarted -- which is the one thing ADR 0062's "a changed asset reloads itself" promises does not happen | fixed | **Found while reading `mesh_loader.cpp` for F1's Part D**, and it is independent of terrain. `failed_` exists so a URN that will not load costs one attempt rather than one per frame for ever, and it was cleared in exactly one place: `destroy`. `forget` -- the function S6.4 added precisely so a changed asset could be dropped and re-read -- removed the library entry, the GPU handles and the cache slot, and left the refusal behind. So `sync`'s `binary_search` skipped the URN on every subsequent frame and the corrected file never arrived. **The fix is four lines and the reasoning is one sentence**: forgetting a URN means forgetting everything about it, the refusal included, because the reason it failed may be the thing that just changed. **Break-verified** by removing the erase, which fails the new case on both assertions -- the sync loads nothing and the library stays empty -- and passes with it. The case is written the way the defect actually arrives: sync with no mounts so the URN is blacklisted, then mount the store (the file being fixed), then `forget`, then sync again |
| D153 | agent, 2026-08-27 | A block placed into terrain kept its material in the FIRST column of a tile and lost it in the other 1023, so a placed block was one square metre of ground and a thousand square metres of nothing wearing the same height -- and creating a tile at all put an invisible flat plane at y = 0 under every column of it | fixed | **Two causes wearing one symptom**, found by a test written for `paintBall` rather than for either of them, and each break-verified alone. **One**: `afterEdit`'s add branch tested `inside > 0`, so a sample lying exactly ON a flat-topped brush's face fell through to `return existing` -- and the height layer reads its material from precisely that sample. It also cost four tenths of a metre of height, because the surface crossing was then found a voxel lower; a strict `>` disagreed with the `distance <= 0` convention every other site uses. **Two**: a height tile is 32 by 32 columns and is written one column at a time, so filling the first left 1023 columns with a height of zero and a material of zero. `sample` read that as a real surface. That is what made the first column of a tile behave differently from every column after it, which is the shape that gave the defect away. **The fix states a rule the encoding was already implying**: material zero means "no ground in this column" on the height layer, exactly as zero already means erase to `fillBall` and is refused by `paintBall`. `heightAt` agrees, and a column carved away entirely now says zero rather than keeping the material of the ground that used to be there. Neither half is a special case |
| D154 | agent, 2026-08-27 | The sculpting brush burrowed: holding the button still over one spot dug to the world floor, and the same drag left a fifteen-metre-deeper trench at one framerate than at another | fixed | **A brush that aims at the surface it is CHANGING has a feedback loop in it.** Each stamp lowers the ground, so the next frame's ray lands lower, so the next stamp is lower again. Measured before the fix: a drag that should have laid 21 stamps laid 337. Found by the headless rig asserting that a stroke cut into four frames and the same stroke cut into forty leave the same ground -- not by anybody dragging, which is the point of putting the arithmetic where a test can reach it. **The ray is now cast against the field as it was when the stroke began** and against the live field only while hovering, which is what every editor in this shape does and is affordable here only because a terrain snapshot is a vector of shared pointers rather than a copy of the ground (ADR 0067). It also makes a stroke a pure function of where the pointer went, which is what lets the test assert sameness rather than similarity |
| D155 | agent, 2026-08-27 | A stroke walked backwards from the pointer dropped its fractional remainder every frame, so the number of edits a drag performed depended on how it was cut into frames -- 29 stamps against 21 for the same drag | fixed | **The obvious design is the wrong one, and it is worth writing down because it reads as right.** Walking back from `to` guarantees the pointer's own position is stamped, which is what a person is looking at. But it puts the fractional part of the distance at the `from` end, and a caller continuing the stroke from `to` has silently thrown that fraction away. Forward from `from` in whole steps leaves the remainder at the `to` end, where the pointer carries it into the next frame; the caller resumes from the last stamp exactly. The cost is that the newest stamp can sit up to one step behind the pointer -- a quarter of a brush width at the default spacing, inside the brush that is already there -- and the aiming ring is what follows the pointer exactly. Paired with a gate (`strokeAdvanced`): below one step the frame stamps nothing and the anchor stays put, accumulating |
| D156 | agent, 2026-08-27 | A scene save wrote every instance in the world and none of the terrain, so sculpting, saving and reopening lost the work with no error anywhere | fixed | **Found by looking rather than by a failure**, one commit after the brush landed. Terrain is the one piece of world state that is not a property -- a field is tiles and bricks, not a number -- so nothing in `writeInstance`'s property loop could reach it, and nothing failed to say so. It rides as its own key beside `attributes` and `tags` now. **Its own key rather than a side-car file**, because `writeScene` returns a string and "a scene is one text file" is a contract the editor, the packager, `readScene` and every round-trip test depend on. Base64 over a versioned run-coded binary (`asset::encodeTerrain`) keeps that true at a size the runs make reasonable. **Two details that would each have been a quiet defect of their own**: the reserved height range rides with the field rather than with the properties, because a collider's precision is spread across it at construction and cannot be widened afterwards (ADR 0066); and an empty field writes nothing, which is what stops every existing scene in the repository moving for a feature none of them use |
| D157 | agent, 2026-09-23 | A partitioned scene boots with no block world: the partitioner rebuilt the residual scene from three keys and dropped `voxels` | fixed | `Partitioner::run` wrote the residual as `format`, `version` and `root`, and the block world is a top-level `voxels` member beside `root`, so any run that partitions -- a game outside the editor with four or more cells of parts -- booted with an empty `VoxelService` and no error. Found reading the partitioner to stream terrain from disk; nothing had tested a partition with blocks in it. **Fixed 2026-09-23** by copying every top-level member but those three through verbatim, so a member `writeScene` grows later cannot be dropped the same way, with a test that partitions a scene carrying one |
| D158 | agent, 2026-09-23 | A streamed chunk whose read could not start waited for ever, holding an in-flight slot | fixed | `StreamingHost::beginRead` runs inside the manager's `beginLoad` and reported a missing path or a refused read with `onChunkFailed` on the spot -- and `beginLoad` then returned true, so the manager marked the chunk loading as it returned. The chunk sat in `Loading` with nothing reading it, and its in-flight slot was never given back. Found writing the terrain streamer, which had copied the shape. **Fixed 2026-09-23** in both: a failed start is remembered and reported after the manager's tick, when the chunk is in the state the failure moves it out of |
| D159 | agent, 2026-09-23 | A terrain larger than about a square kilometre saved, and reopened empty | fixed | A scene writes its whole field as one `.lterrain` cell, and `readScene` decoded it against a STREAMED cell's ceiling of 4,096 tiles -- which half-metre ground passes at about a kilometre square. The encoder enforced nothing, so the save succeeded; the load refused the field, counted a dropped reference and booted flat. Found generating a 1.6 km terrain to test streaming. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: a scene's field is decoded against `WholeFieldLimits` (a million tiles), and a compressed body larger than 128 times what is left in the file is refused before anything is allocated, so a corrupt count is bounded by the file whatever ceiling it is held to |
| D160 | agent, 2026-09-23 | A script holding a streamed instance was left holding a dead handle when its chunk was evicted: the husk contract was never wired outside a test | fixed | Found while building the replica's half of the same contract (ADR 0069 decision 6). `StreamingGlue::evict` asks a reference probe before destroying, and `setReferenceProbe` had exactly one caller: `streaming_glue_tests.cpp`. In the engine the probe was empty, so every evicted instance was destroyed, `InstanceStreamedOut` never fired, and a handle a script kept to a streamed-out part resolved to nothing. The glue's own test passed because it installed the probe itself. **Fixed at the seam that was missing**: `script::instanceHeld` answers from the VM's weak instance cache (conservative -- a dropped handle counts until the collector takes it), the engine hands one probe to the streaming host and to the replication seam, and the world host sweeps its husks at every publish, destroying one nothing holds any more and forgetting one a script parented back. A test boots a real VM and checks all three: a held husk's handle resolves and its handler runs, an unheld one is swept, and one parented back is left alone. |
| D161 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | Digging a crater into a selected terrain drew a tinted lid over the hole, an orange outline around an odd shape, and a row of boxes | fixed | Reported with a screenshot. Reproduced headless first: the same dig, one stamp a frame, renders a clean shaft, so the ground was not wrong. The difference was the SELECTION, and `Create Terrain` selects the terrain it makes. `extract` marked a selected terrain's cave meshes as outlined and the height-layer ground as nothing; the outline pass tests no depth, by design, so a selected object shows through what is in front of it, and fills its silhouette with a tint. A cave mesh's sides and skirt sit buried under the height layer, so selecting the terrain drew exactly them, through the ground: the tinted lid and the boxes. **Fixed by never outlining a cave draw**; the ground was never outlined and a line around the whole world would say nothing. `render_world_tests.cpp` extracts the same cave with the terrain selected and requires no outlined draw. |
| D162 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | A hill raised, smoothed or flattened over a dug area kept a slot through it, down to the old ground, as wide as the dig | fixed | Every height brush -- raise, lower, smooth, flatten, Generate Flat Ground and `WriteHeights` -- skipped any column carrying voxels, on the reasoning that a cave has no single height. It always has a TOP, and the top is what a height brush means. Now a bricked column moves by its top: the height layer's value where the top is the height layer's, and otherwise a band of ground laid on it or taken off it, joined exactly (a fillet re-applied every stamp walked the surface centimetres a stamp). The tunnel under it stays. A bricked column's height layer now follows its top after every volumetric edit, with the levels that would change made bricks first, so nothing samples differently -- which is what the ground's normals beside a cave, the ground drawn where a cave is too far to mesh and `HeightAt` all read. `terrain_tests.cpp`: a hill raised over a tunnel ends where the brush's falloff puts it, bricked or not, and the tunnel stays; a hundred small stamps up and down come back to where they started. |
| D163 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | A dig near a hill cut the hill off flat at eight metres above the dig, and a dig high on a hill deleted the lower ground under its reach down to the floor | fixed | Reported as "ainda ta zuado" with a screenshot of floating plates and see-through holes, after D161 and D162 were fixed. **The worst of the three, and the oldest.** A brush examines each column over its own reach plus a margin; a column that range holds entirely as ground has its surface above the range, one entirely air has it below, and the brush touched neither. The comment said the column keeps its height; the code wrote the range's top as the height, and its bottom with no material. Found by comparing the height of every column after a dig with the same world undug: 32 columns of a 17 m hill were 11.00 or 6.00. Every seam hypothesis before it -- snapping, skirts, normals, level of detail -- was a symptom of this, and none of those changes shipped. A column with no surface in range is now not written, except air all the way to the world's floor, which is still no ground (D153). Two tests fail without the fix: a dig at a hill's foot leaves its peak, and a dig high on a hill leaves the plain under its reach. |
| D164 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | Digging caves lagged badly, and building a cave kept producing new faults where it met the ground around it | fixed | Reported as "travadas insanas" while digging, and "vamos ter que fazer estilo o do roblox" after D161 to D163. **The cause was the representation, not any one bug.** The terrain was a height layer with voxel bricks where it stopped being a height function (ADR 0067), and every column a dig reached was promoted to bricks: meshed on the CPU to be drawn, meshed again for its collider, and opened as a hole in the GPU height atlas -- and every fault the owner found was where the two encodings met. Terrain is now one grid of voxels, each a material and an occupancy (ADR 0082), so there is no join to fault. A dig writes occupancies and rebuilds the one mesh and the one collider whose chunks it changed: each is keyed on its chunk and on only the two layers of each neighbour it reads, where whole neighbours rebuilt the nine meshes and twenty-seven colliders round a dig. `terrain_loader_tests.cpp` and `physics_sync_tests.cpp` assert that a dig in the middle of a chunk rebuilds one of each. |
| D165 | agent, 2026-09-23 | A looped sound lost one frame at every loop point | fixed | Found writing the looped-stream test for D129. The mixer wrapped a looped voice's cursor inside the loop that fills the device's buffer, and the `continue` that followed advanced that loop's frame index as well -- so the output frame the wrap happened on was never written, and every loop of every looped sound carried one frame of silence: a faint click, as often as a short loop repeats. Present since M7 made `Content` real. **Fixed in `60ecd6d8`**: the wrap refills the same output frame from the new cursor, in the decoded mixer and the streamed one, and `audio_tests.cpp` asserts the head of a looped sound follows its tail with no frame between |
| D166 | agent, 2026-09-23 | On macOS a headless window cannot be created, so every test that opens one fails there | fixed | Found reading the macOS job's log while proving level-C determinism: the Tier-3 test step is non-blocking and nobody had read its failures. `platform_tests` and two `debug_overlay_tests` fail with "Could not initialize OpenGL / GLES library". Headless mode selects SDL's `offscreen` driver, and on macOS SDL adds `SDL_WINDOW_OPENGL` to every window that names no graphics backend (`SDL_DefaultGraphicsBackends`); the offscreen driver implements OpenGL through EGL, which a Mac does not have. Windows and Linux are unaffected: the default is macOS-only. **Fixed in `299b1060`**: with the offscreen driver on macOS, a window is created through `SDL_CreateWindowWithProperties` with `EXTERNAL_GRAPHICS_CONTEXT` set, which asks SDL for no OpenGL at all -- the renderer brings its own context. CI run 35929395297 ran `platform_tests` and `debug_overlay_tests` green on macOS |
| D167 | agent, 2026-09-23 | On macOS the five capture gates run with no compiled shaders and fail | fixed | Found with D166. `capture_gate` and its four siblings compare a recorded command stream against a golden, and on macOS the engine logs `render.err.shader_manifest_missing`: there is no DirectXShaderCompiler for macOS, so ADR 0032 says the blobs come from a Tier-1 or Tier-2 host -- and the CI job never brought any. The stream recorded without pipelines cannot match the one recorded with them. **Fixed in `299b1060`**, as ADR 0032 says it should be: the Linux leg hands its compiled shaders to the macOS job as an artifact, and the macOS job waits for it. CI run 35929395297 passed all five capture gates on macOS |
| D168 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | In the terrain editor, one ctrl+Z undoes every change made to the terrain | fixed | Reported as "eu aperto ctrl+z ele desfaz todas as modificacoes que fiz no terreno". A stroke opens an undo gesture (`Inspector::beginGesture`) and never closes it, so the next stroke's `beginGesture` returns the same open gesture, `UndoStack::record` coalesces its step into the last one, and every stroke of the session becomes one step. The manipulator and the block tool close theirs; the sculpt stroke is the one that did not **Fixed in `d361cadf`**: the stroke closes its gesture on every path that ends it, and `editor_tests.cpp` asserts two strokes are two undo steps -- a test that fails without the fix |
| D169 | agent, 2026-09-23 | A project whose scene carries terrain and no camera ran with its scripts' `Heartbeat` never firing | fixed | Found loading the owner's saved project to reproduce their editing report. A world whose ground streams holds the simulation on its first load until the ring round every focus is resident, so a character standing on streamed ground does not fall through it. The foci are the workspace's camera and whatever `AddFocus` named; with neither there is no ring, and the wait never ended. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: with no focus the first load does not wait, and says so once (`app.warn.field_no_focus`), naming the two ways to give the world one. Scripts have run by the first pump, so a game that makes its own camera still waits for its ground. `field_streamer_tests.cpp` asserts a world with no camera primes on its first pump and loads nothing |
| D170 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | While editing terrain, shading went wrong next to an edit and stayed wrong | fixed | Reported as "as sombras ficam bugadas" while editing, not on a fresh load: the same ground reopened was right, which placed it in the rebuild and not the mesher. A terrain node was rebuilt when a chunk in its footprint changed, or the two layers of a neighbouring chunk that face it (D164). That is all its triangles read; the openness baked into every vertex reads the columns up to 12 m round it (ADR 0082). An edit a few metres into the next column therefore changed what a node's vertices should hold and left the node as it was: dark where ground had gone, bright where it had come. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: a node's content is its footprint and the whole ring of columns round it, and `terrain_loader_tests.cpp` asserts a dig in the middle of one column rebuilds that node and the eight round it. The colliders keep the narrower key, since a collider reads no openness |
| D171 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | The terrain flickered while it was being edited | fixed | Reported as "parece que da muito flick enquanto edito". The loader rebuilt at most four nodes a frame, nearest first, whether they were loading or edited, so the nodes of one edit were redrawn over several frames and neighbours showed two versions of the same stroke for that long. A held brush changes the ground every frame, so it never settled. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: every edited node is rebuilt in the frame its edit lands (up to 64), meshed in parallel on the job system, and uploaded nearest first; only loading keeps the per-frame budget. The same test asserts the nine nodes of one dig are rebuilt in one frame with the loading budget set to one |
| D172 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | Smoothing with a large brush stalled the editor | fixed | Reported as "a operacao do smooth trava demais", with a radius-20 brush. Smooth read every voxel of its box through the field one at a time, a search of the chunk table each, and blurred it with a kernel walked tap by tap: 13.75 ms a stamp at radius 20, and a held brush stamps up to four times a frame. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: the box is read a chunk row at a time, and each blur pass is a running sum whose cost does not depend on the kernel's width. 2.2 ms a stamp, and every brush at radius 20 is now at or under that |
| D173 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | Generating 5 km of flat ground froze the editor | fixed | Reported as "fui gerar um terreno 5000m travou tudo". `Generate Flat Ground` laid every column voxel by voxel through `writeHeights`: 0.6 ms a chunk column, so 5 km was about thirty seconds on the frame that pressed the button, and about 240 MB of chunks that were all the same two. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: flat ground over empty columns comes out the same in every whole column, so `fillFlat` lays one column and shares it -- chunks are copy-on-write, as undo already relies on, so the first edit to one clones that chunk alone. 5 km now lays in 0.12 s. A scene saves a shared chunk once and reopens chunks with the same bytes shared, so the memory stays what it was when saved. `terrain_tests.cpp` asserts the shared ground is the ground laid column by column, and that a dig in one column leaves the next untouched; `terrain_cell_tests.cpp` that it comes back shared |
| D174 | the owner, 2026-09-23 | Dark lines across a large plain, along the sides of the distant terrain nodes | fixed | Reported with a picture of a 5 km plain crossed by a grid of grey lines. Not streaming and not a crack: measured, the top-level node of a flat plain had vertices 32 m ABOVE the ground. A skirt is two of its level's cells long -- 64 m at the top level -- and ground laid on an empty world is a slab 32 m deep, so the skirt hung from the slab's bottom, which hangs up along the bottom's negative normal, came out of the top and stood in the air along every side of every coarse node, lit from below and so dark. Close up the skirt is 2 m and the slab hides it, which is why only the distance showed it. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: a skirt reaches at most half-way through the ground behind it, measured along its normal over the samples already read (ADR 0082, amended). `terrain_mesher_tests.cpp` meshes the top-level node of a plain and requires nothing above the ground or under the slab |
| D175 | agent, 2026-09-23 | Terrain nodes at the top level read chunks two columns out, and those were neither prepared before meshing in parallel nor part of what the node was rebuilt by | fixed | Found while measuring D174. A node's mesh reads its level's samples two cells past its sides, and its openness map further still with small voxels; at the top level a cell is a whole chunk, so it reads two chunk columns out. The loader prepared, and keyed the node on, one. Meshing in parallel (D171) then prepared a mip lazily on a worker for a chunk another worker could be preparing too, and an edit two columns away left a far node as it was. **Fixed 2026-09-23 before it shipped**: the mesher says how far it reads (`asset::meshReach`), and the loader prepares and keys every node on exactly that |
| D176 | agent, 2026-09-23 | `streaming_soak` failed once on macOS, leaving nothing but a status line | fixed | Run 35926582319's macOS leg failed `streaming_soak` in 7.7 s; the next run, on the next commit, passed it in 9.1 s, and 55 of 55. ctest ran without `--output-on-failure` and the report was not kept, so which of the soak's checks failed is not known. The likeliest by construction is the hitch check -- any one frame whose streaming time passes 33 ms fails the run, and that is wall-clock time on a shared runner -- but that is an inference, and the gate is not loosened on one. **The instrument is in place** (the commit that made the macOS test step blocking): a failure now prints its output, and the soak's report is kept as the `macos-soak-reports` artifact. Open until a failure is read, or until enough clean runs make the one failure a curiosity. **2026-09-24: twelve clean macOS runs since**, from `4dd970ef` to `b283b507`. The one macOS failure among them was the bash 3.2 `mapfile` in `assert-no-skips.sh`, a later step, not the soak. Still open: twelve runs in one day on one runner pool is a streak, not a rate **2026-09-26: fixed, with D194, because the report finally said what failed.** The failure D194 kept (run 36099847925) was the hitch check: one frame spent 40.4 ms "inside streaming" by the wall clock, in a run whose frames with no streaming at all reached 79 ms, whose median was 0.34 ms and whose p99 was 1.65 ms -- and the streaming pump's local worst is 1.1 ms. The pump never waits (its reads are polled), so wall time beyond its CPU time is the runner keeping the process off a CPU. The hitch check now reads the pump's **thread CPU time** (`platform::threadCpuNs`: `CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID` on POSIX, the thread's cycle count on Windows), and reports both (`worstStreamingMs`, `worstStreamingCpuMs`); streaming that really works 33 ms in a frame still fails it (`soak_tests.cpp`). Locally the two agree to 5 microseconds. |
| D177 | agent, 2026-09-23 | `ParticleEmitter`'s emitted total was replicated and not in the world hash | fixed | Found by the gate S3 asked for: the wire schema's non-property fields and the state `world_hash.cpp` hashes by hand were two lists nothing compared. The gate compares behaviour rather than names -- it changes every such field through the replication module's own `applyField` and requires the world hash to move -- and exactly one field did not: `Emitted`, the count of particles `Emit` has asked for. Two runs whose scripts emitted differently hashed equal. No determinism scenario has an emitter, so no trace had noticed. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: hashed with the rest of the non-property state, and `engine/app/tests/wire_hash_tests.cpp` holds every field the wire carries to the same test; with the fix removed, it fails on that field and no other |
| D178 | agent, 2026-09-23 | A character controller put somewhere new still leaned on what it had touched where it was, so its first step there could be blocked | fixed | Found building the replay a replica corrects its prediction with (ADR 0076, as amended): a character that had walked into a wall, put back where it started and stepped with the same commands, lost the first step's whole horizontal move. Jolt's `CharacterVirtual` resolves an update against the contacts of its last one, and `SetPosition` keeps them -- its own documentation says to refresh them after moving the controller. A script that teleported a character away from a wall hit the same thing. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: `setCharacterTransform` refreshes the contacts at the new position. `world_host_tests.cpp` replays forty ticks of a walk into a wall and a jump from where they started and requires every tick to land where the simulation did; the five determinism traces are unchanged |
| D179 | agent, 2026-09-23 | A replica's own character was never corrected while the authority held it still | fixed | Found with D178. A replica writes only what changed in a snapshot, and its own character's transform was compared with the prediction only on that path -- so an authority that had stopped the character against a wall, sending the same transform every tick, never corrected a prediction that walked on through it. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: every snapshot that answers a newer intent is compared, moved or not. `session_tests.cpp` walks a replica's character into a wall only the authority has and requires it to end at the wall |
| D180 | agent, 2026-09-23 | The open world's trees and towers were never atomic, so a trunk could stream in without its crown | fixed | Found putting the Deep into `examples/10-open-world` (ADR 0086): a `Persistent` model the generator wrote kept its name in the scene and lost its parts to the grid. The generator wrote `StreamingMode = "Enum.StreamingMode.Atomic"`, and a scene names an enum item by its name alone (`"Atomic"`), as every other scene in the repository does. The reader refused the value and counted it, silently, so every tree and tower was `Nonatomic` -- each part filed by its own position, the exact case the generator's comment says it avoids. **Fixed 2026-09-23**: the generator writes item names, and the scene is regenerated. Headless, the Deep's lift now finds its persistent pads |
| D181 | agent, 2026-09-24 | Bloom wrapped round the screen: a bright bottom edge drew a faint band along the top | fixed | Found looking at the first 2D frames (the platformer's ground filled the bottom of the view and a hairline of its glow sat on the top edge, only above the columns that had ground). The bloom chain and the tonemap sampled their targets with the material sampler, whose address mode is `Repeat`, so the downsample's kernel at the coarsest levels -- a large fraction of the screen wide -- reached past one edge and read the opposite one. A 3D frame hid it: the top of the view is usually sky, already bright. **Fixed 2026-09-24**: every bloom and tonemap read is clamped to the edge (`environmentSampler_`) |
| D182 | agent, 2026-09-24 | A windowed frame with about two thousand distinctly tinted parts crashes the engine: the D3D12 descriptor heap is written past its end | fixed | Found benchmarking a small game the owner's friend wrote (SNAKE), under a stress harness that drives the game's own `Snake` module unchanged: eight snakes of 250 segments, each segment a different `Color` override, so every segment is its own material slot and the frame is ~756 draws. **Reproduced 2 of 2 on the Windows package** (`LuauG-1.1.0-win64`, D3D12, RTX 4070 Ti SUPER), exit 2173, exception `0x087D` raised from the D3D12 debug layer. 1000 parts windowed does not crash, and the same 2000 parts headless do not crash either. The debug output, captured from `OutputDebugString`, names it: `ID3D12Device::CopyDescriptorsSimple: Specified CPU descriptor handle ... does not refer to a location in a descriptor heap. DestDescriptorRangeStart is the issue. [EXECUTION ERROR #646: INVALID_DESCRIPTOR_HANDLE]`. **The cause is in the vendored SDL**, `third_party/sdl3/src/gpu/d3d12/SDL_gpu_d3d12.c`, `D3D12_INTERNAL_WriteGPUDescriptors`: the overflow test is `currentDescriptorIndex >= maxDescriptors`, made once BEFORE a group of `resourceHandleCount` descriptors is copied, so a group that starts a few slots short of the end runs past it -- the function carries `// FIXME: need to error on overflow`. The sampler heap is `SAMPLER_GPU_DESCRIPTOR_COUNT` = 2048, which several samplers per draw across ~756 draws reach. **Without the debug layer this is not safe, only silent**: it is a write outside the heap. The fix is a `third_party/patches/` patch that tests `current + count > max` (R13), sent upstream, plus whatever the engine can do to bind fewer samplers per draw. D184 is the load that reaches it and D183 is why it is fatal here. **Fixed 2026-09-24** in `605c2995` (the SDL patch, listed in the manifest; upstream main still had it that day) and `7929c198` (the regression scene). Verified: the windowed 8x250 repro ran three times to the end at 756 draws with `--gpu-debug`. **Why headless 'did not reproduce'**: it did -- unpatched, the same repro died headless in one of one and ran windowed in one of one in the same session. The overflow happens in both; the layer reports it only when the out-of-bounds handle lands outside EVERY descriptor heap, which is the driver's address layout. So the regression scene cannot promise to fail unpatched (ten runs of it did not) and says so; it walks the heap-replacement path the patch added, under the layer, on every gate. Fewer samplers per draw (the forward pass binds thirteen) was measured as not worth a shader change once D184 took the draws from 756 to 4 |
| D183 | agent, 2026-09-24 | Every host, the `player` and `shipping` profiles included, creates its GPU device with the debug layer on | fixed | Found with D182. `engine/app/src/engine.cpp:489` passes `.debug = true` to `createDevice` unconditionally, and has since the frame loop landed (`00a67ce6`, M1); `createSdlGpuDevice` forwards it to `SDL_CreateGPUDevice(requested, desc.debug, nullptr)`. So a game exported by `luaug build` runs with D3D12 validation on wherever the SDK layers are installed, which costs frame time (so every windowed baseline recorded on such a machine is inflated) and turns any validation message into a hard crash, which is how D182 presented. Validation belongs to the `debug` and `dev` profiles, and to an explicit opt-in flag for a player who wants it; never to `player`, `shipping` or `editor` by default. **Fixed 2026-09-24** in `7929c198`: `app::gpuValidationWanted` answers the `debug` and `dev` profiles and `--gpu-debug`, and `launcher_tests.cpp` asserts `player`, `shipping`, `editor` and `profile` never request it. The windowed baselines were re-taken without it (`docs/perf-baselines.md`, "A user's game") |
| D184 | agent, 2026-09-24 | Parts that differ only by a material parameter are never instanced: each distinct `Color` override is its own material slot and its own draw | fixed | Measured with D182's harness: 1009 visible parts drew in 756 draws and 2013 in the same 756, because the snake's gradient gives each segment index its own colour and `extract` deduplicates material slots by (material, every override value). ADR 0090 kept that deduplication on purpose and deferred moving overrides into per-draw data "until a bench says it matters"; this is that bench. `examples/11-ocean`'s README met the same wall from the other side (a distinct colour per tile was 3,629 draws, so it quantised to twelve). The fix is per-instance override data in the instanced stream, so a thousand tints of one material are one run. **Fixed 2026-09-24** in `7929c198`: a material FAMILY (same bind set but for the base colour's rgb), the opaque sort by family, the instancer batching by family with each colour in `GpuInstance.alphaTint`. Draws 756 -> 4 (SNAKE) and 119 -> 17 (11-ocean); the frame time did not move on this machine, and the A/B and the likely reason (a whole-world batch in every shadow cascade) are in the baselines. The command stream of every capture golden is unchanged but for shader sizes |
| D185 | agent, 2026-09-24 | A `luaug.toml` saved with a UTF-8 byte-order mark is rejected whole, and the project silently runs on engine defaults | fixed | Found by accident during D182's benchmark: Windows PowerShell 5.1's `Set-Content -Encoding utf8` writes a BOM, and `engine/core/src/toml.cpp` then fails line 1 with `a line is a table header or key = value`. The host logs one warning and carries on with the engine's own defaults -- so the project's boot scene, window and graphics settings all vanish, and what the person sees is the wrong scene, not an error. Notepad and several Windows editors write the same mark. The reader should skip a leading BOM, as the JSON reader should if it does not already. **Fixed 2026-09-24** in `7a572239`; `toml_tests.cpp` reads a file with the mark, keeps one inside a string, and reads a file that is only the mark as empty. The JSON reader already skipped it |
| D186 | agent, 2026-09-24 | `streaming_soak`'s return check -- the replacement D066 asked for -- failed once under machine load | fixed | Failed in the Linux stage of a full local gate that ran the Windows, Linux and shipping builds together: `[engine.soak.err.return_grew] ... 1153 instances the first time, 1691 the second`. Passed on Windows in the same gate, and passed at once when the Linux stage was re-run alone on the same tree. **One flake, so not quarantined** (MASTER_PROMPT.md section 12 sets that bar at two), recorded so the second is recognised as a second. The likely mechanism is D066's again: the check compares two INSTANTANEOUS resident counts at nearby points of the path, and both are how far materialisation and eviction had got under a millisecond budget -- which load moves. A check load cannot move would compare the settled resident set, or give the streamer the frames it needs to settle before it samples **Fixed (2026-09-25), on the fourth attempt, and the first three are the finding.** One place is not a measurement. `05-streaming` flies an exact circle, and what is resident where a MOVING focus stands depends on how far materialisation trails it -- the millisecond budget again: one failing run held 1194 instances at a place on one lap and 1689 on another, while both quarters of the run averaged 1938. Comparing only idle-streaming frames left none under load; skipping the initial load shifted the strided samples off every exact revisit; and taking the latest early visit rather than the first tie (every phase of an exact circle ties) still failed one Linux run in three under load, because it still read ONE place. Comparing the median of every revisited place then failed once with a CPU burner running alongside -- 1499 against 1675 -- because under that load the world was still arriving across the whole first lap. So the check now reads the SECOND quarter against the fourth, as the growth check always has, pairs every late sample with the latest visit of the same place, and compares the median of one side with the median of the other: lag moves single places either way, a leak lifts every place. `soak_tests.cpp` models both -- a world arriving over the entire first quarter, and a third of a place's instances missing on a pattern with no period -- and each passes, while a twenty per cent leak under the same lag fails. The check gates again; the Windows run reads 1677 against 1677. **And the tolerance is fifteen per cent, not eight** (2026-09-25, later): a full gate on a busy machine moved even the median by 9.4% -- 1464 against 1601, in a run whose late quarter held FEWER instances than its early one. The leak this watches for, instances never freed, grows by the world's whole population every lap, which fifteen per cent does not hide; the unit case that grows by twenty per cent still fails. |
| D187 | owner, 2026-09-24 | A new project from the Starter had two scripts, `main` and `Main` | fixed | The launcher scaffolds from `content/templates`, which the build staged with `cmake -E copy_directory` -- a copy that only adds. The old starter's `src/scripts/main.luau` stayed in the staged tree after the template became a scene holding `Main`, and the package copied the staged tree, so the launcher made both (`luaug new` reads the repository's `templates/` and was right, which is why it looked like the template's fault). **Fixed 2026-09-24**: the staged templates are removed before the copy (`engine/app/CMakeLists.txt`) |
| D188 | owner, 2026-09-24 | Resizing a `TextScaled` label by its handles flooded the console with `The glyph cache filled (90 entries) and was cleared` | fixed | `TextScaled` asked for the exact size that filled the box, so every frame of a drag rasterised the label's glyphs at a new size until the 1024x1024 atlas filled, and every clear logged a line. **Fixed 2026-09-24**: a scaled label draws at `ui::scaledTextSize` -- never larger than fits, on a ladder (1 px to 24, 2 px to 48, 4 px to 100) and capped at 100 px as the reference platform caps scaled text -- and a clear is said on the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th... occurrence with the running count (`glyph_tests.cpp`) |
| D189 | owner, 2026-09-24 | In the script editor, selecting near the top after selecting near the bottom and pressing Enter jumped the view back down | fixed | The completion list stayed open when the caret was moved by a click, keeping the range it was opened on -- far down the file -- and Enter accepted into that range, moving the caret and the view there (and writing the suggestion where nobody was looking). **Fixed 2026-09-24**: any caret move by hand closes the list (`placeCaret`, and every click in the text), and an accept whose caret is no longer at the end of the word it was completing accepts nothing |
| D190 | owner, 2026-09-24 | Two diagnostics on one line drew their messages on top of each other | fixed | The list is the parser's, then the tree lint's, then the type checker's, so one line can appear in it twice and far apart, and each spoke at the end of the line; an unknown global was reported by both the lint and the checker at the same name. **Fixed 2026-09-24**: one message per line, the most serious first with `(+N more)`, an identical mark drawn once, and every message still under the pointer |
| D191 | owner, 2026-09-24 | A `PointLight` or `SpotLight` at night seemed to do nothing | fixed | Two causes. The falloff was physical inverse square, so a light of the default `Brightness` 1 lit about a metre around itself and nothing of its `Range`; and a light under an `Attachment` shone from its part's centre rather than from the attachment. **Fixed 2026-09-24**: the falloff is `1 / (1 + 25 (d/range)^2)`, windowed to zero at the range as before, so `Brightness` is the strength at the source and `Range` shapes the whole curve (the lit goldens move, on purpose); `render::lightAnchorOf` applies the attachment's offset, and the editor draws a selected light's reach -- rings for a point light, the cone for a spot -- from the same answer |
| D192 | agent, 2026-09-25 | `luaug check` runs `luau-lsp` on the old solver, so R2's "new solver" has not been what the gate checks | fixed | Found moving the editor to the new solver: `tools/cli/commands/check.luau` runs `luau-lsp analyze --platform=standard` with no `--flag:LuauSolverV2=true`, and a type only the new solver knows (`index<>`) was "unknown" there. Turning the flag on is not a one-line fix: the engine definitions' `Instance.new` intersection is "code too complex" to the new solver in `luau-lsp` as well, so every `Instance.new("Part")` in the repository would become an `Instance`. The editor solved it with a magic function (ADR 0093's amendment); the command line needs either the same inside a checker the repository controls, or definitions the new solver accepts. Until then the gate's type check is the old solver's **Fixed (2026-09-25).** `luaug check` runs `luau-lsp` with `--flag:LuauSolverV2=true`, and gives it a copy of the definitions it can read: `Instance.new`'s intersection rewritten as a table from name to class read through `index<>` over a name constrained to `keyof<>` of it, so `Instance.new("Part")` is a `Part`, assigning it to a `Model` is an error, and an unknown name is one too. A copy, because the old solver cannot read `index<>` at all and an editor extension on its default still runs it; the engine's checker keeps its magic function. Turning it on found sixty diagnostics in sixteen files the old solver had passed, among them one real bug: `luaug new`'s default folder was lute's `Path` object where a string was promised. |
| D193 | agent, 2026-09-25 | A windowless run's frame time collapses under CPU contention -- worst frames of 45 to 212 ms where the same scene in a foreground window stays under 17 -- because a headless process gets none of the foreground scheduling boost | fixed | Found comparing a city scene against Godot 4.7.2 on the same machine (i5-14600K, 6 P-cores and 8 E-cores; the harness is outside the repository, at the owner's `luaug-playground/benchmark`). Our side ran `--headless` (the GPU renders, nothing is presented) and Godot ran in a window with `--fixed-fps 60`, both one frame per tick. With other sessions building on the machine, our p99 swung between 5 and 23 ms from one session to the next, while Godot's held at about 3.2 ms. **Bisected rather than guessed.** With `--rhi=null` the spikes stay, so they are CPU-side. Temporary per-phase timers showed the physics step and `render::extract` slowing by about 10x in the SAME frames (6.7 and 8.4 ms, against medians of 0.55 and 0.76); `extract` is single-threaded, so the main thread itself was being descheduled. Pinning the process to the E-cores reproduces the bad sessions exactly: median 6.2 ms, p99 19.2, worst 54. Under a deliberate load of 16 spinning processes: plain priority gives median 5.2, p99 8-9, worst 45-91; turning EcoQoS off makes it WORSE (p99 17-115, worst 129-212), so that hypothesis is refuted; Godot in its foreground window gives median 3.3, p99 4.5, worst 5-17; and **our same binary at `AboveNormal` priority gives median 5.2, p99 6.0, worst 7-9.5**. So the tails are not in the simulation or the renderer: they come from a windowless process competing at plain priority, where a foreground window is boosted by Windows. Two consequences. **Every headless timing this repository records** (benches, soaks, `--frame-stats` baselines) is measured without that boost, so a busy machine inflates it: raise the priority of the measured process, or say that it was not raised. And **a windowless `--serve` authority on a shared machine** would stutter the same way. The candidate fix is for the host to raise its own process to `ABOVE_NORMAL_PRIORITY_CLASS` when it is headless and serving or measuring, which is not decided here. **Separately, and not a defect:** the median gap is real. The quiet machine gives 3.3 ms for us and 2.1-2.3 for Godot; under load it is 5.2 against 3.3. Of our CPU frame (2.4 ms with `--rhi=null`), physics takes about 0.55 ms and `extract` about 0.76, rebuilding all 4879 visible objects every frame although 4400 of them never move **Fixed 2026-09-25**: `platform::raiseProcessPriority` puts the process at `ABOVE_NORMAL_PRIORITY_CLASS` on Windows, and returns false and changes nothing elsewhere, where there is no foreground boost for it to stand in for. The host calls it when it is headless AND serving (`--host`, `--serve`) or measuring (`--frame-stats`, `--soak-report`), and `--bench` always calls it; a windowed game, a test run and a capture never do. It logs `engine.info.priority_raised`, so a baseline says which way it was measured. `platform_tests.cpp` asserts the answer per platform and that the class really changed on Windows. |
| D194 | agent, 2026-09-25 | macOS CI: one frame of `streaming_soak` spent 40 ms inside streaming against the 33 ms hitch limit | fixed | `build-macos` on the push of `edc2046b` (ADR 0096 Stage 1, which touches no streaming): `[engine.soak.err.hitches] 1 frame(s) spent longer than 33 ms inside streaming; the worst spent 40.44 ms`. The re-run of the same job passed. A shared CI runner's one slow frame, most likely -- **one flake, so not quarantined** (MASTER_PROMPT.md section 12 sets that bar at two), recorded so a second is recognised as a second. **2026-09-26: fixed** -- see D176: the check counted a runner's pause as streaming, and now counts the pump's CPU time. |
| D195 | owner, 2026-09-25 | Ambient occlusion drew blocky stripes on the ground under a floating box's edge | fixed | Reported as a shadow bug with a picture of a box whose shadow stood apart from it. The detached shadow was right -- the box floated, which a render of the same box resting on the ground and of one 0.8 m above it showed -- but the render of the floating one found a real defect beside it: a band of 4-pixel dark blocks along the ground just under the box's bottom edge, gone with `--no-ambient-occlusion` and unchanged with shadows off. The occlusion pass reconstructed its normal with `ddx`/`ddy` of the position, and those differentiate across a 2x2 quad: a ground pixel whose quad held one pixel of the box got a normal across the depth jump, tilted into the ground, and a dark block. The shader now reads both neighbours on each axis, one of its own pixels apart, and keeps the one whose depth jumps least -- the same surface. A first version stepped one FULL-resolution texel and striped a face seen edge-on with the depth buffer's steps; stepping this pass's pixel keeps flat faces as they were. Every command-stream golden moves by the one line of `ssao`'s byte size, and the two GPU goldens that use occlusion (`contact-0930`, `specular-frame120`) differ only along silhouettes and contacts, mean 1.1 of 255 on the spheres. |
| D196 | owner, 2026-09-25 | A script edited during play ran its old text on the next play | fixed | The tab writes its text into the world as it is typed, and during play that world is the playing one -- which stop replaces with the snapshot play took. The tab kept the new text and the world went back to the old, so the next play ran what was no longer on the screen. Stop now writes every scene tab's text back into the restored world, where it differs. |
| D197 | owner, 2026-09-25 | Enter after the `then` of an `if` that already had its `end` wrote a second one | fixed | The automatic `end` asked whether the WHOLE file balanced -- `function`, `if`, `do` and `repeat` against `end` and `until` -- so any block open anywhere, a function still being written, made every Enter after a `then` write a closer, and an `if` expression (`local x = if a then b else c`), which has no `end`, left every file with one open for good. It now asks about the block itself: from the caret it nests to the closer that matches this line's opener, skips `if` expressions, and counts the block closed when that closer is indented at least as deep as the line -- one indented less is an outer block's, reached because this one never closed. `else` and `elseif` are inside the block and change nothing. |
| D198 | owner, 2026-09-25 | A frame reparented into another kept its old place and size until one of its properties was written | fixed | A screen re-runs its layout only when marked dirty, and only a layout-affecting property write marked it: a move is not a write. `World::setParent` now marks the screen the instance left and the one it joined -- found from the instance itself, so a `ScreenGui` that moves marks its own tree -- and destroying goes through it as a move to nil. |
| D199 | owner, 2026-09-25 | A friend's UI buttons did not answer when the game was played in the editor | fixed | His project (`my-game`): a `TextButton` with no text inside a rounded `Frame`, `Activated` connected to a `print`. Nothing in the scene was wrong, and the hit test was not: a button's whole box takes the click, with or without an image, text or drawn background, as a new case in `interaction_tests.cpp` holds for `ImageButton` with and without an image and for his button. What was wrong was the pointer. In play inside the editor the game is drawn into the viewport panel and its UI laid out against that panel's size, but the pointer the game read was in WINDOW pixels -- every click landed by the panel's offset away from where it was aimed. The game's pointer events are now moved into the viewport's pixels before the game reads them (`toViewportEvents`), which the UI, the canvases in the world and scripts reading the pointer all go through. |
| D200 | owner, 2026-09-25 | Textures imported after a script had asked for them showed only once the engine was reopened | fixed | A friend pasted `examples/14-voxels`' script, played it before its textures were in the project, then imported them: the blocks stayed untextured until a restart. The loader remembers a texture it could not find, so it is not read and warned about every frame -- and it remembered it for the life of the process. The files arrived and nothing asked again. It is asked again now when the content is read again (an import, a refresh, a folder made -- `ContentTree::refreshes`) and when play starts, which is where somebody who copied files in by hand will look next. |
| D201 | CI, 2026-09-26 | The hot-reload end-to-end test failed on a loaded Linux runner with `eq: 63 ~= 60` | fixed | The test asks a running dev session for the world hash at tick 60, one second of wall clock after the engine starts, and the engine answers a tick already past at the tick it is on. On `ced311dd` -- a documentation-only commit -- the request arrived three ticks late and the sample came back from tick 63. A race with the clock, not a defect in reload: the sample tick is 240 now, four seconds ahead, which keeps the hash's meaning and gives a slow runner margin. Answering a past tick exactly would need a per-tick hash history in every dev session, which costs a world hash a tick for a test's convenience. |
| D202 | CI, 2026-09-26 | The nightly Android build failed: `surface_shader.cpp` called `std::from_chars` for a float | fixed | Run 36232926755, `Triangle APK`: `call to deleted function 'from_chars'`. A surface shader's `LUAUG_PARAM` default (ADR 0091) was parsed with the floating-point overload, which the NDK's libc++ does not have -- the exact reason `core/number_parse.h` existed, private to `core` until then. It is public now and the reflection uses it. Reproduced locally with the pinned NDK (installed by the new `scripts/install-android.ps1`) before the fix and built after it; `scripts/localgate.ps1` gained an `android` stage that cross-builds arm64 on every run where the NDK is installed, so the next one is found before a push rather than the morning after |
| D203 | agent, 2026-09-26 | A service's settings were lost when nothing was under it, and opening another scene kept the last one's | fixed | Found while designing scene switching (ADR 0106), by reading the writer: a scene wrote a service only when `holdsAuthored` said something was inside it, so `Lighting.ClockTime` or `UIService.ScreenOrientation` set in the Properties panel on an empty service was gone at the next save -- no example had noticed because every one sets its lighting from a script. And `clearScene` emptied the services but left their settings, so a scene that said nothing about one opened with whatever the scene before had left. A service is written now when a setting differs from a freshly made one or it has an attribute, and a new scene puts every setting back to the engine's first. The IDL's `Transient` had never reached C++; it does now, the writer skips it, and the overlay and pointer states are marked with it, so a scene saved with F3 open is the file it was. |
| D204 | owner, 2026-09-28 | A `TextInput` in an exported game showed its caret and never received a letter | fixed | SDL3 delivers no text until something calls `SDL_StartTextInput`, and nothing did: `platform::setTextInputEnabled` existed with no caller, and the editor only worked because its ImGui asked for its own fields. `app::TextInputFocus` switches it when a game box takes or loses focus -- on a change only, so an editor field that has the keyboard keeps it -- and on Android the same call raises the on-screen keyboard. Tests: `text_input_focus_tests.cpp` and a platform case that the switch reaches the window. |
| D205 | owner, 2026-09-28 | A character with a non-default `WalkSpeed` was pulled back every snapshot on a client | fixed | Only `VerticalVelocity`, `Grounded` and `State` replicated, and a replica skipped every `CharacterBody` field of its own character, so a server that set `WalkSpeed = 24` left the client predicting at 16: 59 corrections in 60 ticks in the new case in `session_tests.cpp`. `WalkSpeed`, `JumpSpeed`, `MaxSlopeAngle` and `AutoStepHeight` replicate (protocol 20), the replica keeps only the motion state its own, and a replay steps the unanswered commands at the authority's speeds. After: no correction at 24, and a change to 30 mid-run settles. |
| D206 | owner, 2026-09-28 | Reading `.Parent` of a destroyed instance raises instead of answering nil | fixed | Divergence #25 as designed: a dead handle raised `script.err.instance_dead` on any access, so a game's `if part.Parent then` -- the way it asks whether something is still in the world -- threw instead. Brought to the owner as an API decision, who chose on 2026-09-28 that `.Parent` alone reads nil; every other access still raises. A new case in `instance_destroy.spec.luau` failed before and passes after; api-design.md, migrating.md and the manual say so. |
| D207 | owner, 2026-09-28 | A script's `NetworkService:Join()` after leaving made the player a stranger | fixed | The command line's redial kept the player's token; a script's `Join` began a session with none, so the server gave a new `UserId` (the new case in `network_session_tests.cpp`: `joined:2 user:3`). `NetworkSession` keeps the token by `address:port` for the life of the process and presents it; the multiplayer guide says so. |
| D208 | owner, 2026-09-28 | A server that vanished took up to thirty seconds to notice | fixed | ENet's own default timeout (5 to 30 s). `TransportConfig::timeoutMs`, ten seconds by default and `[network] timeout` in `project.toml`, is applied to every peer both ways with `enet_peer_timeout`. Test: a closed server is gone within four seconds at a one-second limit, which ENet's five-second minimum could not do. |
| D209 | owner, 2026-09-28 | A headless client back in solo logged "Frame took 18446440048196 ms" | fixed | Leaving the network sent the frame loop from the real clock, days into uptime, back to the synthetic one, which counts from zero; `now - last` wrapped. Time that goes backwards is now no time, and the clock choice no longer goes back (D213). Test: `frame_scheduler_tests.cpp`, failing with a 1.8e10 s frame before. |
| D210 | owner, 2026-09-28 | `Enum.KeyCode` had no punctuation and no keypad | fixed | A comma, a period or the keypad arrived as `Unknown`. 33 keys -- punctuation, Insert, PageUp, PageDown, CapsLock, NumLock and the keypad -- are appended at the END of the enum (103 to 135), so no value a game held moved; `platform::Key` carries them after `Delete` and the resolver maps them as a second keyboard block. The keypad's Enter submits a text box as Return does. |
| D211 | owner, 2026-09-28 | `ludwerk check` and `ludwerk fmt` walked into `dist/` | fixed | The ignore list matched substrings and did not name `dist`, and StyLua was given the whole folder. Folders are matched by whole name now, `dist` is ignored, and StyLua is given the same exclusions as globs. Test: `check.test.luau`. |
| D212 | owner, 2026-09-28 | `ludwerk check` announced the engine's definitions under another platform's name | fixed | An unnamed `--definitions` file takes the analyser's default name. Each is named after its file now (`@engine`, `@scene`), and the repository's editor settings name theirs the same way. |
| D213 | owner, 2026-09-28 | A headless client back in solo ran frames without waiting for the clock | fixed | The same switch as D209: back on the synthetic clock it ran flat out. Decided: a headless process that has had real time -- a network session or a dev session -- keeps it (`app::FrameClock`); only a run that never had it, a golden capture, is synthetic. |
| D214 | agent, 2026-09-28 | The hot-reload end-to-end test failed again on a loaded machine, with `eq: 241 ~= 240` | fixed | The second failure of the check D201 moved to tick 240, and D201's reading -- a request that arrived late -- was not the cause: one tick past is a frame that ran two. The engine answered a `sample` once a frame, after all of its ticks, so a loaded machine that went from 239 to 241 in one frame answered from 241. A sample is answered after each tick now, and only a tick already past when the request arrived is answered with a later one. |
| D215 | agent, 2026-09-28 | `TextInput.FocusLost`'s `submitted` never arrived, so Enter could not submit a field | fixed | Found re-exporting the owner's multiplayer test after D204: typing worked, Enter left the field and the game did not join, because the event was raised with no arguments -- its own doc said so. A change kind that carries one boolean (`InstanceEventBool`) delivers it: true for Return, false for a press elsewhere. Tests: the interaction push in `interaction_tests.cpp`, and a handler receiving both in `text_input_focus_tests.cpp`. |
| D216 | agent, 2026-09-28 | A focused, empty `TextInput` drew its placeholder with the caret in the middle of it | fixed | Seen in the same run: the draw showed the placeholder whenever the text was empty, focused or not, and a field with no placeholder returned before its caret was drawn. A focused, empty field draws only the caret now. Test: `layout_tests.cpp`. |
| D217 | agent, 2026-09-28 | A player who left a match from a script stayed on the server until the timeout | fixed | Found re-exporting the owner's test: `NetworkService:Disconnect()` closes the transport, and ENet's `close` reset its peers without a word, so the server went on holding the player -- a statue for everybody else -- until its timeout, which was up to thirty seconds before D208, and a rejoin showed as a leave and a return in the same instant. The memory transport tells its peers on close, which is why no test saw it. `close` sends the notice with `enet_peer_disconnect_now`, which needs no round trip. Test: `transport_tests.cpp`, the server told within half a second; the D208 case now goes silent rather than closing, so it still measures the timeout. |
| D218 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A remote message with a NaN table key terminated the process (audit N1) | fixed | Luau raises on a NaN key and the decoder called `lua_rawset` with anything but nil; Luau is built with C++ exceptions and nothing caught one, so any peer's message ended the server. The decoder refuses a NaN number key and a vector key with a NaN in it as a malformed payload, every decode of foreign bytes runs under `lua_pcall` on a thread of its own (the destination may be a parked coroutine), and the frame loop catches what still escapes a tick and logs it. Test: `service_tests.cpp`, both keys dropped with the stack as it was, a NaN value still arriving. |
| D219 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A malformed `.lmesh` animation channel or skin index corrupted memory on first playback (audit F1, F15) | fixed | `decodeMesh` read a channel's stride, its values and its joint without checking them, and the sampler wrote a stride's worth of floats into a four-float buffer; skin joint indices were never compared with the skeleton. The decoder now refuses a stride other than the target's (4 for rotation, 3 otherwise), fewer values than keys times stride, a channel on a joint the skeleton lacks, and a skin index that is not finite or not below the joint count; the sampler refuses the same shapes for a channel built in memory. Test: `mesh_format_tests.cpp`, each of the five refused, the valid mesh still decoding. |
| D220 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A path from a script or a content file could leave the project: `LoadScene("../../x")`, `require` with a backslash, a glTF naming `C:/...` or `//host/...`, an `asset://` URN with a drive (audit F2, F3, F4, F5) | fixed | Reproduced with `LoadScene("../../outside.scene.json")`, which loaded a scene from outside the project. Every such path now goes through one function, `core::safeRelativePath` / `resolveUnder`: no root name, drive, share, backslash, `:`, control character or Windows device name, and no `..` above the root. Its callers: `require`, `LoadScene` and `LoadSceneAsync` (a keyed error at the call, and the host refuses too, for an authority's scene a replica follows), the scene and stamp readers of the game, the editor and the partitioner, `ContentProvider`, `isValidUrn` (now canonical as well), the mesh and sky loaders' fallback (no more `root / raw string`), a glTF's companion files -- checked in a first pass before anything is read, under the model's folder and no larger than the buffer's declared length, images capped at 256 MiB -- and the import copy of those files. The mesh loader's tests named maps by absolute path, the hole itself, and now name them under a content root. Tests: `content_path_tests.cpp`, the F5 case in `world_host_tests.cpp`, the F2 case in `gltf_tests.cpp`, the F3 case in `content_tree_tests.cpp`. |
| D221 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A function that spawned itself overflowed the native stack and killed the process (audit S4) | fixed | Reproduced: `local function again() task.spawn(again) end again()` ended `engine-host` with a segmentation fault. Every `lua_resume` restarts Luau's C-call count, so resumes nested inside one another -- `task.spawn`, a thread resumed at once, a `require` chain -- had no bound but the stack. The signal system counts the resumes running now; past 100 the resume does not happen and the thread that asked is told (`script.err.resume_too_deep`); a `require` raises it at the call. Test: `task_tests.cpp`, the chain stopping near 100 and a second chain starting from nothing. |
| D222 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A script that never yielded froze the game, the server or the editor for ever (audit S5) | fixed | Reproduced: `task.spawn(function() while true do end end)` held `engine-host` until it was killed from outside. The VM's interrupt now checks, every 256 safepoints, how long the outermost resume running has run, and past the limit raises `script.err.script_timeout` in the thread running -- the game goes on without it. The owner's limits (2026-09-28): where a person is testing (editor, `dev`, a match's windows) a hitch past 10 ms is warned about, at most once a second, and a thread is stopped at 1 s; in an exported game nothing is warned and the limit is 5 s. Test: `task_tests.cpp`, the loop stopped and the script after it running. |
| D223 | audit, 2026-09-28 | The script heap had no cap (audit S8) | fixed | `luaL_newstate` ran on the default allocator, so a table growing without end took the machine's memory. The VM runs on a counting allocator; an allocation past `[script] max_memory_mb` (the owner's defaults: 1024 on a computer, 512 on Android) fails, which Luau makes `LUA_ERRMEM` in the thread that asked, reported as `script.err.out_of_memory` -- and that thread's stack is cleared and the heap collected at once, or the next allocation, anybody's, failed too. Test: `task_tests.cpp`, a hoarding thread stopped at an 8 MiB margin and the VM running a script after it. |
| D224 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A live instance could be parented under a destroyed one, and its links outlived the parent (audit E1, S9) | fixed | `setParent` refused a retired parent and accepted one destroyed and not yet retired; after retirement the child kept its parent, sibling and same-name links, because unlinking returned early when the parent was gone, and the next same-name walk read a retired record. A destroyed parent is refused now (`scene.err.parent_locked`, the lock a destroyed instance's own Parent already has); unlinking clears the child's links even with its parent gone; and retiring a record unparents any live child of it first. Test: `world_tests.cpp`, the parent refused and the tree readable after the retirement. |
| D225 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A terrain raycast with an infinite or huge direction never ended, or took seconds (audit E2) | fixed | Reproduced: `workspace:Raycast(origin, vector.create(0, 1e9, 0))` over a small terrain held the game for 12.7 s -- in C++, where the watchdog cannot reach -- and an infinite reach made the march's step NaN and the loop endless. A non-finite origin, direction or reach is no hit; and only the stretch of the ray inside the field's box (its chunks', a voxel wider) is marched, so a ray that misses the field misses at once. The same ray takes 0.1 s now. Test: `terrain_tests.cpp`, an endless ray, a 1e12 one, a NaN direction, a 1e6 one still finding the ground, and one past the field's edge. |
| D226 | audit, 2026-09-28 | NaN and infinity reached the simulation from scripts and from peers, and a peer's action names grew the atom table (audit E3, and the network items beside N1) | fixed | Reproduced: `part.CFrame = CFrame.new(0/0, 0, 0)` was accepted and the part's position read `nan`; `ApplyImpulse(math.huge, ...)` was accepted. One rule, `core/finite.h`, for every number that reaches the simulation: the `CFrame`, `Position`, `Orientation` and `Size` setters, a weld's `C0`/`C1`, an attachment's `CFrame`, `ApplyImpulse` and `CharacterBody:Move` refuse a non-finite value (a keyed error at the call), and Jolt's shape builder holds a size it is still given within finite bounds, NaN-safely. From a peer: an owned part's state is taken only when finite and within the world's bounds, with speeds clamped; an intent's axis is made finite and bounded, its type checked, and its action name only looked up -- `GetIntent` interns the name, so a server's own code is what makes a name arrive. Tests: `instance_binding_tests.cpp`; `session_tests.cpp`, a NaN position and an infinite speed not taken, an invented action name not interned and a NaN axis made 0. |
| D227 | agent, 2026-09-28 | A replica that rejoined its authority destroyed its own `Workspace` | fixed | Found by D224's refusal: the rejoin test's world came back with nothing under `Workspace`. `resetForRejoin` destroyed every replicated instance it had mapped, and the `Workspace` has been on the wire since protocol 19 (for its wind) -- so every instance of the rejoined world had been parented under a destroyed service, the stale-link hazard audit E1 describes. A rejoin leaves services alone: they are this machine's own, mapped and never spawned. Test: `session_tests.cpp`, the rejoin with the world whole and the `Workspace` alive. |
| D228 | audit, 2026-09-28 | The editor ran a `tools/cli/main.luau` found in the open project's own folders (audit T1) | fixed | `locateCli` climbed from the project root and ran the first CLI it met with Lute, unsandboxed, as the Export window opened -- so a downloaded project carrying one ran it. It now looks beside the installed editor, then in the repository the build itself came from (`ENG_SOURCE_DIR`, in the builds with an editor) and the host's own ancestors; never above a project. Test: `export_runner_tests.cpp`, a CLI planted in a project not found, and the named repository's found. |
| D229 | audit, 2026-09-28 | Choosing a feed in `26-security-cameras` could crash in `D3D12_BindFragmentSamplers` (the two crash reports the audit found in the example's folder) | fixed | The example raises a `CameraTexture`'s `Resolution` when a feed is chosen, and `ViewHost` remakes the view's texture at the new size, destroying the old one. The UI's picture table -- which the frame hands the renderer -- took the new handle only at `UiText::sync`, after the renderer had drawn with the destroyed one; SDL then dereferenced the null the RHI passed it for a handle naming nothing. The table follows a view's texture the moment it is asked for, `refreshViews` renews every view picture right after the views are made (for a UI laid out once), and the RHI never hands SDL a null: a texture or sampler that no longer exists is bound as a 1x1 placeholder and said once (`rhi.err.stale_binding`). Test: `ui_text_tests.cpp`, a view remade twice -- once asked for, once not -- and one that stopped drawing. The two reports and the example's stray `engine.log` were deleted. |
| D230 | audit, 2026-09-28 | Seven bytes from an authority crashed a replica; a snapshot, a despawn and an attribute message cost their size squared; a spawn of an unknown class interned its name for ever (audit N1's network review, replica side) | fixed | Reproduced by a test that is the server and writes the bytes itself (`session_tests.cpp`, "a hostile authority's message is refused whole"): a `SceneChange` claiming 0xFFFFFFFF bytes reserved four gigabytes from the claim before reading one, and a failed allocation ends the process; a spawn of `NoSuchClassFromAPeer` left the name in the atom table; a snapshot with its records out of order was built and then failed its checksum. A message's lengths are now checked against the bytes it has before anything is sized from them (`SceneChange`, `TilemapBlocks`); snapshot records must ascend, as the authority writes them, and new ones are merged once instead of inserted one at a time; a despawn forgets all its ids with one copy of each remembered state; departed ids leave the filter after `DepartedMemoryTicks`; attribute names are matched through a set; class names are looked up, never interned; and the replica bounds its names, instances, roster and `RemoteEvent` messages a tick. The wire format is unchanged, so the protocol version is too. |
| D231 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A peer could make an authority parse without end, take half an owned state, hold a slot without a hello, and grow its memory (audit N1's network review, authority side) | fixed | Reproduced (`session_tests.cpp`, "a peer's intents and owned states are bounded a tick" and "a connection that never says hello is let go"): ten intents in one tick were all parsed and the last taken, and one of 300 entries was accepted; an owned state cut short in its second record moved the part by its first and made the whole one after it, at the same tick, look old; a skewed rotation was taken as one; a connection that never sent a hello held its slot for 600 receives and more; a `RemoteEvent` message naming 1025 instances was delivered. Now: two intents and two owned states a peer a tick, at most 256 entries and 4096 parts; an owned state is read whole, then applied, and its tick taken only then; a rotation must be one (`core::isRotation`); `MaxRemoteRefs` and a megabyte of `RemoteEvent` payload a peer a tick; a peer not welcomed in `MaxUnwelcomedReceives` is let go; the players remembered across connections are bounded (`MaxKnownIdentities`, the longest-known one not connected forgotten); the messages held for a peer are aged by when each was held and bounded in count and bytes; a peer's network id is found through an index, not a walk of the capture. ENet on an authority takes a message of at most a megabyte and eight connections from one address, and one `poll` hands up at most 8192 events or 64 MiB. |
| D232 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A chunk size of sixteen hex digits wrapped the HTTP client's bounds, and a server sending a byte at a time held a request past its timeout (audit N1's network review) | fixed | Reproduced (`http_tests.cpp`): `ffffffffffffffff` as a chunk size passed both checks because they were sums, moved the cursor back, and the response came back "whole" with bytes read twice; a server writing one byte every 100 ms kept an 800 ms request open for three seconds, because the timeout was a read's while `HttpRequest` promised it was the request's. The bounds now compare against what is left, and the socket and OpenSSL clients give each read what is left of the request's deadline; WinHTTP and Android, whose timeouts are their stacks' own, check it between reads. |
| D233 | audit, 2026-09-28 | Every `task.defer`, and every resumption of a thread that parked again, kept a registry reference for the VM's life (audit S2, S3) | fixed | Reproduced (`task_tests.cpp`, "a deferred callback and a thread that parks again let go of their references"): 500 `task.defer` calls left 504 threads in the registry, and `while true do task.wait() end` added one a tick -- 300 in 300 ticks. The deferred queue never released its entry's reference, and every resume site -- `task.spawn`, the timers, `WaitForChild`, the save and preload waiters, the HTTP and `RemoteFunction` answers, `Scene:SendMessage` -- released only when the thread finished, while a thread that parks again takes a reference of its own where it waits. Each now lets go after the resume, whatever the thread did. A `BindToClose` handler and a `RemoteFunction` handler that park are still held on purpose, where the host waits for them. |
| D234 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A key built at run time grew the world's name table for ever, and once 32767 had been used a chunk loaded later could not name a single member (audit S6, S11) | fixed | Reproduced (`instance_binding_tests.cpp`, "a script's strings do not grow the name table"): after `workspace["junk" .. index]` for 40000 keys, `junk20000` was a name in the world's table, and a chunk loaded afterwards failed on `folder.Parent` with "Folder has no member named Parent". Luau asks for an atom when a string first names a member and when a chunk loads its field names; the callback interned every such string into the world's append-only table and gave each string object a slot of its own. It now looks the text up -- a string naming nothing the world has is no atom -- and gives a name one slot however many strings carry it; `resolve` looks a name up by its text when a string has no atom, so a name made later, or one past the last slot, is still found. `RemoveTag` looks up rather than interns. |
| D235 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A call that waits, made where nothing can wait, left its waiter registered, and the thread was resumed later wherever it had gone on to (audit S7) | fixed | Reproduced (`task_tests.cpp`, "a wait where nothing can yield raises and leaves nothing registered"): a `task.wait()` inside an `__index` metamethod failed under `pcall`, and its timer then ended the script's next `task.wait(0.5)` after two ticks. Every call that waits -- `task.wait`, `Signal:Wait`, `WaitForChild`, `LoadAreaAsync`, `InvokeServer`, `net.request` -- now raises `script.err.cannot_yield` before it registers anything or sends anything; `SaveAsync`, `GetSlotAsync` and `PreloadAsync`, which already answered the main thread at once, answer such a caller the same way. |
| D236 | audit, 2026-09-28 | A terrain brush of any size was walked voxel by voxel, and a huge coordinate was converted to an integer with undefined behaviour (audit S10) | fixed | Reproduced with the release package: `terrain:FillBall(vector.create(0, 0, 0), 1e5, 1)` held `engine-host` for more than 30 s, in C++ where the watchdog does not reach, growing towards the machine's memory.
