Run on the game: 10.6 MB with its terrain and its 235 models, where the build before wrote 53 KB. | Two tools held "the whole world" by two routes, and only one of them knew about streaming. |
| D426 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: `Terrain:HeightAt` is nil until the ground streams in, so scripts at boot read no height anywhere, and there was nothing to wait on (P1) | `StreamingService:LoadAreaAsync` waits for an area's cells -- the parts' cells. The ground streams by a streamer of its own, which the wait never asked. | fixed | The wait is for both (`FieldStreamer::areaResident`), and `HeightAt`'s documentation says where the nil comes from and how to wait. | A second streamer was added beside the first, and the one call that means "is it here yet" went on asking only the first. |
| D427 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: `ColorCorrectionEffect.Contrast` crushes the dark half of a picture -- on a night frame +0.12 took a sky of sRGB (9, 30, 64) to (1, 1, 41) and +0.5 to (1, 1, 1), and grass lost its blue channel and went neon (P1) | Contrast was a line through the pivot in linear light: everything darker than a share of the pivot came out below zero and was clamped, a channel at a time, so hues shifted on the way to black. | fixed | Contrast is a power about the pivot, `pivot * (c / pivot) ^ (1 + Contrast)`, which never leaves the positive and keeps the channels in their order. A power does not fold into a sum, so a grade is a list of stages (`GradeStage`, eight a frame) the tonemap applies in order; an effect at its defaults is no stage and the frame draws through the plain tonemap. A 3D lookup instead of stages is the next step when a user's own table is wanted; the arithmetic is the same and `render::applyGrade` holds it. Tests: `look_tests.cpp`, "D427: contrast darkens the dark half without taking it to black". | "It folds into one multiply" was the reason for the shape, and the shape was wrong for the operation. |
| D428 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: with a `DepthOfFieldEffect`, a pane of glass at the focus distance was smeared with the far hills behind it, and flames were sharp over the floor and smeared over the sky (P1) | What blends writes no depth, so the focus pass read the depth of what was behind it and blurred it as that. | fixed | With a depth of field the lens runs inside the forward pass, after the opaque world and the air and before what blends: glass, particles and the interface in the world are drawn over the focused picture. Pictured: panes at the focus distance sharp over a blurred sky. | The order of two passes was right for each alone and wrong for the pair. |
| D429 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: an emitter of 3000 a second living two seconds drew clumps and gaps where a column was asked for (P1) | That many would be six thousand alive at once and an emitter holds 4096: it filled, stopped being born, and began again as the first ones died. | fixed | The stream is born at what the cap sustains (`ParticleSystem::effectiveRate`), evenly, and the log says so once for the emitter. Test: `particles_tests.cpp`. | A cap that refuses is a cap that pulses; one that thins is one nobody notices. |
| D430 | the owner on his phone, 2026-10-02: a touch never presses the interface -- no button's `Activated` fires and no `TextInput` takes focus, so the keyboard never opens; the same taps reach `InputService.InputBegan` as touches (P0) | The interface took a press from the frame's events and its PLACE from the input system's pointer. That pointer follows the mouse, and deliberately not the mouse events the system makes out of a finger (`Event::fromTouch`), since the finger has already arrived as a finger -- so on a phone it never left the corner it began in, and every tap was a press on nothing at the top left. Being "a press that lands anywhere else", it also took the focus from whatever had it. | fixed | **A press carries its own place** (`app::UiPointer`): the interface's pointer is wherever the last event that moved or pressed it said -- a mouse's, one made of a finger, or the first finger's own -- and the input system's only before any event has. The canvases in the world are pressed by the same pointer. Tests: `ui_pointer_tests.cpp` -- a finger and the mouse made of it, at a button's middle and at a field's: `Activated` fires, the field focuses, and the mouse's own pointer has not moved. | The manual said "a tap also presses the interface's buttons as a click does" and nothing checked it; two readers of one event took its two halves from two places. |
| D431 | the owner on his phone, 2026-10-02: `NetworkService:Join` fails with "the transport could not open on port 0; the port may already be in use" -- no game can join or host from a phone (P0) | An exported APK declared no permission at all, and Android refuses every socket to an app without `INTERNET`. The message named the one cause that could not be it: with no port asked for there is none to be in use. | fixed | The player's manifest always declares `INTERNET` and `ACCESS_NETWORK_STATE` -- normal permissions, nobody is asked -- since joining, hosting and HTTPS are core services. A socket the system refuses outright says so (`net.err.transport_socket_failed`), and names the permission. Test: the Android export test reads the built APK's permissions with `aapt2`. | The export was checked for being signed and versioned, and not for being able to do what the engine's own services need. |
| D432 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: a server that restarts strands every client for ever -- each stays "connected" to a world nobody decides, redialling a server that no longer knows it (P0) | A client that lost its server dialled again and said nothing: `State` stayed `Connected`, no signal fired, and the dial had no end. And the server that answered, being a new run, did not know the token shown -- so it welcomed a NEW player, while the client took the welcome for its old seat and kept the old world's copies. | fixed | A lost connection is `Connecting` while it is dialled again; it ends after `[network] timeout` with `Disconnected` and the machine solo. A welcome whose token is not the one shown is a fresh join (`Status::freshJoins`, counted by the replica; the protocol is unchanged): the world is replaced, the scene's client code starts again, and `Connected` fires -- as it does on every return. A machine that goes solo with no scene drops the server's instances. The first dial of `--join` still waits for ever (ADR 0085). Tests: `network_session_tests.cpp`, the four "D432:" and "D433:" cases. | A reconnect was tested against the server it had left, which is the one case where "say nothing" is right. |
| D433 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: client scripts do not start again when a join replaces the world; with `--join` they run before the join and see `LocalPlayer.UserId` 0 (P1) | `ClientScriptService`'s scripts started once, at boot, in the scene the package carried, and a join replaced that world under them: their variables named instances that were gone and nothing ran in the world that arrived. Started with `--join`, they ran their file scope before any server had answered. | fixed | A scene's client code is live only in a world that is here (`EngineState::sceneClientHeld`, `WorldHost::holdSceneClientCode`): held while a join is asked for, started fresh when it succeeds or fails, and again when the match ends. `GlobalScriptService.Client` lives across all of it. The manual's scripts and multiplayer pages say so. Tests: `network_session_tests.cpp`, `script_matrix_tests.cpp`. | The side rules said WHERE client code runs and not WHEN, and the when was "at boot" by accident. |
| D434 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: a 2D hero cannot be a player's character -- it is drawn a few ticks in the past like everyone else's (130 ms on loopback) and every client is sent the whole world (P1) | `Player.Character` was `BasePart?` and there was nothing for the plane. And a `Part2D` named as a character had nothing behind it on a replica: its snapshots were kept to be drawn from the past and the interpolation skips what a machine owns, so its own hero would not have moved at all. | fixed | `Player.Character2D`, typed `Part2D?`, over the one slot `Character` reads as a `BasePart?` -- two properties so neither kind of game casts at every read (a union broke three examples the first time the gate ran). The sprite is the centre of what its machine is sent and the streaming focus, and on its own machine it is reconciled as a script-moved 3D character is (`ReplicaSession::reconcile2d`): the authority's place at the intent it last applied, against where this machine had the sprite then, the difference carried to now -- so a client that steps it with the server's code is never corrected, and one that does not is at the newest snapshot. The multiplayer page has the pattern. Test: `session_tests.cpp`, "D434:". | A type that says "a part" in a 2D engine is a decision nobody made. |
| D435 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: a `BillboardGui` cannot hang over a `Part2D` -- no names over sprites, no health bars over monsters (P1) | `Adornee` was `BasePart?`, a billboard could only be parented to a part or an attachment, and the anchor was read from the 3D part pool alone. | fixed | `Adornee` takes a `Part2D`, a billboard may be its child, and the anchor is where the sprite is DRAWN (ADR 0134). Test: `world_ui_tests.cpp`. | The same omission as D434, one class over. |
| D436 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: a script cannot turn a world point into a pixel or a pixel into a ray -- no tap-to-move, no label that follows, no arrow at the screen's edge (P1) | The camera had no conversion at all; the manual said so under "What is not here". | fixed | `Camera:WorldToViewportPoint` (pixel, depth, on screen), `ViewportPointToRay` (origin, direction) and `ViewportPointToWorld2D`, in window pixels, for either projection (`scene/camera_view.h`). Tests: `camera_view_tests.cpp`. | Every game needs it in its first week; it was left out as "the UI tree covers screen space". |
| D437 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: the interface has no scale -- a layout drawn for a 720-line monitor is a third of the size on a phone (P1) | UI coordinates are pixels and nothing mapped a design size to the window; the game computed a scale and multiplied every number by hand. | fixed | `ScreenGui.ReferenceHeight`: the tree is laid out in units of a window that tall and scaled whole -- offsets, text, corners, strokes, scroll bars -- with `AbsolutePosition`/`AbsoluteSize` still in window pixels. `UIService.ViewportSize` says what it is scaled to, readable from a script's first line. Test: `layout_tests.cpp`. | A pixel is not a unit of design on two screens. |
| D438 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: an animation track played, stopped and played again shows nothing the second time -- "the idle only runs once" (P1) | `Stop` faded the track's weight to zero and left it there, and `Play` fades in "to the track's own weight": zero. With a fade it also stopped itself again at the end of the fade-in. | fixed | `Weight` is what the script set and a fade never writes it (`Track::ownWeight`); `Stop` fades the blend, `Play` ends a pending fade-out and fades back in from where the fade was. `TrackState::blend` is what is mixed. Test: `animation_tests.cpp`, "D438:". | One number was both the setting and the animation of the setting. |
| D439 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: a character walks uphill at 4.4 m/s and downhill at 6 with `WalkSpeed` 6 -- crawling up hills and running down them (P1) | The walk was handed to the sweep flat and the sweep laid it along the slope: uphill the horizontal speed came out as the speed times the square of the slope's cosine, and downhill it was kept whole. | fixed | On ground it can walk, the walk is given along the ground -- the rise or fall that keeps its horizontal part `WalkSpeed` (`PhysicsSync::stepController`, from the ground's normal). Measured on ramps of 10, 20 and 30 degrees: 6.00 up and down. Test: `physics_sync_tests.cpp`, "D439, D440:". | The speed a designer types is the one the map is measured in: horizontal. |
| D440 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: `CharacterBody:Move(1, 0, 1)` walks 1.41 times `WalkSpeed`, and `Move(5, 0, 0)` five times it (P1) | The vector was scaled by `WalkSpeed` and never bounded: the diagonal-is-faster bug handed to every game, and a speed hack to any server that forwards a client's vector. | fixed | The vector's length is clamped to 1; shorter still walks slower. The API text and the manual say so. Same test as D439. | "Not normalised, so a stick works" needed its other half. |
| D441 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: `CharacterBody.LinearVelocity` reads 6 m/s for a character walking into a wall and going nowhere (P2) | It reported the velocity the controller was ASKED for. | fixed | It is what the step did: displacement over the tick (`CharacterRecord::moved` in the Jolt backend). Test: `physics_tests.cpp`, "D441:". | A velocity a game picks its animation from has to be the one that happened. |
| D442 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: a script has no chance that is not the simulation's and no hashing -- `Random.new()` gives the same numbers every launch, so the game seeds from the clock and carries its own SHA-256 in Luau; a login has nothing to hash a password with (P1) | By design for the simulation (R10), with nothing beside it. | fixed | `CryptoService` (ADR 0151), over libsodium, vendored now rather than at the network ledger's C4: `RandomBytes`, `RandomInteger`, `UniqueId`, `Sha256`, `HmacSha256`, `SecureEquals`, and `HashPasswordAsync`/`VerifyPasswordAsync` (Argon2id, on a thread of its own, one at a time). Tests: `crypto_tests.cpp` against FIPS 180-4 and RFC 4231, `crypto_service_tests.cpp`. | Determinism took the only random number a script had, and a token is the one thing that must not be reproducible. |
| D443 | found making a game, 2026-10-02: four virtual keys are a stick and two buttons -- an action game's HUD on a phone needs more (P2) | `Virtual1` to `Virtual4`, sized for "a thumbstick and two or three buttons". | fixed | Sixteen: `Virtual5` to `Virtual16` appended to `Enum.KeyCode` (136 to 147), so no value moved; the two sticks stay the first four. Test: `input_tests.cpp`, "D443:". |  |
| D444 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: a tap on a button also reaches the game as an unprocessed touch -- a game that aims by tapping the world fires at whatever is behind its HUD (P2) | Touches were never marked consumed: the interface's claim on the pointer is judged a frame ahead, from where the pointer rests, and a finger rests nowhere before it lands. | fixed | The host asks the interface at the event that puts a finger down, and that finger's `Began`, `Changed` and `Ended` carry `uiConsumed` (`InputSystem::setFingerTakenByUi`). Test: `input_tests.cpp`, "D444:". | A flag a mouse earns by hovering has to be earned another way by a device that cannot hover. |
| D445 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: `engine-host <project>` by hand saves into the player's folder (`%APPDATA%/<company>/<name>/saves`), which the saves page says no test run touches (P2) | The project's `.engine/saves/` was for the three runs the engine could name (the editor's Play, `ludwerk dev`, a match's windows) and the player's folder for everything else. | fixed | The other way round (`app::saveHomeFor`): the player's folder is the PACKAGED game's -- the `game/` beside the executable -- and every run of a project folder saves into the project. Test: `project_config_tests.cpp`, "D445:"; and run: a plain run of a project wrote `.engine/saves/`. | Name the one case that is the exception, not the three that are the rule. |
| D446 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: on Android a landscape game's window is upright for its first frames -- a full-window frame measured at start reads 1080 by 2340 (P2) | The manifest holds the activity at the start scene's orientation, and making the window undid it: SDL hands the activity a requested orientation at every window's creation, and for a resizable window with no hint that is "any way the user holds it". The first frame then applied `UIService.ScreenOrientation` and turned it back. | fixed | The window is made with the orientation hint the display's shape gives -- the shape the manifest gave the activity -- until the world says which of the five it wants (`platform/window.cpp`). To be confirmed on the device. | A library's default was a second opinion on a decision the package had already made. |
| D447 | found making a 2D multiplayer game for a desktop and a phone, 2026-10-02: safe-area insets on a phone are on all four sides -- 89 px left and right upright with nothing there to avoid, 180 left and 85 top lying down -- so every HUD loses about 8% of the screen a side (P2) | SDL's safe area is the union of every inset the system has, the gesture strips included: the edges where a swipe belongs to the system, about 24 dp on all four sides of a phone with gesture navigation. | fixed | The player's own surface (`PlayerSurface.java`, through `createSDLSurface`) reports the cutout and whichever bars are showing, and nothing else -- what a HUD must stay out of. To be confirmed on the device. | A gesture strip is glass; "safe" meant "drawn over or not there". |
| D448 | found making a block-world game, 2026-10-02: with one fluid type registered, building a world from Luau costs twenty times more (13.4 ms a 16 by 16 column against 0.66) and the tick sits at 50 to 170 ms, for seconds after the last block is written -- though nothing in that world moves (P0) | Three things. Every write queued itself and its neighbours whenever the registry held a fluid TYPE, whether or not there was fluid within a mile: a mountain of stone paid a map and a set insert for every block of its surface, some seventy thousand a column. `FluidWakes::takeDue` then copied everything due into a vector, sorted it, kept 8192 and threw the rest away, every tick, for as long as a backlog of millions took to drain. And the budget being "the first 8192 by position", water beside the player waited behind every block at lower coordinates. | fixed | **A block is queued only if a look would change it** (`consider` in `voxel_fluid.cpp`): the wake decides it as the step would, from the grid as it stands, and what changes the answer later is a write, which asks again. A chunk knows which block types it has been given (`VoxelChunk::typesSeen`), so a write with no fluid in any chunk around it reads nothing at all. `takeDue` takes the first `budget` of the index it already keeps in order -- oldest first, then position. And every block whose decision READS the written one is asked, the four diagonally above included: a floor put under a pouring source now makes it spread. Measured on the game: Simulation 5.9 ms to 0.3 ms, and a block broken beside the sea still lets it through. Tests: `voxel_fluid_tests.cpp`, the four "D448:" cases. | "Wake everything that might care" is free until the thing that might care is not there, and then it is the whole cost. |
| D449 | the owner on his phone, 2026-10-02, of a block game made with the engine: "a lot of lag while the game is building the chunks... once it has built them it is smooth" -- on the desktop, one 16 by 16 column built every four ticks put 1465 of 2389 frames over 33 ms (P1) | Measured before it was designed, and it was not the meshing. A frame that found chunks changed meshed up to 32 of them (1 to 3 ms, in parallel) and then MADE TWO GPU BUFFERS FOR EVERY MESH: a third of a millisecond a mesh, 18 ms for a batch, against 0.8 ms to copy what went in them. And before any of that, every frame asked every chunk for the digests of all 27 around it -- twenty thousand lookups for a world nobody had touched -- and read 1 700 blocks of the shell of each chunk a change might have reached, up to 7 ms. | fixed | Three things (`voxel_loader.cpp`, `mesh_cache.cpp`). **Chunk meshes are slices of shared buffers** (`MeshUsage::Pooled`): a page of vertices and one of indices hold hundreds of chunks, a mesh released hands its slice to the next, and no buffer is made while a page has room. **What changed is found in one walk** of the chunks' own digests against the ones the last frame saw. **And the mesh is made on a worker**, from the chunk and the 26 around it shared and never copied (`VoxelGrid::around`): the worker decides whether the mesh would read what the one drawn read -- most of the 26 around a change answer that it would -- and the frame goes on drawing the old mesh until every chunk asked together is ready, then swaps them in one frame. A block broken or placed is waited for where it is asked, so it is in the frame it was made; a frame a picture is taken from waits for everything. The block sprint, `tests/perf/blocksprint`: 1465 frames over 33 ms to 0, median 38.9 ms to 5.5 ms. Tests: `voxel_loader_tests.cpp` and `mesh_cache_tests.cpp`, the "D449:" cases. The collider is not moved: at most 1.4 ms a tick here, and what the simulation reads cannot arrive when a worker happens to finish (the ledger says what moving it would take). | The plan said "mesh on workers", and meshing was a tenth of the cost. A probe first. |
| D450 | found making a block-world game with a menu scene, 2026-10-02: `Workspace` attributes survive a scene change -- back in the menu, before its server had run a line, a client read the world scene's "143 chunks built" (P1) | `clearScene` put every "carried" service back to the engine's settings and emptied its attributes, and `Workspace` is the file's root, not a carried service: its attributes, its tags and its own properties (gravity among them) were never reset. Tags were reset for nobody. | fixed | A scene change resets `Workspace` and every scene-scoped service alike: properties to the engine's, attributes and tags gone. `game` keeps what it was given. The scenes page lists which service is the scene's and which is the game's. Test: `world_host_tests.cpp`, "D450:". | The rule was written for "the services" and the world is one of them in everything but the code's list. |
| D451 | found making a block-world game with a menu scene, 2026-10-02: a `RemoteEvent` fired before the server listens is dropped without a word; a scene's client and server scripts start in the same tick, so the first message of every scene is a race (P1) | A received call was fired into its signal whether or not anything was connected, and a signal with no connection fires into nothing. | fixed | A call that arrives with nobody connected -- or parked in `:Wait()` -- is kept, in order, and handed to the first listener at the next tick's delivery; both ways (`ServerReceived`, `ClientReceived`); 256 a remote each way, past which the call is dropped and the remote warns once (`net.warn.remote_unheard`). Test: `world_host_tests.cpp`, "D451:". | Every game writes a "ready" flag for this; the engine owning the queue is the only version of it that is always right. |
| D452 | found making a swipe puzzle from `ludwerk new`, 2026-10-02 and the block game: a press on a label or an image INSIDE a button never reaches the button; a full-screen clear frame swallows every press meant for the screens under it, and makes every touch "the interface's" (P1) | The hit test took the topmost VISIBLE element, whatever it was. A star on a button was on top of the button; a clear layout frame in a higher `ScreenGui` was on top of everything. | fixed | `UIObject.Active`: whether an element takes the pointer. Until written it is what the object is -- buttons, `TextInput` and `ScrollFrame` take it; labels do not; a `Frame` takes it when it draws a background -- and a written value wins. Reading it gives what is so now; a scene file holds it only when written. The hit test, "the pointer is over the interface" and a touch's `uiConsumed` all ask it (`ui::takesPointer`); the editor's click still selects anything (`ui::elementAt`). The suspected inset defect (buttons under `ScreenInsets` on a phone) was this: a test presses a button under an injected inset and it activates. Tests: `interaction_tests.cpp`, the four "D452:" cases. | "What is drawn on top takes the press" is a rule about pictures; presses belong to things that do something with them. |
| D453 | found making a swipe puzzle from `ludwerk new`, 2026-10-02: an icon cannot be faded -- `ImageLabel` has no transparency of its own (P2) | A label's words had `TextTransparency`; a picture had only the background's. | fixed | `ImageLabel.ImageTransparency`. Test: `appearance_tests.cpp`, "D453:". |  |
| D454 | found making a swipe puzzle from `ludwerk new`, 2026-10-02: `ludwerk new LabirintoDeTinta` writes `id = "dev.local.LabirintoDeTinta"`, which an Android package may not be called (P2) | The template put the name into the id as typed. | fixed | The id is made from the name: lower case, anything but letters, digits and underscores an underscore, never a digit first. Test: `tools/cli/tests/tools.test.luau`. |  |
| D455 | found making a swipe puzzle from `ludwerk new`, 2026-10-02: in a project made by `ludwerk new` the packaged CLI's `check` and `fmt` do not work -- "luau-lsp is not on PATH. Run rokit install." -- and the CLI calls itself "engine" (P1) | The commands looked for their programs on PATH alone, and their answer to a missing one named a tool manager the person never installed. The package did not carry `branding/brand.toml`, so the CLI fell back to the neutral word. | fixed | An installation carries its analyser (`tools/bin/luau-lsp`, at the pinned version, with its licence and Lute's) and the brand file; the CLI runs its own copy before PATH's (ADR 0152). StyLua is NOT carried -- MPL-2.0 is not a licence R6 ships under, and the owner said to leave it out: `check` checks the types and says the formatting was not checked, `fmt` says where to get it. Test: `tools/cli/tests/tools.test.luau`; and run: `new` then `check` with nothing on PATH. | The first command a new user runs was the one nobody ran from a fresh machine. |
| D456 | the owner on his phone, 2026-10-02: a script cannot know it is on a touch device before the first input arrives (P2) | `InputService.LastInputDeviceType` says what was used last, and nothing said what is there. | fixed | `InputService.TouchAvailable`, `KeyboardAvailable`, `GamepadAvailable` and `RunService.Platform` (`Enum.Platform`), readable from a script's first line; a run with no window says what the platform normally has. Test: `world_host_tests.cpp`, "D456:". |  |
| D457 | found making a physics brawler, 2026-10-02: attributes set before a client joined are nil on that client until they are written again -- a round's number, the scores, for fourteen seconds (P1) | A new peer is sent the attributes of what ENTERS its view, and the workspace and the services never enter: they are on both ends from boot. And had they been sent, the replica could not have said whose they were -- it learns which of its instances a service id names from the first state, which arrives after. | fixed | A peer's first attributes include every owner nothing spawns, and a replica finds the workspace and the services by what they are. Test: `session_tests.cpp`, "D457:". | The seed was written for things that arrive, and the oldest things in the world never do. |
| D458 | found making a physics brawler, 2026-10-02: `RaycastResult.Instance` is nil, or the part just destroyed, for the ticks after a `Destroy` (P2) | A destroyed instance keeps its body until the simulation's next step, and stays in the pools until the tick retires it. A ray met the body and answered with whatever the id still named. | fixed | Destroyed is gone from the moment of `Destroy` (`PhysicsSync::instanceOf`), and a ray or a sphere that meets such a body goes on to what is behind it (`castPastTheGone`); `GetBodiesInBox` leaves it out. Conformance: `physics/queries.spec.luau`. |  |
| D459 | found making a block-world game with a menu scene, 2026-10-02: `PlayerRemoving` hands over a player that is already dead -- `player.UserId` raises in the handler (P1) | The player was destroyed where it was removed, and the signal is deferred like every other: the handler ran after. | fixed | A leaving player is taken out of the tree at once -- out of `GetPlayers` and every lookup -- and destroyed at the start of the next tick, after its handlers (`scene::finishLeavingPlayers`). Test: `world_host_tests.cpp`, "D459:". | A deferred signal about something ending has to keep the thing until it has been said. |
| D460 | found making a block-world game with a menu scene, 2026-10-02: automatic exposure has no floor or ceiling a world can set -- an evenly lit scene is as bright at 23:00 as at noon (P2) | The limits existed (an average between 0.15 and 3 against a key of 0.45) and were the engine's constants. | fixed | `Lighting.ExposureMin` and `ExposureMax`, in EV stops, replicated with the rest of `Lighting` (protocol 31). The defaults are the old limits to the bit, so no picture moved; `ExposureMax = 0` is a night that is as dark as it is lit. Test: `look_tests.cpp`, "D460:". |  |
| D461 | found making a block-world game with a menu scene, 2026-10-02: F3 in the host opens the overlay AND reaches the game's own F3 binding (P2) | The overlay read the key and the event went on to the game. | fixed | A host with an overlay takes its key out of what the game hears, press and release; `[debug] overlay_key` names the key, or "None". A shipped player has no overlay and every key is the game's. Test: `project_config_tests.cpp`, "D461:". |  |
| D462 | the owner, 2026-10-02: "I swiped right, up, down, left -- the engine has to support this" -- every touch game writes its own gesture recogniser (P1) | Nothing recognised a gesture: there were fingers, by id, and what they did was the game's to work out. | fixed | `InputService.TouchSwiped`, `TouchTapped`, `TouchLongPressed`, `TouchPinched`, `TouchPanned`, and `Enum.KeyCode.SwipeUp/Down/Left/Right` as keys an action binds (a press of one tick). From a finger and from the mouse's left drag; said while the finger is still down; one long drag one way is one swipe; a press the interface took starts none; the length is `InputService.SwipeThreshold` in millimetres. Recognised from the device snapshot at each tick, so a replay makes the same gestures. Tests: `input_tests.cpp` and `world_host_tests.cpp`, the "D462:" cases. |  |
| D463 | found closing a batch, 2026-10-02: the gate's log carried a stack trace under "the documentation site builds" and the stage said ok (P1) | The documentation site had built nothing since the rename. The page shell read the brand while it was being required; reading it asks the file system, which yields, and a module that yields while loading does not load. The runner printed the stack and returned zero, so the stage that exists to catch a reference that no longer resolves passed with no site at all -- and nine such references gathered behind it: the two brand pictures the generator looked for under names the rename had changed, five links in manual pages that the site cannot resolve, and a doc string citing a section. | fixed | The shell reads the brand when a page is rendered (`api/generator/site/shell.luau`), and the stage also requires the page the build must have written (`scripts/gates/luau-check.sh`): with only that second half applied the stage fails, which is the failing test. The nine references are fixed and the site builds whole (442 files). |  |
| D464 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02, a 2D game on the packaged engine (finding L2): the palette on the screen was not the palette in the script (P1) | A sprite was drawn into the HDR target and resolved like light: exposed, tone-mapped, graded, with bloom and dither on top. `Color3.fromRGB(200, 80, 40)` under the default `Lighting` arrived as 181, 64, 7. | fixed | ADR 0153: `Part2D.ExactColor` and `Tilemap2D.ExactColor`, true by default. The sprite pass writes a one-channel mask of the pixels it covers, the resolve passes those through as they are and the bloom leaves them out; a sprite keeps its place among glass and particles. Made only in a frame that has such a sprite, so nothing else's command stream moves. Protocol 32. Test: `sprite_color_gate` (`tests/screenshots/spritecolor`), nine probes at a tolerance of one step -- the lit square in the same scene is what every sprite was before. |  |
| D465 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02, installing the batch's APKs on the owner's phone: `INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE: signatures do not match` for every game (P1) | The debug key lived under the per-user folder named by the brand. A package without its brand file read the neutral name and signed with one key; the repository's CLI, and the package once D455 gave it the file, signed with another. What a game is signed with moved with a string in a toml -- and so did the folder `install-tools` puts gigabytes of toolchains in. | fixed | The debug key is Android's own: `~/.android/debug.keystore`, alias `androiddebugkey`, the standard passwords -- used if there, made there if not -- so a debug build from this engine and from any other tool update one another; `[export.android] debug_keystore` (and `debug_key_alias`) names a team's instead, and one that is missing is an error, never made. `install-tools` installs under `~/.android/engine` (or `ENG_TOOLS_ROOT`), keeps an install already under the brand's folder, and both are searched. When the phone refuses an update across keys the Export window and `scripts/android-player.ps1` say what it means and that the game must be uninstalled once, and uninstall nothing. Test: `export.test.luau`, "the debug key and the tools folder do not carry the brand's name". Left as they were, and why: the Gradle cache under the brand's folder (a cache), and the editor's preferences -- the remembered release passwords among them -- which are where the platform layer puts a program's preferences, by its name. |  |
| D466 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02, a block-world game (finding V3): "`ApplyImpulse` on a `CharacterBody` is a silent no-op (measured), `LinearVelocity` is read-only, no `GravityScale` in 3D, `JumpSpeed` refuses negatives. Swimming in the game is `JumpSpeed = 0; Jump()` every tick" (P1) | The controller read `Move` and `Jump` and nothing else: an impulse queued on a character was never taken, a controller had no gravity of its own, and nothing about it knew what a fluid was. | fixed | An impulse is a change of speed of the impulse over the controller's mass (`Mass` says 80): across, a push that fades and that `Move` is added to; up and down, part of what gravity integrates. `LinearVelocity` can be written. `GravityScale`, `SwimSpeed`, `FlySpeed`, `Flying`; `Enum.CharacterState` gains `Swimming` -- entered by itself under a `Water`'s surface or inside a fluid block -- and `Flying`; in both `Move` is read in three dimensions. `JumpSpeed` takes any finite number. The push travels with the character's other motion state (protocol 32, the rollback snapshot, a replay's start) and is hashed only while there is some, so no trace moved. `@engine/camera.firstPerson`. Tests: `tests/conformance/physics/character_modes.spec.luau` (18 cases) and the fluid case in `world/voxel.spec.luau`. |  |
| D467 | found writing D466's tests, 2026-10-02: a character made in the tick another was destroyed in its place came out a metre and a half to one side, or lost the jump it was given (P1) | The sweep that takes a destroyed instance's body out of the simulation ran after the walk that makes the tick's new ones. A rigid body was gone again before the step; a character controller keeps the contacts it finds when it is made, and its first step was resolved against a body that no longer existed -- shoved out of it, or standing on it. A respawn at the place of death is exactly this. | fixed | What was destroyed leaves first: `PhysicsSync::retireGone` runs before the walk and takes out the joints, bodies and controllers of destroyed instances; the sweep after the walk is still what takes what left the world some other way. Test: `character_modes.spec.luau`, "is not shoved by the one that is gone" -- which failed, with four other cases of the same file, before the fix. |  |
| D468 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02, a game with a menu scene and a world scene (finding S3): "no way to boot into a scene other than `project.toml`'s ... tests of a level must not have to click through the menu" (P2) | A run started in the project's scene and nowhere else, and a scene's load data could only come from a `LoadScene` call. | fixed | `engine-host <project> --scene=PATH [--scene-data=JSON]`, and the same two on `ludwerk dev`. The scene named wins over the project's and over the one an editor had open; the JSON is what `GetLoadData` answers, set before the first script runs; text that is not JSON is a usage error where it was typed. Tests: the two "D468:" cases in `world_host_tests.cpp`, and `host_scene_data_must_be_json`. |  |
| D469 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02 (finding S4a): a `ScreenGui` parented to `UIService` by a script in `GlobalScriptService`, without `KeepOnSceneLoad`, died at the scene change, and the module raised `instance_dead` a scene later (P2) | Nothing was wrong and nothing said so: the game's code outlives the scene and the screen it made did not, and the first sign was an error somewhere else, later. | fixed | A screen remembers that the game's own code put it in `UIService`; when a scene change takes one that is not kept, the host warns once by name with what to set. A scene's own screens go with their scene in silence, as they should. Test: "D469:" in `world_host_tests.cpp`. The scenes guide lists what is the scene's for code that is the game's -- the block types of `VoxelService` among them. |  |
| D470 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02, a physics brawler (the F2 findings): "the plain servo never settles" -- a hinge servo asked for 30 degrees swung round its target for seconds, and a ball a metre out on an arm did it whatever the numbers (P1) | Two causes, one under the other. The servo was a speed proportional to its error under a torque cap, which arrives too fast to brake. And under that: the solver's own joint motor is a row about each body's centre of mass beside the rows that hold the pivot, and a solver that goes round its rows in turn settles two rows quickly only when they have little to do with each other -- a body that swings from a pivot away from where it balances makes them nearly the same row. Measured on a ball a metre out: a spring a seventh as stiff as asked and none of its damping. | fixed | A hinge's and a ball socket's motor is a constraint of the engine's own beside the joint (`PivotMotor` in the Jolt backend): one row that is the turn WITH the pivot already held, which asks nothing of the pivot's rows and is solved in a round. What it feels as its mass is what the pair is to turn about that pivot, so a servo is a critically damped spring at `AngularResponsiveness` radians a second on a finger and on a crane. Far from its target it travels at `AngularSpeed`; near it is the spring. Tests: `movers_settle.spec.luau`, "A servo is a critically damped spring" -- 0.01, 1 and 100 kg m2 within a degree in a second and never a degree past; the line of the spring it says it is within 2 degrees at every tick; under a cap it sags and rests, over it holds. The coordinator's `servo` probe reads 24 29 30 30. A servo at its cap is a constant torque and what brings it to rest is the world's damping: said in the manual. |  |
| D471 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02, the same brawler: `AlignPosition`, `AlignOrientation`, `LinearVelocity` and `AngularVelocity` are wrong on a jointed body -- an upright sized for a whole fighter turned the hips and fought every joint below them (P1) | They were impulses worked out before the step from the one body alone. Right for a body nothing else holds; on any other the joints answered a tick later and the two never agreed. | fixed | The four that hold something are motors in the solver: a six-axis joint with every axis free and a motor on the ones a mover holds (`ConstraintType::Drive` at the seam), against the world or -- with `Reaction*Enabled` -- between the two bodies. Solved in the same rounds as the joints. `VectorForce`, `Torque` and a spring without stops stay forces. A spring said in `Stiffness` and `Damping` reads as that spring on a body with limbs: tests in `movers_settle.spec.luau` -- one, two and three jointed pieces against the ideal line, a ten-piece rope lifted by its end with no overshoot, a spring a hundred times stiffer than a tick can show, a `LinearVelocity` that tows what a rod joins and an `AngularVelocity` that turns a door about its hinge. The coordinator's `align` and `upright` probes read as the ideal line. **What the solver cannot do is said, not hidden**: a rigid chain of five turned from its middle wants four times the rounds a tick has, and eleven is out of reach at sixteen times -- measured, and in the manual. A cap is along each axis, not a magnitude. `GetMotorForce()` and `GetMotorTorque()` say what a motor used. |  |
| D472 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02, the same brawler: `BasePart.Mass` is 0 in the resumption that made the part, and for a tick after -- every spring sized from it in the line after `Parent` was sized for nothing (P2) | `Mass` was what the simulation last measured, and it measures when it makes the body: the tick after. | fixed | `Mass` and `AssemblyMass` answer from what the part is -- its shape, its size and its density, by the volume the solver builds for that shape -- so a part weighs at once, and weighs its new weight the moment it is resized. A `MeshPart` that collides as its hull weighs its box until the solver has hulled it. Tests: "a Block/Ball/Cylinder/Capsule/Wedge says what it weighs the moment it is in the world" in `movers.spec.luau`, and `physics_tests.cpp` holds the formula to the solver for every solid at sizes where its floors bite. |  |
| D473 | found writing D471's tests, 2026-10-02: a hinge locked by giving `LowerAngle` and `UpperAngle` the same value printed `Jolt reported a broken assumption ... Better use a fixed constraint` once for every such joint (P3) | The solver holds a joint whose two limits are one value exactly there, but making one that way trips an assertion in its constructor, and the engine reports the solver's assertions as errors. | fixed | A hinge or a rail whose limits are equal is made a hair wide and then told the range it was asked for, which the solver takes without complaint and holds as before. Exercised by every chain in `movers_settle.spec.luau`; the run's log carries no report. |  |
| D474 | found timing a boot test CI had timed out on, 2026-10-02: `examples/22-atmosphere` takes ten seconds to start, every time, to load six pictures (P1) | The import cache's key hashed `MeshCompileOptions` "byte for byte", and that struct has three bytes of padding after a `bool`. Padding is whatever the memory held, so the key was a different key in every process: every run of the engine compiled the project's meshes and pictures again and wrote a cache nothing ever read. The example had 2,208 cache entries for six pictures. The test that existed compiled twice in ONE process, where the padding is the same both times. | fixed | The key is made field by field, with a `static_assert` on the struct's size so a field added to the options cannot be left out of it. That boot is 0.09 s warm (was 10.0). Test: "the second build..." in `compiler_tests.cpp` now builds the options over memory that held something else and asks for no misses -- it failed first. The boot tests' timeout goes from 120 to 300 s: a hosted runner checks out cold and still compiles once (24 to 52 s over the last ten green runs, more than 130 on the red one). |  |
| D475 | ludwerk-08, 2026-10-02, a physics brawler: a part made in a resumption is not found by a query until the simulation has stepped -- `Parent = workspace`, then `Workspace:Raycast` through it in the next line, returns nil (P2) | A query asks the solver, and a part becomes a body when the tick's mirror walks the world. D458 made a destroyed part leave every query at once; a made one had to wait. | fixed | Before a query the mirror gives a body to every part that has come into the world since it last looked (`PhysicsSync::syncForQuery`), in the pool's own order, as the tick would; one compare when nothing was written. `Raycast`, `Spherecast`, `GetBodiesInBox`, `GetBodiesInSphere` and the detectors' ray call it. A part that is ALREADY a body and is moved by its `CFrame` stays the tick's: an anchored part is swept to where it was written, so what stands on it is carried, and placing it early would make the sweep a jump. Tests: "A part made this instant" in `queries.spec.luau`, three cases, failed first. |  |
| D476 | found building `HapticService`, 2026-10-02: no gamepad has ever worked in any build (P1) | `third_party/CMakeLists.txt` builds the vendored SDL with `SDL_JOYSTICK`, `SDL_HAPTIC` and `SDL_HIDAPI` off -- "they stay off until the milestone that needs them" -- and the build's own `SDL_build_config.h` says `SDL_JOYSTICK_DISABLED 1` with the dummy driver. M6 wrote the gamepad half of the Input Action System against that library: it compiles, links, opens every gamepad SDL reports, and SDL reports none. Every gamepad test drives the input layer with made events, so nothing noticed. | fixed | The three switches are on (a push of its own, after the one that found it). What holds it: `gamepad_tests.cpp` plugs in a VIRTUAL gamepad the platform library hosts -- `platform::attachVirtualGamepad` -- and presses a button on it, and asserts that an `InputAction` bound to `ButtonSouth` is pressed, that a stick reaches an axis, and that `HapticService`'s levels arrive at the device through the library's own rumble call. Every link is the one a real controller uses, on a machine with none; a build without a joystick subsystem answers zero for the device and fails. `SDL_build_config.h` says `SDL_JOYSTICK_HIDAPI`, `XINPUT`, `GAMEINPUT`, `WGI` and `VIRTUAL` on Windows. Android gains the `VIBRATE` permission. A real controller in a real hand, and a phone that shakes, are the owner's to feel. |  |
| D477 | found building F3's example, 2026-10-02: a `ScrollFrame` cannot be scrolled by a player -- not by the wheel, not by a finger (P1) | Nothing ever read the wheel for the interface, and a press inside a scroll frame was only ever a press: `CanvasPosition` moved when a script wrote it and at no other time. Every list a game shipped scrolled by its own code or not at all. | fixed | The wheel over a scroll frame moves its canvas three lines a notch, and a frame that cannot move that way hands the wheel to the one it is inside; a press that is then dragged moves the canvas with the pointer, and the row under the finger is not pressed when the list went somewhere. `selection_tests.cpp`: the wheel moves the canvas and stops at its end; a drag scrolls and fires no `Activated`; a list with nowhere to scroll still presses its rows. |  |
| D478 | found building F3's layouts, 2026-10-02: a child that is not `Visible` keeps its room -- a slot in a `UIListLayout`, and its extent in a parent with `AutomaticSize` (P2) | `collectChildren` took every `UIObject` child, and only `place` looked at `visible`: a hidden row was measured, given its place in the line, and then not drawn there. | fixed | The children a layout arranges are the visible ones, for a list, a grid, pages and a parent that sizes to its content alike. `adaptive_layout_tests.cpp`: a hidden cell leaves no hole, a hidden child of a list keeps no room, and the parent is not as large as what is hidden. |  |
| D479 | found building F3's example, 2026-10-02: in a `ScrollFrame` whose `CanvasSize` is zero on one axis -- every list that scrolls down -- a child sized as a fraction of its parent is nothing wide, and a grid has room for one cell a row (P1) | The layout took the canvas as given whenever either axis of it was above zero, so `(0, 400)` was a canvas 400 tall and nothing wide. A row of `UDim2.new(1, 0, 0, 40)` came out zero wide; rows sized in offsets looked right, which is why nobody saw it. | fixed | The canvas is never smaller than the frame on either axis, less its padding like any other parent's. `adaptive_layout_tests.cpp`, failing first with a width of 0 against 200: a row as wide as the frame, a grid that fills it, and a canvas smaller than the frame that is the frame. |  |
| D480 | reported from play, 2026-10-02 (ludwerk-08, reproduced with a real window and real keys): after a client has one long frame, the server ignores everything it presses for the rest of the session -- its character never moves again, or never stops (P0) | A replica that drops simulated time after a long frame numbers its ticks that much behind where the authority has got to, and `AuthoritySession` dropped every intent whose tick was at or before the one it had applied as "too late for its tick". `applyIntents` advances that tick every server tick whether or not anything arrived, the last intent standing in -- so the replica stayed behind for ever, every intent it sent was late, and whatever was held at the moment of the long frame stood in for the rest of the session. There was a catch-up for a peer that got AHEAD and none for one that fell BEHIND. A route whose latency rose past the queue's room did the same with no long frame at all. | fixed | Intents that arrive whole after their ticks for four ticks in a row are a clock that moved: the stream is anchored again on the newest tick the peer sent, as it is on its first, and the snapshot answers in the peer's own ticks from there, so the replica is corrected once for the time it lost. A stand-in has a lifetime, a quarter of a second, after which nothing is held for a peer that says nothing. A press made in the ticks before the stream is anchored again is carried into the next tick, as a late press already was, whether or not the authority still remembers what stood in for its tick. **And at once when the newest tick of a message is late by more than the redundancy window** (the same day, after the first fix was measured on a real window): a window in the background runs ten frames a second and sends six ticks together, so four late ticks in a row was four frames, and after a frame of 400 ms the authority went 18 to 47 ticks without a real intent, the character standing still for up to 26 of them. The stream is anchored on the newest tick that came, not the oldest the redundancy carried, and a button newly down waits for its own tick rather than standing in for the whole wait. Measured on the real window again (`MMO2D`'s intent walk, frames of 210 and 400 ms, the window behind another): the four legs walked whole with 5 corrections through 13 long frames, against 104 corrections and a character that never left its cell before. An audit of that follow-up (ludwerk-08, modelling `applyIntents`) found three more, fixed with it: a button tapped in an older tick of the burst the anchor collapsed was erased with it, and is carried now; a stand-in's lifetime counted ticks without a real intent, so a key held through a frame of 400 ms was let go in the middle of it -- it counts ticks in which no message came at all, half a second of them; and the catch-up that skips a queue that grew too deep dropped the presses in what it skipped, and carries them. `session_tests.cpp`, twelve cases, eight of them red before their fix (two more model a frame a tick and six ticks a frame, two frames of 400 ms each, and one runs both sessions for real through three): a replica eight ticks behind is heard within the delay; a key held at the long frame is let go when the replica lets it go; a press in the first ticks after a half-second frame is applied once; a silent replica stops; a replica at five ticks to the authority's six is followed for five seconds; and, both sessions real, a long frame while walking is one correction and the two characters stop in the same place. |
